⏺ fabrik:builder(Build SQLite cgo binding) Opus 5.5

⏺ One Opus builder is now working on the SQLite binding in the worktree
  sqlite-binding. I scoped item 3 as the whole binding, with the symbol clash as
  a question it must answer with evidence; say so if you only wanted a spike on
  the clash.

  On item 2, here is the story I'd propose. It keeps the proposal in AGENTS.md
  and fills in the gaps.

  The rule: any local I/O failure on the cache puts the database handle into a
  terminal stopped state. That covers a failure at commit, in a read, in a write
  before the swap, or during catch-up. There is no retry in place, because
  after a failed fsync the local state cannot be trusted until it is reopened.

  What "stopped" means: s4ql is a library, so it cannot crash the process. The
  handle is dead instead: no new statement starts, in-flight reads and queued
  writers get a sticky error that wraps the cause, and renewal stops.

  The committing caller: Commit returns success if the swap succeeded, because
  the transaction is durable. The handle stops first, so no read can ever show
  the old state after the acknowledgement. The spec's rule becomes "acknowledged
  after the cache has it, or after the instance has stopped serving".

  The lease: stop first, then try once to release it. If the release fails, the
  lease times out as it would after a crash.

  Recovery: the application closes and reopens, which is the normal startup
  path. The cache's recorded position says whether the local commit landed, so
  recovery does not need to know what went wrong. It also covers the case where
  the local commit succeeded but reported an error.

  A persistent failure: Open fails loudly. Nothing deletes or rebuilds the cache
  automatically, in line with the spec's "refused loudly, not rebuilt
  silently".

  Simulation: two buggify variants after the swap. One rolls back the local
  commit and reports an error; the other commits and reports an error. The
  invariant to check is that no read starting after an acknowledged commit
  returns a state without it.

  Three decisions are yours:

  1. Uniform stop. Should every local I/O failure stop the handle, including a
     full disk in a write before the swap, where nothing was committed? I
     recommend yes: one rule and one tested path, at the cost of availability on
     transient failures.
  2. No automatic reopen. Should the library leave reopening to the application?
     I recommend yes for the first design.
  3. In-flight reads. They started before the commit, so letting them finish
     would still be a consistent snapshot. I recommend aborting them anyway,
     since the disk just failed.

  Which of these do you disagree with?

⏺ The builder has been told to cut the binding down to what slice 1 needs:

  First decision: does every local I/O failure stop the handle?

  The case that needs a stop is the one we started with: the swap succeeded and
  the local commit failed. The question is whether the same rule applies when
  nothing was committed, for example a full disk during a write before the swap,
  or a read that hits an I/O error.

  I recommend yes. It gives one rule and one recovery path to test, and a disk
  that just failed is not one I'd keep trusting for reads. The cost is
  availability: a transient full disk takes the handle down until the
  application reopens it, where a narrower rule would only fail that one
  transaction.

  Do you agree that any local I/O failure stops the handle?

✻ Cogitated for 21s · done Tuesday 4:18 PM

❯ Yeah, sounds good. We can relax later if needed.                              

⏺ Second decision: who reopens after a stop?

  Once the handle has stopped, recovery is the normal startup path: close,
  reopen, take the lease again, and catch the cache up from the log. Either the
  application does that itself, or the library does it automatically behind the
  same handle.

  I recommend leaving it to the application for the first design. An automatic
  reopen hides a failing disk behind retries, and it adds a second way into the
  startup path that the simulation would have to cover. The cost is that every
  application needs its own reopen or restart logic, and one that has none stays
  down until someone restarts it.

  Do you agree that the library does not reopen by itself?

✻ Cooked for 2s · done Tuesday 4:23 PM

❯ Yes, also for now. Recovery is then the same as normal app crash recovery.    
  Same path, that’s nice.                                                       

⏺ Third decision: what happens to reads that are already running when the handle
  stops?

  A read transaction that began before the failed commit holds a consistent
  snapshot of the old state. Letting it finish would break no rule, because the
  invariant only concerns reads that start after the commit is acknowledged. The
  alternative is to abort it, so its next step returns the stop error.

  I recommend aborting. The disk has just failed, so the rest of that read comes
  from storage we have decided not to trust, and it matches a real crash, where
  those reads would die too. The cost is that a long read which might have
  completed fails instead.

  Do you agree that in-flight reads are aborted?

  Next: what the committing caller is told, which needs a spec change.

  When the swap succeeded and the local commit failed, I propose that Commit
  returns success, because the transaction is durable in S3 and an error would
  invite a retry that writes it twice. The handle stops before Commit returns,
  so the caller learns of the failure on its next call.

  The spec currently says a commit is acknowledged only after the cache has it.
  The amended rule would read: "A commit is acknowledged after the manifest swap
  has succeeded and either the cache has it or the instance has stopped
  serving."

  Do you agree to Commit returning success here, and to that wording in the
  spec?

✻ Baked for 29s · done Tuesday 4:30 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Short answer

  "Return success, then stop serving" is how established systems end up
  behaving, but they get there by ordering, not by a decision made after the
  failure. In the closest analogies (FoundationDB, CockroachDB, Neon, etcd),
  applying the entry to the local store is not on the acknowledgement path at
  all. The client gets success as soon as the authoritative log is durable. If
  the local apply fails afterwards, that node or role dies, and it recovers by
  replaying the log or rebuilding from other copies.

  I found no system that sees the local failure before replying and then
  deliberately replies with plain success. The nearest is PostgreSQL's
  synchronous-replication cancel path. It replies with success plus a WARNING,
  because an ERROR "would lead the client to believe that the transaction
  aborted, which is not true".

  Servers that hit a fatal error before replying die without replying:
  PostgreSQL PANIC, MySQL `ABORT_SERVER`, etcd, rqlite. The client sees a lost
  connection, which client libraries report as "outcome unknown". No system I
  checked returns an error meaning "not committed" once the authoritative record
  is durable.

  The precedents support the proposal, with one caveat: make the stopped state
  loud and visible, as PostgreSQL does with its WARNING.

  ## Per system (V = primary source read in this run, I = inferred or from
  memory)

  - **PostgreSQL, sync replication wait cancelled** (V, `syncrep.c`, https://git
  hub.com/postgres/postgres/blob/master/src/backend/replication/syncrep.c)
    - Client told: on cancel, success plus WARNING "The transaction has already
  committed locally, but might not have been replicated to the standby". On
  terminate, the same WARNING, then no reply, and the backend exits.
    - Reasoning: an error would falsely mean "aborted", and a plain ack would
  falsely mean "replicated".
  - **PostgreSQL, fsync failure** (V, `data_sync_retry` in
  https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/runtime-config-error-handling.html)
    - Node: PANIC, then recovery from WAL.
    - Reasoning: page-cache state is unknown after a write-back failure, so
  retrying in place can report success after data was lost.
    - `xact.c` (V) runs the commit record write in a critical section, and it
  PANICs on "cannot abort transaction ..., it was already committed".
  - **MySQL** (V,
  https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/replication-options-binary-log.html)
    - `binlog_error_action=ABORT_SERVER` (the default) shuts the server down on
  binlog write, flush or sync errors. Restart recovers as after a crash.
    - V, `sql/binlog.cc`: the engine-commit step after the binlog sync returns
  `void`, so MySQL treats it as infallible and has no error path to the client.
    - I: InnoDB aborts the server on redo-log I/O errors.
  - **CockroachDB** (V, `kvserver/apply/task.go`, `replica_raft.go`,
  https://github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach)
    - Client told: success before the write is applied ("the raft log is the
  provider of atomicity and durability ..., not ... the replicated state machine
  itself").
    - Node: any apply error is `log.Fatalf`, so the process exits.
    - V,
  https://docs.cockroachlabs.com/docs/stable/cluster-setup-troubleshooting: a
  disk stall of 20 s or more terminates the process.
    - `AmbiguousResultError` (V, `errors.proto`) is what a client gets when the
  response is lost.
    - This is the strongest precedent for success followed by the node stopping.
  - **etcd** (V, `etcdserver/server.go`, `backend/backend.go`, `batch_tx.go`)
    - Client told: success after the entry is applied to an in-memory batch
  transaction. The bbolt commit happens later on a timer.
    - Node: `Fatal("failed to commit tx")`, then restart and replay of the raft
  WAL.
  - **rqlite** (V, `store/store.go`)
    - Node: a whole-request apply error is `Fatalf("... aborting to prevent
  divergence between nodes")`.
    - Client told: nothing, because the reply comes after apply.
    - I: I could not verify whether an SQLite I/O error counts as a
  per-statement error or a request error.
  - **TiKV** (V, `raftstore/.../apply.rs`)
    - Node: `panic!("failed to write to engine")`.
    - Reply timing: not verified.
  - **FoundationDB** (V, https://apple.github.io/foundationdb/architecture.html,
  `fdbserver/worker/worker.cpp`)
    - Client told: success once the mutations are durable on the transaction
  logs. Storage servers pull them later.
    - Node: a storage role with `io_error` ends, data distribution repairs the
  team, and the old disk files are deleted. So the store is rebuilt, not
  repaired in place.
    - V, developer guide: `commit_unknown_result` is used only when the client
  genuinely does not know. Retrying after it can double-apply non-idempotent
  transactions.
  - **Neon** (V, https://neon.com/docs/introduction/architecture-overview)
    - Client told: committed at a safekeeper quorum.
    - Node: a failed pageserver is replaced and rebuilds its pages from WAL and
  object storage.
  - **Durable Objects** (V,
  https://blog.cloudflare.com/durable-objects-easy-fast-correct-choose-three/)
    - If the authoritative write fails, the held responses are replaced with
  errors, which is truthful there.
    - The object is "shut down and restarted from scratch".
  - **SlateDB** (V via the project's note `slatedb.md`, not re-read here)
    - A fatal WAL error makes the writer unusable. Recovery is reopen.
  - **SQLite** (V, `pager.c`)
    - In rollback-journal mode, COMMIT can return an I/O error after the commit
  point. The code says "At this point the transaction is committed", then runs
  truncates that can fail.
    - In WAL mode (V for the ordering in `wal.c`, I for the consequence): the
  commit frame is written before the fsync, so a failed sync may still leave a
  transaction that recovery will treat as committed.
    - Docs (V): the docs do not say whether a failed COMMIT may have committed.
  - **Not checked:** mvSQLite, turbopuffer, WarpStream, dqlite, Kafka. Aurora
  only from memory (I).

  ## Precedents against, and what they imply

  1. **Most servers never reply in this state.** They die, and the client sees
  "unknown". An embedded library cannot drop the connection, though, and s4ql
  knows the outcome. Returning "unknown" would push the application into a retry
  or an idempotency check for a question that is already answered.
  2. **PostgreSQL refuses a plain ack when the ack promises more than
  happened.** If s4ql's "committed" also promises that this handle will show the
  write, success is honest only because the handle stops. Make the stop loud:
  every later call returns a distinct stopped error that wraps the I/O cause, it
  is logged at error level, and it is visible in health checks.
  3. **An error invites a retry that applies the transaction twice.**
  FoundationDB documents this as its biggest gotcha. That supports returning
  success, not an error.
  4. **For the recovery story, SQLite's IOERR on COMMIT does not tell you
  whether the cache committed.** Recovery must read the cache's recorded
  last-applied change file and catch up from there, never assume either way. The
  spec already records that position in the same local transaction as the rows.
  Retrying in place is unsafe, for the same reason PostgreSQL gives for
  `data_sync_retry`.

  ## Notes

  The fuller notes, with quotes and sources, are in
  /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb
  0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/commit-ack-precedents.md. The downloaded source
  files I searched are in /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4q
  l/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/src/. Nothing in the
  repository was changed.

⏺ The research supports returning success, with one caveat about making the stop
  loud.

  - Closest analogies: FoundationDB, CockroachDB, Neon and etcd tell the client
    "committed" as soon as the authoritative log is durable. If applying it
    locally fails afterwards, the node kills itself and recovers from the log.
    That is success followed by a stop, though they get there by ordering: the
    local apply is never on the acknowledgement path.
  - Servers that fail before replying: PostgreSQL, MySQL and rqlite die without
    answering, so the client sees a lost connection and an unknown outcome. A
    library cannot drop a connection, and we do know the outcome.
  - Nearest deliberate case: when PostgreSQL's wait for synchronous replication
    is cancelled, it returns success with a warning. Its reasoning is that an
    error would make the client believe the transaction aborted, which is not
    true.
  - Against an error: no system the agent checked reports "not committed" once
    the authoritative record is durable. FoundationDB documents retry after an
    unknown result as its main source of double-applied transactions.

  The agent found no system that sees the local failure first and then returns a
  plain success, so we would be slightly unusual in form, not in substance. It
  did not check mvSQLite, turbopuffer, WarpStream, dqlite or Kafka.

  The caveat comes from PostgreSQL's warning: a plain success is honest only
  because the handle stops, so the stop must be loud. I'd add that every later
  call returns a distinct stopped error that wraps the I/O cause.

  Do you agree to Commit returning success, with the spec wording from before
  plus that loud stopped error?

✻ Brewed for 14s · done Tuesday 4:37 PM

⏺ Agent "Research commit-ack precedents" finished · 6m 45s

❯ Could also be return an error that means committed but the stopped, and every 
  later query returns the stopped error from before signaling the app should    
  restart or something                                                          

⏺ That works mechanically, and it is the closest Go gets to PostgreSQL's
  "success with a warning". I'd still advise against it, for one reason: what a
  caller does when it does not look closely.

  Almost all Go code treats any error from Commit as "it did not commit". A job
  worker would return the error and the queue would redeliver the job after the
  restart; a web handler would show a failure and the user would submit again.
  Both write the transaction twice, which is the outcome we were avoiding. Only
  callers that check for the specific error get it right.

  With a nil return, the careless caller is correct by default, and Commit's
  result keeps one meaning: nil if and only if the transaction is known to be
  committed. We will need that clarity anyway, because a swap that times out has
  a truly unknown outcome and must return an error.

  Your concern is that the application should learn about the stop at once and
  not on its next query, and that can be met separately. The handle can expose
  the stop itself, in the style of a context: a channel that closes when it
  stops, and the cause as an error. The application watches that and restarts.

  So my recommendation is: Commit returns nil, later calls return the stopped
  error, and the handle signals the stop directly. Do you want that, or the
  "committed but stopped" error from Commit?

✻ Crunched for 18s · done Tuesday 4:43 PM

❯ Your recommendation                                                           

⏺ Settled: Commit returns nil, later calls return the stopped error, and the
  handle signals the stop directly.

  Next: the lease when the handle stops.

  I propose that the handle stops serving first and then tries once to release
  the lease. If that release fails, the lease times out, exactly as after a
  crash.

  Releasing lets the reopened handle, or another instance, take over at once
  without waiting up to 30 seconds. It is safe because the handle has already
  stopped, so nothing can be served under a lease it no longer holds. S3 was
  reachable a moment ago, since the swap succeeded, so the release usually
  works. The alternative is to skip the release and let the lease expire, which
  is one step simpler but makes every such failure cost the full timeout.

  Do you agree to stop first, then release once?

✻ Brewed for 4s · done Tuesday 4:45 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Next: recovery, and what happens if the disk keeps failing.

  On reopen, the cache's recorded position decides everything. If the failed
  local commit did not land, startup applies the one missing change file. If it
  did land despite the error, there is nothing to apply. The research confirmed
  this matters: an I/O error from SQLite's COMMIT does not say whether the
  commit happened. So the cache is caught up in place, never assumed to be in
  either state.

  If the failure persists, Open returns an error and the application stays down.
  Nothing deletes or rebuilds the cache automatically. Rebuilding from S3 is
  always possible, but a person or the application chooses it, for example by
  pointing at an empty cache. That matches the spec's existing rule that a bad
  cache is "refused loudly, not rebuilt silently".

  Do you agree: catch up in place from the recorded position, and no automatic
  rebuild?

✻ Churned for 4s · done Tuesday 4:45 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Next: how the simulation injects this failure.

  I propose a buggify point between the manifest swap and the local commit, with
  two variants:

  - The local commit is rolled back and reports an error.
  - The local commit goes through and still reports an error.

  The second one covers the case from the research where SQLite's error does not
  tell you what happened. The simulation checks one invariant after either: no
  read that starts after an acknowledged commit returns a state without that
  commit. It also checks that reopening shows the transaction, and that the
  lease was released or timed out.

  A fault-injecting layer under SQLite itself (torn writes, failed fsyncs) would
  be more realistic. I'd leave it out for now, since SQLite tests its own I/O
  error handling heavily and using the official C code is how we inherit that.

  Do you agree to the two buggify variants and that invariant, without a
  fault-injecting layer for now?

✻ Cooked for 3s · done Tuesday 4:51 PM

❯ I don’t understand why we need the second variant if we trust SQLite code     
  (which we should)                                                             

⏺ It isn't about distrusting SQLite. The second variant simulates something
  SQLite legitimately does: COMMIT can return an I/O error after the transaction
  has already become durable. The research agent read this in SQLite's source
  for rollback-journal mode, where a comment says "At this point the transaction
  is committed" before cleanup steps that can still fail. For WAL mode, which
  the cache will most likely use, the agent verified the ordering (commit frame
  written before the fsync) but only inferred the same consequence.

  So the variant tests our code, not SQLite's. The bug it guards against is
  ours: recovery that assumes "error means the cache did not get it" and applies
  the change file a second time. With only the first variant, that wrong
  assumption would pass every simulation run.

  It costs one extra branch at the buggify point. If we always recover by
  reading the recorded position, both variants go through the same path and the
  second one just proves it.

  Do you want to keep the second variant on that basis?

✻ Baked for 9s · done Tuesday 4:52 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ That settles the story. Last item: the wording for the spec, since nothing
  goes in there without your agreement.

  Replace the acknowledgement rule with:

  ▎ A commit is acknowledged after the cache has it, or after the instance has 
  ▎ stopped. The application is told that a transaction is committed once the 
  ▎ manifest swap has succeeded and either the commit to the local cache has 
  ▎ succeeded or the instance has stopped serving. In the second case Commit 
  ▎ still returns success, because the transaction is committed.

  Add two constraints:

  ▎ A local I/O failure stops the instance. If reading or writing the local 
  ▎ cache fails, the instance stops serving for good. Running reads and waiting 
  ▎ writes fail, every later call returns an error that carries the cause, and 
  ▎ the instance signals the stop to the application. It then tries once to 
  ▎ release the lease. It does not retry, and it does not reopen itself.

  ▎ Recovery from a stop is a normal start. The application reopens the 
  ▎ database, and the instance catches the cache up from its recorded position, 
  ▎ as after a crash. If the cache cannot be opened, the start fails, and the 
  ▎ cache is not rebuilt automatically.

  The rest would go in AGENTS.md in place of the open question: the two buggify
  variants and the invariant, the lean towards aborting reads, that uniform stop
  and no automatic reopen are "for now", and a pointer to the research, which
  I'd save as a note in docs/research/.

  Do you agree to this wording and that split?

✻ Baked for 16s · done Tuesday 4:53 PM

❯ But that’s wrong? The commit is acked once s3 has it, and normally the cache  
  will have it too.                                                             
                                                                                
  Stop putting decisions into AGENTS.md, we need a better spot for it. We want  
  to keep that file lean, not a dumping ground.                                 

⏺ You're right, I framed it backwards. The cache is not a condition for the
  acknowledgement; committing to it is a step Commit takes before returning. One
  thing must survive from the old rule: Commit does not return straight after
  the swap, or a read right after it could still see the old state.

  Reworded:

  ▎ A commit is acknowledged once S3 has it. Commit returns success if the 
  ▎ manifest swap succeeded. Before it returns, the instance commits the 
  ▎ transaction to the local cache, or stops if that fails. A read that starts 
  ▎ after a commit is acknowledged therefore always sees it.

  The other two constraints stay as I proposed them.

  Nothing from this goes into AGENTS.md; where it goes instead is the next
  question.

  Does this wording match what you mean?

✻ Cogitated for 17s · done Tuesday 4:56 PM

✻ Worked for 1h 4m 13s · done Tuesday 5:09 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## SQLite binding: report from binding-builder

  I built the binding in `internal/sqlite`, cut to Markus's minimal scope, with
  the vendored source, cross-checks, sanitizer and fuzz runs, and CI. Six
  commits are on `worktree-sqlite-binding` (6060454..132a86f), not pushed. `make
  test` and `make lint` pass on this machine (lint: "0 issues." in both
  modules), and the final `make test-asan FUZZTIME=60s` run passed with no
  sanitizer reports. I wrote Steps 2–5 in
  `docs/diary/2026-11-10-sqlite-binding.md` (author binding-builder), including
  Markus's words verbatim.

  ### What I built and where
  All paths are under
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/sqlite-binding/`.
  - **`internal/sqlite/c/`**: the official 3.54.1 amalgamation, unmodified
  (`sqlite3.c`, `sqlite3.h`, `sqlite3ext.h`, `shell.c`).
    - `c/source.json` records the URL
  (https://sqlite.org/2026/sqlite-amalgamation-3540100.zip), the size, the
  SHA3-256 (92281c86…8e30, the same as sqlite.org's download page), the zip's
  SHA-256 and each file's SHA-256.
  - **`internal/sqlite/update/`**: the tool behind `make sqlite-update
  VERSION=x.y.z` and `make sqlite-verify`.
    - It also writes `internal/sqlite/source.h` (version and file hashes).
  Without it, the go command does not rebuild the binding when files in `c/`
  change; both reviewers showed it building stale.
  - **`internal/sqlite/sqlite.go`**: the binding. All C calls are in this one
  file.
    - `options.h` holds the compile options; `asan.go` and `noasan.go` say
  whether the build uses `-asan`; `README.md` (from a writer sub-agent, which I
  reviewed) explains vendoring, updating and testing.
  - **`internal/sqlite/crosscheck/`**: a separate module for the tests against
  mattn.
  - **Elsewhere**: `Makefile` targets `fuzz`, `test-asan`, `sqlite-update` and
  `sqlite-verify`; `make test` and `make lint` also cover the nested module.
  There are new CI jobs and one paragraph in the top-level README.

  ### The minimum API, decided from slice 1
  - `Open(path)` (read-write, create, extended result codes) and `Conn.Close`.
  - `Conn.Prepare`: exactly one statement. It refuses trailing SQL, comments,
  NUL bytes and empty SQL, because SQLite would otherwise ignore part of the
  input.
  - `Conn.Exec`: written in Go over Prepare, Step and Finalize.
  - `Stmt.BindInt64`, `Stmt.Step`, `Stmt.ColumnCount`, `Stmt.ColumnName`,
  `Stmt.ColumnType`, `Stmt.ColumnInt64`, `Stmt.Finalize`.
  - `Error{Code ResultCode (extended), Message}` with `Code.Primary()`.
  - `Version`, `SourceID`, `CompileOptions`.

  There is no reset, no `sqlite3_exec` and no transaction API: each statement
  runs in its own transaction, as SQLite does by default. I kept two calls
  beyond the strict list:
  - **`ColumnType`**: without it, an integer read cannot tell an integer from
  NULL or a converted value.
  - **`ColumnName`**: `select *` results have names that the oracle should
  compare.

  Strike them if you disagree.

  Misuse of the column methods panics. That covers a read without a current row,
  an index out of range, and any column call on a finalized statement. Bind and
  Step on a finalized statement return `SQLITE_MISUSE`.

  ### Compile options (`options.h`), for review
  The rule: an option must implement a roadmap restriction, make misuse
  detectable or impossible, or be needed by the build. Options that only tune
  performance are left out.
  - **`SQLITE_DQS=0`**: double quotes mean identifiers only (roadmap). One
  exception remains: SQLite reads any identifier after `DEFAULT`, quoted or not,
  as a string. That is pinned by a test, and the engine must reject it itself.
  - **`SQLITE_DEFAULT_FOREIGN_KEYS=1`**: foreign keys are on for every new
  connection (roadmap).
  - **`SQLITE_THREADSAFE=1`**: SQLite's default, stated explicitly. It protects
  SQLite's own structures if a connection is misused from two goroutines.
  - **`SQLITE_ENABLE_API_ARMOR`**: misuse of the C API returns `SQLITE_MISUSE`
  instead of undefined behaviour. No SQL result changes.
  - **`SQLITE_OMIT_AUTORESET`**: stepping a finished or failed statement returns
  `SQLITE_MISUSE` instead of silently running it again. The exception is after
  `SQLITE_BUSY` or `SQLITE_LOCKED`, where SQLite still restarts the statement.
  - **`SQLITE_OMIT_SHARED_CACHE`**: shared-cache mode is obsolete and changes
  isolation.
  - **`SQLITE_OMIT_LOAD_EXTENSION`**: the binding cannot load extensions, and
  without this Linux also needs `-ldl`.

  Left out on purpose:
  - `SQLITE_OMIT_DEPRECATED`: I tested it, and it does not remove the deprecated
  PRAGMAs as I had assumed. It does change how the default cache size is
  chosen.
  - `SQLITE_LIKE_DOESNT_MATCH_BLOBS`: it changes `LIKE` on blobs compared with
  stock SQLite.
  - `SQLITE_ENABLE_MATH_FUNCTIONS`: the official CLI has it.
  - `SQLITE_TRUSTED_SCHEMA=0`, and the performance options (`WAL_SYNCHRONOUS`,
  `MEMSTATUS`, `OMIT_DECLTYPE` and so on).

  ### Symbol clash: finding and decision
  I linked a plain copy of the amalgamation and `mattn/go-sqlite3` v1.14.53 into
  one test binary; the diary has the verbatim errors.
  - **macOS (Apple clang 21)**: `duplicate symbol '_sqlite3_vsnprintf' in: …`,
  then `ld: 271 duplicate symbols`.
  - **Linux (GCC 14.2.0)**: 271 errors of the form ``multiple definition of
  `sqlite3_mutex_free'; …sqlite3-binding.c:30683: first defined here``.

  Decision: file-local symbols. The amalgamation is `#include`d into the cgo
  preamble of `sqlite.go` after `#define SQLITE_API static`, so all of SQLite is
  static in that one translation unit. The two copies then link and run side by
  side.

  Guards:
  - **`TestSymbols`** reads a freshly built test binary with `debug/macho` or
  `debug/elf` and fails on any global `sqlite3*` symbol. Removing the define
  makes it fail with 276 globals.
  - **The cross-check module** only links if the symbols stay local.

  Two consequences:
  - Every C call must stay in `sqlite.go`, and that file must never use
  `//export`.
  - Two copies of SQLite must never open the same database file in one process,
  because POSIX locks belong to the process. This is documented, and the
  cross-checks use `:memory:` only.

  ### Cross-checks
  **Shell.** The tests build `sqlite3` from `c/shell.c` and `c/sqlite3.c` with
  `options.h` and the compiler from `go env CC` (`-noinit`, so no rc file is
  read). They assert:
  - the same version and source ID as the binding;
  - exactly the same `pragma compile_options` list, including `COMPILER=`;
  - the same results for slice-1 statements, with each value encoded in SQL;
  - exactly the same error messages.

  Building the shell without `options.h` makes these tests fail.

  **mattn v1.14.53.** I verified that it bundles 3.54.1, with the same source
  ID. The tests check:
  - that the two are separate copies (FK default 1 in ours and 0 in mattn's;
  `DQS=0` only in ours);
  - version and source ID;
  - that mattn's `sqlite3-binding.c` and `.h` are byte-identical to ours apart
  from its `USE_LIBSQLITE3` wrapper;
  - integer binding with single and several numbered and named parameters, typed
  reads, column names, and extended codes and messages for slice-1 scripts,
  plus two differential fuzz tests.

  Statement reuse is not compared, because the binding has no reset.

  Together, any drift in version or source between the three references fails a
  test.

  ### Sanitizer and fuzz runs
  Go's `-asan` does not work on darwin (`-asan is not supported on
  darwin/arm64`). `make test-asan` therefore runs itself in `golang:1.27.3`
  (linux/arm64) with Docker.
  - **Probe**: I checked that ASan fires in that container with a deliberate
  heap overflow: "ERROR: AddressSanitizer: heap-buffer-overflow".
  - **Lookaside**: SQLite's lookaside allocator hid use-after-finalize from
  ASan. Under `-asan`, `Open` now turns lookaside off at runtime, so the compile
  options are unchanged. A temporary canary (prepare, finalize, then read the
  freed statement) now gets "heap-use-after-free" under `-asan`. Without the
  sanitizer it silently reads 3. I removed the canary afterwards.
  - **Final run, after all fixes**: every test in both modules under `-asan`,
  then each fuzz target for 60s under `-asan`, exit 0, no reports. Execs:
  FuzzStmt_BindInt64 135,104, FuzzConn_Prepare 80,624, FuzzBindInt64 118,009,
  FuzzSlice1 23,479.
  - **Earlier runs**: a 60s-per-target run before the review also passed under
  `-asan`, and so did native runs without ASan of 60s per target.
  - **What did not run**: native macOS with `-fsanitize=address` in `CGO_CFLAGS`
  instruments only the C side. I used it for a probe, not as the real run.
  - **Limitation**: Go's fuzzer measures coverage of Go code only, so for the C
  code fuzzing is close to random mutation of the seeds.

  ### CI
  - **Existing reusable test workflow**: already runs `go test -race ./...` with
  cgo, which includes the binding and the shell cross-check.
  - **`crosscheck`**: tidy check, race tests and lint for the nested module,
  with `setup-go` pointed at that module's `go.mod` and `go.sum`.
  - **`asan`**: `make test-asan FUZZTIME=30s`; it prints any failing fuzz input.
  - **`sqlite-verify`**: downloads from sqlite.org on every run, so it fails
  whenever sqlite.org is down.

  None of this has run on GitHub yet, because nothing is pushed.

  ### Dependencies
  `mattn/go-sqlite3` is only in the nested module. Its `internal` import works
  because Go's rule is about import paths, not modules. The main `go.mod` gained
  only `maragu.dev/is` for tests.

  ### Self-review
  Two competing reviewers checked the code, then I fixed everything both found,
  plus the serious single-reviewer findings:
  - the build cache going stale when the vendored files change;
  - ASan being blind to statements used after finalize, because of lookaside;
  - stale `SQLITE_MISUSE` messages, such as "no more rows available (code 21)";
  - column reads without a current row returning a plausible 0;
  - `-verify` trusting its own manifest: it now requires the sqlite.org URL and
  checks against the download page;
  - fuzz targets sharing one connection: `pragma hard_heap_limit=1` broke every
  later `Open` in the process;
  - the shell reading the user's sqliterc file;
  - weak comparisons of shell errors and of the option list;
  - the CI `go-version-file` path;
  - comments that named their callers, and single-use constants.

  Before the review I had also caught that SQLite re-runs a finished statement
  on the next step (auto-reset), which is why `SQLITE_OMIT_AUTORESET` is in the
  options.

  ### Not verified, or left open
  - `go mod vendor` drops `internal/sqlite/c/`, so a module that vendors s4ql
  cannot build. Not fixed, because nothing depends on s4ql yet.
  - The bulk allocation in SQLite's page cache is also invisible to ASan. The
  glue does not touch pages, so I left it.
  - There is no permanent ASan canary, because it would need a C entry point in
  production code. The check is recorded in the diary instead.

  ### Decisions for Markus
  1. Approve the compile-option list and its rule, including the options left
  out above. Turning lookaside off under `-asan` only is my call and can be
  revisited.
  2. SQLite updates are gated on mattn's releases: `TestVersion` and
  `TestSource` fail until mattn bundles the same version. That is the drift
  guard you asked for, but it ties our upgrade schedule to mattn's. Decide what
  happens when mattn lags.
  3. Whether to keep `ColumnType` and `ColumnName` beyond the strict minimum,
  and whether column misuse should panic or return an error.
  4. Whether the `sqlite-verify` CI job may depend on sqlite.org being up.

  ### For you, the lead
  - `AGENTS.md` still says the symbol clash is "Not yet verified". The diary now
  has the evidence; updating the memory entry is your call.
  - No GitHub issue is referenced in the commits, and issue #1 (govulncheck) is
  untouched.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The binding is built and is up as PR #2
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/2), which I opened so that CI runs; the
  new CI jobs have never run on GitHub before. make test and make lint pass on
  this machine when I run them myself.

  - Symbol clash, answered: linking two plain copies of SQLite fails with 271
    duplicate symbols on both macOS and Linux. The builder compiles our copy
    with file-local symbols, so both link into one binary. A test fails if any
    SQLite symbol becomes global again.
  - Scope: integers only, as you asked: open, prepare, bind, step, read,
    finalize, and errors with extended result codes. There is no reset and no
    transaction API yet.
  - Cross-checks: the binding is compared against the official shell built from
    the same source and options, and against mattn/go-sqlite3. mattn lives in a
    nested module, so the main go.mod does not depend on it. A test fails if the
    three drift apart in version.
  - Sanitizer and fuzzing: Go's address sanitizer does not work on macOS, so it
    runs in a Linux container. All tests and 60 seconds per fuzz target passed
    with no reports.
  - Limits: Go's fuzzer only sees coverage of Go code, so for the C side it is
    close to random input. A project that vendors s4ql with go mod vendor would
    not build, because Go drops the C directory; nothing depends on s4ql yet.

  The builder raised four points. I'm taking two myself and will tell you what I
  chose; two are yours.

  First: the compile options, which shape the oracle. The builder's rule is that
  an option must implement a roadmap restriction, make misuse detectable, or be
  needed by the build, and nothing is added for performance. That gives:

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            Option             │                  Effect                  │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLITE_DQS=0                  │ Double quotes mean identifiers only      │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLITE_DEFAULT_FOREIGN_KEYS=1 │ Foreign keys on for every connection     │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLITE_THREADSAFE=1           │ SQLite's default, stated explicitly      │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLITE_ENABLE_API_ARMOR       │ Misuse of the C API returns an error     │
  │                               │ instead of undefined behaviour           │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                               │ Stepping a finished statement is an      │
  │ SQLITE_OMIT_AUTORESET         │ error instead of silently running it     │
  │                               │ again                                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLITE_OMIT_SHARED_CACHE      │ Removes the obsolete shared-cache mode   │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLITE_OMIT_LOAD_EXTENSION    │ No extension loading                     │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  I recommend approving the rule and the list. Do you agree?

✻ Cooked for 1m 9s · done Tuesday 5:15 PM

❯ Sounds good. Any other good compile time options to consider?                 

⏺ Three are worth considering, all within the builder's rule. This is from my
  own knowledge of SQLite's options, so a builder would verify each one before
  adding it.

  - SQLITE_MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0: removes memory-mapped I/O entirely. It is off by
    default, but with it on, a disk error arrives as a signal that kills the
    process, not as an error code. That would bypass the stop we agreed on
    today, so making it impossible fits the rule.
  - SQLITE_OMIT_UTF16: implements the roadmap's "UTF-8 only" inside SQLite. One
    caution: SQLite's documentation says the omit options are less tested and
    may not all work with the amalgamation, so this needs checking first.
  - SQLITE_DEBUG, in a test-only build: switches on SQLite's thousands of
    internal assertions. It is slow, so it would be a second variant for the
    sanitizer job, alongside the production options and never in place of them.

  Options I'd leave out for now:

  - Feature switches such as SQLITE_ENABLE_MATH_FUNCTIONS get added when a slice
    needs the feature.
  - Hardening such as SQLITE_TRUSTED_SCHEMA=0 protects against hostile SQL or
    schemas, and only our own engine and tests talk to SQLite.
  - Performance options from SQLite's recommended list are excluded by the rule.

  I recommend adding the first now, the second if it checks out, and the third
  as a follow-up to the sanitizer job. Shall I have a builder do that?

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Recommended default configuration (11 options)

  I classified all 214 names on compile.html: 203 option entries in sections 3
  to 12, the 10 size limits in section 5, and `SQLITE_CUSTOM_INCLUDE`. A script
  confirmed none is missing. Marks: [V] read in the docs, [T] tested in this
  run, [S] read in the vendored `sqlite3.c`, [I] inferred. Nothing in the
  worktree was changed.

  **Keep the seven already in `options.h`:**
  1. `SQLITE_DQS=0`: roadmap restriction. It is on the official recommended list
  [V].
  2. `SQLITE_DEFAULT_FOREIGN_KEYS=1`: roadmap restriction. A connection can
  still turn it off at run time, so assert it when a connection opens [V/I].
  3. `SQLITE_THREADSAFE=1`: SQLite's default (serialized). The binding never
  passes `SQLITE_OPEN_NOMUTEX` [V/S].
  4. `SQLITE_ENABLE_API_ARMOR`: turns some API misuse into `SQLITE_MISUSE`
  instead of undefined behaviour [V, T].
  5. `SQLITE_OMIT_AUTORESET`: an extra step returns MISUSE instead of re-running
  the statement. Three steps returned 100, 101, 21 [T].
  6. `SQLITE_OMIT_SHARED_CACHE`: obsolete mode, removed [V].
  7. `SQLITE_OMIT_LOAD_EXTENSION`: no extension loading [V].

  **Add now:**
  8. `SQLITE_MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0`: memory-mapped database I/O can then never be
  switched on by a PRAGMA. The stock maximum of 0x7fff0000 let `PRAGMA
  mmap_size=268435456` take effect; with 0 it returns 0 [T].
  9. `SQLITE_OMIT_DEPRECATED`: on the always-tested recommended list, "the right
  thing to do" [V]. It removes deprecated APIs.
  10. `SQLITE_STRICT_SUBTYPE=1`: on the always-tested recommended list. It makes
  subtype misuse in custom functions an error, and affects only
  application-defined functions [V].
  11. `SQLITE_ENABLE_MATH_FUNCTIONS`: the docs say configure adds it on unix
  [V], so stock-built SQLite has these functions. Our build currently fails with
  "no such function: sqrt" [T]. It adds 30 functions and changes nothing else
  [T].

  **Add later:** `SQLITE_ENABLE_PERCENTILE` (median and percentile) when a slice
  needs it. `SQLITE_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT` if read connections must pin a WAL
  snapshot [I].

  **Test-only build variant:** `SQLITE_DEBUG`, optionally with
  `SQLITE_MEMDEBUG`.

  The candidate set and the debug variant compile cleanly both as the binding
  (`SQLITE_API static`) and as the shell, and all options show up in
  `compile_options` [T].

  ## Notable leave-outs
  - **Recommended by SQLite, but they change results:**
  `LIKE_DOESNT_MATCH_BLOBS` ("breaking backwards compatibility") and
  `MAX_EXPR_DEPTH=0` (removes the depth error that protects the stack) [V].
  - **Recommended by SQLite, but they remove useful tools:**
  `DEFAULT_MEMSTATUS=0` (memory limits stop working), `OMIT_PROGRESS_CALLBACK`,
  `OMIT_DECLTYPE`, and `OMIT_AUTOINIT` (a forgotten init can segfault) [V].
  - **Durability trade-off, decide per connection:**
  `DEFAULT_WAL_SYNCHRONOUS=1`.
  - **They change results compared with stock:** `CASE_SENSITIVE_LIKE`,
  `ENABLE_ICU`, and `PRINTF_PRECISION_LIMIT` ("silently truncated") [V].
  - **Better switched per connection by the harness:**
  `REVERSE_UNORDERED_SELECTS` [I].
  - **Cannot be used with the amalgamation:** `ENABLE_UPDATE_DELETE_LIMIT` and
  `ENABLE_ORDERED_SET_AGGREGATES` [V]. The same goes for the 16 omit options
  that the code generators must know about (howtocompile.html) [V]. Some others
  are silently wrong: `OMIT_CHECK` accepts CHECK constraints but does not
  enforce them [V], and `OMIT_TRIGGER` turns off foreign-key enforcement [V].
  `UNTESTABLE` is "discouraged" [V].
  - **No correctness reason:** FTS, RTREE, STAT4 and the other shell extras.
  - **Run-time settings, not compile options:** defensive mode,
  `trusted_schema`, `cell_size_check`, limits, and `FP_DIGITS`.

  **Cache versus oracle:** `MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0` helps the cache. The math and
  percentile functions help the oracle. `SQLITE_DEBUG` is good in tests and bad
  in production.

  **Difference from a developer's `sqlite3`:** there is no single stock build to
  match. Apple's `/usr/bin/sqlite3` has `DQS=3` and `UPDATE ... LIMIT`, and
  mattn has neither `DQS=0` nor foreign keys on by default [T]. Each option that
  SQL can see is also a place where the binding differs from mattn, which the
  cross-check has to allow for [I].

  ## Your three claims
  - **`MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0`:** "Off by default" is right [V]. The signal claim is
  right: "the I/O error causes a signal which, if not caught by the application,
  results in a program crash" (mmap.html) [V]. "Removes it entirely" is
  imprecise: it removes memory mapping of the database file only. In WAL mode
  "The wal-index is implemented using an ordinary file that is mmapped"
  (wal.html) [V], and the source keeps `mmap()` whenever WAL is compiled in [S].
  The spec's parallel reads need WAL [I], so an I/O failure on the `-shm` file
  can still arrive as a signal.
  - **`OMIT_UTF16`:** The docs warn: "The SQLITE_OMIT_* options may not work
  with the amalgamation" and "are mostly unsupported ... not all SQLITE_OMIT_*
  options are tested. Some ... might cause SQLite to malfunction and/or provide
  incorrect answers" [V]. This option is not on the list of options the code
  generators must know, and it compiled and ran with the amalgamation [V, T].
  "Implements UTF-8 only" is imprecise:
    - It removes `PRAGMA encoding` altogether [T].
    - Opening an existing UTF-16 file reported "malformed database schema (11)",
  a corruption error rather than a clear rejection [T, S].
    - It is not in the tested option set.

    I recommend leaving it out. Instead, open with `sqlite3_open_v2`, reject
  PRAGMA in the engine, and assert the encoding when a connection opens. Without
  the option, `PRAGMA encoding='UTF-16le'` does work on a fresh database [T].
  - **`SQLITE_DEBUG`:** Right: "thousands of assert() statements", about "three
  times slower", "for testing and analysis" [V]. Add these caveats:
    - It adds 9 pragmas (such as `vdbe_trace`) [T].
    - In the shell build it adds 9 SQL functions (such as `json_parse`) [T].
    - It implies `ENABLE_EXPLAIN_COMMENTS` [V, T].
    - A failed assert aborts the process [I].

  ## The seven chosen options and the docs' warnings
  Of the seven, only `DQS=0` and `OMIT_SHARED_CACHE` are on the official
  recommended list. The list's `THREADSAFE` entry is `=0`, the opposite of ours
  [V]. The docs themselves counter the omit warning for two of the three omits:
  `OMIT_SHARED_CACHE` is in the "always fully tested" set [V], and
  howtocompile.html uses `OMIT_LOAD_EXTENSION` directly on the amalgamation [V].
  Neither of those, nor `OMIT_AUTORESET`, needs the parser regenerated, and all
  three work in this build [V, T]. Apple ships `OMIT_AUTORESET` and
  `OMIT_LOAD_EXTENSION` [T]. For `API_ARMOR` the docs say: "Applications should
  not depend on SQLITE_ENABLE_API_ARMOR for safety" [V].

  ## A docs claim that failed a test
  On this amalgamation, `OMIT_DEPRECATED` removed only the `default_cache_size`
  pragma. `count_changes`, `full_column_names` and `short_column_names` still
  took effect, although the docs say they are omitted [T]. It is still worth
  setting for the API removal.

  The full classification, with a quote or reference for every option, is in
  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9e
  b0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/compile-options/classification.md`. The test builds
  and scripts are in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f

  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9e

    assertions, runs about three times slower, and a failed assertion aborts the
    process.

  SQLITE_LIKE_DOESNT_MATCH_BLOBS and SQLITE_MAX_EXPR_DEPTH=0.

  each differ from ours in options that SQL can see.

⏺ Agent "Review SQLite compile options from docs" finished · 12m 19s

❯ But do we need debug outside dev on SQLite itself?                            

⏺ No. Its assertions check SQLite's own internal invariants, and SQLite's
  developers already run them in their test suite. For our thin glue, the
  address sanitizer, SQLITE_ENABLE_API_ARMOR and the cross-checks cover the
  misuse it might catch, and a second build variant is extra machinery to
  maintain. I'd drop it; it stays available if we ever chase a suspected problem
  inside SQLite.

  That leaves the builder's seven plus SQLITE_MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0,
  SQLITE_ENABLE_MATH_FUNCTIONS and SQLITE_STRICT_SUBTYPE=1, and no debug
  variant. Do you agree to that list?

  be stale.

  The builder is also handling the two points I decided myself:

  - ColumnType and ColumnName stay. Without the first, an integer read cannot
    tell an integer from NULL. The oracle needs the second to compare column
    names. Misuse of the column methods keeps panicking, as a programming error
    in an internal package.
  - Source verification in CI will run only when the vendored SQLite or its
    update tool changes, so a sqlite.org outage cannot fail unrelated work.

  It also saves the full classification of the 214 options as a research note,
  and records the WAL memory-mapping gap in docs/todo.md.

  The remaining decision for you: SQLite upgrades are tied to mattn's releases.

  The cross-check fails unless mattn bundles exactly our SQLite version, which
  is the drift guard we asked for. The consequence is that we can only move to a
  new SQLite once mattn has. For 3.54.1 that took about three weeks, going by
  the dates in the research note.

  I recommend keeping the gate. We pin SQLite on our own terms and have no
  reason to chase releases. If an urgent SQLite fix ever arrives before mattn
  has it, we skip that one check deliberately and for that upgrade only.

  Do you agree to keep the gate?

  ## How mattn's build differs for the new options

  I found this by running SQL against mattn v1.14.53 in a throwaway test, not by
  reading its source, which is outside the worktree.
  - **Memory map:** mattn reports `MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0x7fff0000`, and `pragma
  mmap_size` raises the mapping on a file database. On an in-memory database the
  pragma returns no row at all.
  - **Math functions:** none by default ("no such function: sqrt"). With the
  build tag `sqlite_math_functions` it has them and reports
  `ENABLE_MATH_FUNCTIONS`. I did not turn that tag on, because it would change
  the Makefile and the CI crosscheck job.
  - **`OMIT_DEPRECATED`:** mattn already sets it.

  The existing comparisons touch none of these, so nothing broke. A new
  `TestCompileOptions` in `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/crosscheck_test.go` pins
  the four differences SQL can see (foreign keys, double-quoted strings, `sqrt`,
  `mmap_size`), so a change in mattn's build shows up there.

  ## Self-review

  The code-review skill (medium) returned seven findings; I fixed two:
  - **Fixed:** a pragma without results compared `[""]` with the binding's
  `nil`. My change caused this by running every `pragma` as a query; it is now
  covered by a `pragma user_version` comparison.
  - **Fixed:** a doc comment about `:memory:` had ended up on the wrong helper.

  ## Decisions for you

  1. **`options.h` rule:** I widened the rule at the top so that it covers the
  two new kinds of reason. It now also allows an option that "implements a rule
  in docs/spec.md" or "makes this build match what a normal build of SQLite
  has". Under the old wording, neither `MAX_MMAP_SIZE` nor
  `ENABLE_MATH_FUNCTIONS` qualified. Please confirm the wording.
  2. **Research note opening:** it says the agreed set departs from the
  recommendations in two places. `SQLITE_OMIT_DEPRECATED`, marked SET, was left
  out, and `SQLITE_DEBUG`, marked TEST, is not used because there is no
  test-only build.

✻ Brewed for 57s · done Tuesday 7:52 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  The brief is written and saved to
  /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb
  0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/driver-design-brief.md. Nothing in the repository
  changed. In the brief, [V] means read this run in a primary source or the
  project's notes, and [I] means inferred; the tags below follow it.

  **What makes it hard**
  - **Go does not let us choose which goroutine runs next.** `testing/synctest`
  fixes time, not order. The Go blog (testing-time, 2025-08-26) says timers due
  at the same instant now fire in random order [V].
  - **The single path has to keep running while a write transaction is open.**
  Transactions are interactive and a statement can be long, but the lease still
  has to be renewed on the same path. So a statement must be an action in
  flight, with several in flight at once. That does not fit the spec's "answers
  with the next action" (singular).
  - **Renewal and commit both compare-and-swap `manifest.json`.** Only one owner
  of the ETag can work, or our own renewal makes our commit fail with 412.
  - **cgo is fine for determinism but cannot replay parallelism.** synctest
  counts a goroutine inside a cgo call as running, not blocked, so `Wait` simply
  waits for the call to return [V: `runtime/synctest.go` and `cgocall.go` in Go
  1.27.3, and `go doc`]. SQLite results depend only on the order of calls. What
  no replayable run can show is two SQLite calls truly running at once.
  - **The race detector cannot check the stop requirement's logic.** A correct
  gate built on atomics or a mutex has no data race to report, even when its
  logic is wrong.
  - **The failing `COMMIT` returns its error on whatever goroutine ran it.**
  Every hop from there to the read gate is a window in which a read can start.
  - **The lease depends on clocks.** On some systems Go's monotonic clock stops
  while the computer is asleep [V `go doc time`].

  **Shared by every design:** a pure `Core.Handle(now, event) []Action` with
  numbered actions, several in flight. Readers never go through the path. They
  do one atomic load of a `ReadState` the path publishes (phase, lease deadline,
  cause). The wrapper around every cache call trips that state to Stopped the
  moment a call fails, before the event reaches the core. Readers check after
  their snapshot is fixed, not before.

  **The four designs**
  - **A: two drivers.** Production has a loop goroutine, a worker that owns the
  write connection, a goroutine per S3 call, and an inbox channel. The
  simulation is a separate single-goroutine event loop that calls the core and
  the executors directly. Replay is exact, but the production driver and the
  blocking public API are never in it. Polar Signals names this risk ("code …
  leaks up into the production driver") [V]. It also strains the spec's
  "production code runs unchanged".
  - **B: one driver plus a seeded baton scheduler on synctest.** The real
  library runs in the bubble. Every point that meets the outside world parks
  under a stable ID: S3 calls, timers, reader yield points, workload actors. The
  scheduler calls `synctest.Wait()` and then releases exactly one goroutine by
  seed. Interleavings replay at yield points and API calls. Every run is done
  twice and compared, like FoundationDB's "unseed" check and SlateDB's
  determinism test, so any leaked nondeterminism fails the test. The discipline
  is not enforced by the language: no runtime timers, never block while holding
  a mutex (mutexes are not durably blocking [V]), and stable IDs. Under the race
  detector the baton mode hides races, because parking before `Wait` creates a
  happens-before edge [V]. So `-race` must run in a free-running mode. The baton
  mode only works under `go test` [V].
  - **C: caller-runs.** The core sits under a mutex, and whichever goroutine has
  the event runs the actions. The simulation is the same as B. The risk is that
  work moves between goroutines (a deferred commit swap gets handed across),
  with re-entrancy rules on every path.
  - **D: real concurrency.** The seed fixes faults, not order. The core replays
  exactly from a logged event trace, not from the seed. It exercises true
  parallelism, but failures are flaky, and it needs the spec's "same seed
  replays the same run" amended.
  - **Left out:** WebAssembly (no cgo), `GOMAXPROCS=1` (Polar Signals: does not
  work [V]), and gosim (immature). Antithesis is a complement, not a
  replacement.

  **Lease check options**
  - **L1 (recommended):** after the snapshot is fixed, load `ReadState` and
  require `now < deadline`. The deadline is the send time of the last successful
  swap plus the lease length minus a margin, on the monotonic clock, published
  only after the swap succeeds. A pause before the snapshot is caught by the
  check. A pause after it is harmless, because the snapshot was taken while the
  lease was held.
  - **L1+:** also require the wall clock (or Linux `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`) to agree.
  This covers a system suspend or VM pause that stops the monotonic clock.
  - **Rejected:** checking before the snapshot, and a timer that flips a flag.
  Both are unsafe under pauses.
  - **Contender side, two options:** an absolute expiry in the manifest, which
  needs bounded skew between machines (Litestream does this, with no margin), or
  seeing the manifest unchanged for a full lease period on its own clock, which
  needs only bounded drift. The spec says only "has a timeout".

  **Recommendation:** Design B with the write connection owned by one worker
  goroutine, every statement going through the loop. Add D's free-running mode
  in the same harness for `-race` stress, and use L1.
  - **Why:** the rules most likely to break live in the driver and API code:
  `Commit` returns nil only if committed, no read starts after the stop, waiting
  writers fail, replies arrive once. B is the only exact-replay design that
  covers that code. It also matches "production code runs unchanged" and says
  plainly what replays. One owner of the write connection means a stop can roll
  back without racing the application.
  - **What would change my mind:** a spike (toy core, real SQLite binding, baton
  scheduler) that cannot pass the run-twice check would send me to A plus
  free-running stress. Measured statement latency would justify lending the
  connection to the writer's goroutine. Reading the driver as outside
  "production code" makes A attractive. Accepting replay from a recorded trace
  makes D viable as the main mode.

  **Decisions, most consequential first**
  1. What must a seed replay exactly: the core only (A), the whole library at
  yield-point granularity (B/C), or fault decisions plus trace replay, with a
  spec change (D)?
  2. Does "write transactions run on the single path" mean every statement, or
  only grant, commit, rollback and stop, with statements on the application's
  goroutine?
  3. Production shape: a loop goroutine that owns the core, or caller-runs under
  a mutex?
  4. Lease clocks: absolute expiry or "unchanged for a full period" for
  contenders; monotonic only, or monotonic plus wall clock, for the holder?
  5. When the deadline has passed but the instance has not stopped, does a read
  fail at once or wait for its context?
  6. How tight is the stop gate? Tripping the state at detection leaves a tiny
  window that is still linearizable. Blocking read starts during every local
  commit closes it, at the cost of read stalls during each commit's fsync.
  7. After losing the lease: stop, or refuse reads and try to take it back with
  a catch-up?
  8. Is there a maximum duration for a write transaction?

  **Ambiguities found in the spec and to-do list** (section 7 of the brief has
  twelve)
  - "Runs unchanged", "single path" and "next action" are unclear for a driver
  and for interactive transactions.
  - "Same seed replays" needs a stated granularity.
  - The lease's timeout semantics and the clock assumption for reads are
  missing. The kickoff's "Clocks never affect writes …" text (transcript line
  2777) never reached the spec.
  - "No read can start between the failure and `Commit` returning" cannot be
  implemented as worded: a read can start between the failure inside SQLite and
  its detection. It is also stronger than linearizability needs.
  - "The race detector tests it too" cannot check the logic.
  - An unknown swap outcome also affects renewal swaps, not only commits.
  - SQLite can return `SQLITE_BUSY` in WAL mode in some cases
  (sqlite.org/wal.html §9), so the spec should say whether that counts as a
  local failure that stops the instance.

⏺ The brief is in, with four designs and eight decisions. Taking them one at a
  time, most consequential first.

  First decision: what must a seed replay exactly?

  The pure state machine replays trivially in every design. The question is how
  much of the code around it is also covered: the goroutines, the public API,
  and the parallel readers. Go does not let a program choose which goroutine
  runs next, and testing/synctest fixes time, not order.

  - A: the state machine only. The simulation is a separate single-threaded loop
    that calls the state machine directly. Replay is exact, but the production
    driver and the blocking API are never simulated. That is where rules like
    "Commit returns nil only if committed" and "no read starts after the stop"
    live.
  - B: the whole library, at defined points. The real library runs in the
    simulation. Every goroutine pauses wherever it meets the outside world: an
    S3 call, a timer, a reader's yield point. A test scheduler waits until all
    are paused and lets exactly one continue, chosen by the seed. Every run is
    done twice and compared, so any leaked nondeterminism fails the test. The
    cost is discipline the language does not enforce, for example never blocking
    while holding a mutex.
  - D: faults only. The seed decides which faults happen and goroutines run
    freely. A failure is replayed from a recorded event trace, not from the
    seed, so failures can be flaky. This needs the spec's "same seed replays the
    same run" amended.

  No design can replay two SQLite calls truly running at the same instant. In B
  only one goroutine runs at a time, so finding data races needs a second,
  free-running mode under the race detector.

  I recommend B plus that free-running mode, which is also the agent's
  recommendation. It is the only exact-replay option that covers the code most
  likely to be wrong, and it is what the spec's "production code runs unchanged"
  already promises. It is unproven, though: the agent suggests a spike first,
  with a toy state machine, the real SQLite binding and the scheduler, to show
  that the run-twice check passes. If it cannot, we fall back to A plus
  free-running stress.

  Do you agree to B, checked by that spike before we commit to it?

⏺ Agent "Driver design options brief" finished · 11m 13s

❯ Honestly I don’t understand that whole state machine stuff. Can we revisit    
  that decision entirely?                                                       

⏺ Yes, and I think it's worth revisiting. Here is what it is, in plain terms.

  What it means. There are two ways to write a commit. The plain way reads top
  to bottom and calls S3 and SQLite directly:

  func (db *DB) commit(tx *Tx) error {
      file := db.s3.Upload(tx.changes)
      db.s3.SwapManifest(file)
      return db.cache.Commit()
  }

  The state machine way splits deciding from doing. The logic never calls S3 or
  SQLite. It is told what just happened and answers what to do next, and
  separate "driver" code does the real calls and reports back:

  commit requested  →  "upload this file"
  upload finished   →  "swap the manifest"
  swap succeeded    →  "commit to the cache"
  cache committed   →  "tell the caller it is committed"

  Why it is in the spec. The spec says it "is what lets the simulation replay a
  run exactly". A test can feed that logic any sequence of events, including
  failures in odd orders, with no real S3, clock or goroutines.

  What today's research showed. That claim is weaker than it looked:

  - The state machine does not solve the hard part, which is the order
    goroutines run in. That lives in the driver, and so do the rules most likely
    to break.
  - It fits interactive transactions poorly, where the application runs its own
    code between statements.
  - If design B's seeded scheduler works, it makes plain code like the first
    example replay exactly too. The replay then comes from S3, time and
    randomness being behind interfaces, which the spec already requires, plus
    the scheduler. The shape of the code no longer matters.

  What dropping it costs. We lose the ability to test the commit logic against
  any order of events with no concurrency at all. We also lose the fallback: if
  the seeded scheduler turns out not to work, a state machine is the one design
  that still gives exact replay of the core.

  I recommend dropping it and writing plain blocking Go: one writer at a time,
  one owner of the manifest, everything external behind interfaces. Plain code
  is also code you can read and review, which matters when the goal is trust.
  The condition is the spike: it must show that the scheduler replays plain
  concurrent code. If it fails, we choose again between a state machine and
  giving up exact replay of ordering.

  Do you want to drop the state machine on those terms?

✻ Brewed for 1h 3m 27s · done Tuesday 8:55 PM

❯ Yes, no state machine. Why are goroutines and concurrency involved at all?    
  Because of the caller of the code?                                            

⏺ Mostly yes. There are two sources, and only one is ours.

  - The callers. An application such as a web server calls the database from
    many goroutines at once. The spec wants reads to run in parallel, and a
    second writer has to wait for the first. So the library must be safe to call
    concurrently, even if it starts no goroutines itself.
  - Lease renewal. The lease lasts 30 seconds and must be renewed even when no
    call is in progress, or while a write transaction sits open and the
    application runs its own code. That needs one background goroutine with a
    timer. It is the only concurrency the library creates.

  Everything else in the brief came from the state machine design: the loop
  goroutine, the worker that owns the write connection, the channel of events.
  Without it, each caller's goroutine does its own work top to bottom, a lock
  lets one writer through at a time, and the renewal goroutine takes the same
  lock around the manifest when it swaps.

  The simulation needs concurrency for the same two reasons. Its test plays
  several callers as goroutines, the renewal goroutine runs beside them, and the
  seeded scheduler decides who goes next.

  If that answers it, the next item is the spec wording that replaces the state
  machine sentence.

✻ Crunched for 16s · done Tuesday 8:58 PM

❯ Have another look at how foundationdb does testing, I liked their testing     
  section in inspiration.md in particular.                                      

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I reread the FoundationDB testing section in docs/inspiration.md and the
  research note behind it. The main point for us: FoundationDB does not use a
  state machine either.

  How FoundationDB gets determinism. Its production code is ordinary
  top-to-bottom code written as coroutines. Three things make it replayable:

  - Network, disk, time and randomness sit behind interfaces.
  - The whole cluster and the test clients run on one thread.
  - A task runs until it waits for something, and then the scheduler picks the
    next one. Nothing is ever interrupted midway.

  Why we ended up with a state machine. Go has no such scheduler. Our research
  note concluded that whole-program determinism "does not transfer" to Go, and
  recommended a state machine as the workaround. That is where the spec sentence
  came from.

  What design B is. It is FoundationDB's model rebuilt in Go: only one goroutine
  runs at a time, it runs until it reaches an S3 call, a timer or a yield
  point, and the seed picks who goes next. The test callers run inside the
  simulation too, as FoundationDB's do. So B is the closest we can get to the
  approach you liked, and dropping the state machine moves us towards it. It is
  still unproven in Go, which is what the spike is for.

  One difference that remains. FoundationDB simulates the disk, including torn
  writes and bit flips. We run real SQLite on real files, and decided today not
  to inject faults underneath it. Its paper names this as a limit: code outside
  the simulated runtime cannot be tested this way.

  What transfers as it is. The rest of that section does not depend on the
  scheduler question:

  - buggify with per-site activation, and randomised tuning values
  - the cycle and sideband workloads as oracles
  - stopping faults before the final checks
  - failing the run on any unexpected error
  - coverage probes for rare paths
  - rerunning a seed and comparing, to catch accidental nondeterminism

  Only deterministic simulation and buggify are in the spec so far.

  So the open decision is the same one as before, now with a clearer reason: do
  you agree to design B, checked by a spike before we commit to it?

✻ Churned for 31s · done Tuesday 9:01 PM

❯ State design B again                                                          

⏺ Design B: the real library, run one goroutine at a time under a seed.

  Production. Plain blocking Go, no state machine:

  - Each caller's goroutine does its own work top to bottom.
  - A lock lets one write transaction through at a time.
  - One background goroutine renews the lease.
  - S3, time and randomness are behind interfaces. SQLite is called through our
    binding.

  Simulation. The same library code, unchanged, inside a testing/synctest test:

  - Fakes. S3 is a simulated object store, time is fake, and randomness comes
    from the seed.
  - Callers. The test starts goroutines that play application callers.
  - Parking. Wherever a goroutine meets the outside world, it stops and waits:
    at an S3 call, at a timer, and at marked points in a read.
  - Scheduling. A scheduler waits until every goroutine is parked, picks one by
    the seed, lets it run to its next parking point, and repeats. So exactly one
    goroutine runs at a time, and the seed decides the order.
  - SQLite. Its calls run to completion inside whichever goroutine is running.
  - Faults. The fake S3 and the buggify points inject failures, also from the
    seed.
  - Self-check. Every seed is run twice and the two runs are compared, so any
    nondeterminism that leaks in fails the test.

  A second mode. The same tests run with the scheduler off, under the race
  detector. Goroutines then run truly in parallel, which finds data races, but a
  failure there cannot be replayed from the seed.

  What it costs. Three rules the compiler does not enforce:

  - All waiting on time goes through our clock interface.
  - No goroutine parks while holding an ordinary mutex. A lock held across I/O
    must be built on a channel.
  - Parking points have stable names.

  What it cannot do. Replay two SQLite calls running at the same instant. It
  also only works under go test.

  What is unproven. That this works at all in Go with real SQLite. The spike
  builds the scheduler with a toy workload and checks that running a seed twice
  gives the same result.

✻ Sautéed for 17s · done Tuesday 9:07 PM

❯ synctest doesn’t play well with CGo AFAIK                                     

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You may be right, and I can't rule it out from the documentation. I just read
  it for Go 1.27.3:

  - It does not mention cgo at all.
  - A goroutine only counts as "durably blocked" in five listed cases: channel
    operations, select, sync.Cond.Wait, sync.WaitGroup.Wait and time.Sleep.
    System calls and mutexes are named as not durably blocking.
  - So a goroutine inside a SQLite call is treated as still running, and the
    scheduler's wait lasts until that call returns.

  For design B that is the behaviour we want, as long as every SQLite call
  returns without outside help. It breaks where a call blocks on something
  another goroutine in the test has to do. The obvious case is SQLite waiting on
  a lock with a busy timeout: it sleeps in real time, the fake clock does not
  move, and the scheduler hangs. The design has to make sure that never happens,
  for example by having our own lock keep two writers out of SQLite.

  The research agent said it confirmed the "still running" behaviour in the
  runtime source. That is one agent's reading, not a test, and there may be
  problems neither of us knows about.

  So I'd make this the first question of the spike, before any scheduler is
  built: real SQLite calls from several goroutines inside a synctest test,
  including a deliberately blocked one, to see exactly what happens. If it
  fails, design B is off the table and we choose between the other options.

  Have you seen a specific problem with the two that I should point the spike
  at?

⏺ fabrik:builder(Spike seeded scheduler on synctest) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed, and I've started the spike on that basis: an Opus builder in the
  worktree sim-spike. I took your reply as the go-ahead for the spike only;
  whether we adopt design B waits for its result.

  Two details go with your point about Go-side locks:

  - The Go lock cannot be an ordinary mutex. synctest does not count waiting on
    a sync.Mutex as blocked, so a lock that is held while a goroutine is parked
    has to be built on a channel.
  - SQLite's own lock wait stays off. With no busy timeout it returns "busy" at
    once and never sleeps. If our Go lock is right, that error never appears, so
    seeing it means a bug of ours.

  The spike answers six questions in order and stops early if one fails:

  1. What synctest really does with SQLite calls, including one deliberately
     made to wait on a lock.
  2. Whether the seeded scheduler can run plain blocking code one goroutine at a
     time.
  3. Whether a toy library with our shape runs under it: fake S3 with a manifest
     swap, one writer, lease renewal, a SQLite cache, and parallel readers.
  4. Whether running a seed twice gives the same trace, over many seeds.
  5. Whether the same code runs freely under the race detector.
  6. Whether a planted concurrency bug is found and then replayed from its seed.

  It reports measured numbers only, and the rules our code would have to follow,
  with a way to enforce each one.

  One decision does not need to wait: the spec still describes the state 
  machine. The third bullet under "Concurrency" says state changes run on a
  single path, "written as a state machine that does no I/O itself". I propose
  deleting that bullet now, and adding a sentence about the seeded scheduler
  under "Testing" only if the spike passes.

  Do you agree to delete it?

  now has two rules: one write transaction at a time, and reads in parallel.

  returning" cannot be met exactly as worded: a read can start between a failure

⏺ Agent "Spike seeded scheduler on synctest" is waiting on its own background
