› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Verdict: adopt design B, with named conditions

  Plain blocking Go replays exactly from a seed under `testing/synctest`, with
  real SQLite through cgo. Over 10000 seeds, each run twice, no pair of traces
  differed, and the same seeds give the same traces on macOS, under `-race`, and
  under `-asan` on Linux arm64. The conditions are at the end. The main caveat:
  the simulation explores only interleavings at waits, which is a strict subset
  of what production Go can do.

  Everything is committed on `worktree-sim-spike` (three commits, not pushed, no
  pull request). `make test` and `make lint` pass. `go test -race -shuffle on
  ./internal/sim/` passes (42 s). `go test -asan ./internal/sim/` passes in the
  Linux container (98 s). I did not change `internal/sqlite`, the spec, or
  `docs/todo.md`.

  ## The six answers

  **1. `testing/synctest` with cgo: it works.** Go 1.27.3, SQLite 3.54.1, file
  database in WAL mode, one connection per goroutine.
  - `synctest.Wait` waits for a running SQLite call: 5 of 5 runs returned only
  after a 230 ms call.
  - Fake time does not advance while a goroutine is in C.
  - A SQLite call that waits on a lock (`busy_timeout = 1500`) does not hang the
  bubble and is not reported as a deadlock. It sleeps 1.6 s of real time and
  zero fake time, then returns `SQLITE_BUSY`.
  - If the lock holder sleeps on fake time, the waiter can never get the lock,
  however short the sleep, because fake time cannot move while the waiter is in
  C.
  - With `busy_timeout = 0` the waiter fails at once.
  - The bubble's own deadlock detection still works after SQLite calls.
  - The race detector does not treat ordering by fake time as synchronisation
  (it reported my own test); it does treat `synctest.Wait` as one.
  - **Finding against Markus's assumption:** a Go lock around the write
  connection does not stop SQLite from finding a lock taken. One writer among
  eight parallel readers, nothing else writing, `busy_timeout = 0`: `begin
  immediate` failed 27, 95, 31, 13, 28 times per 3000 transactions. With
  `busy_timeout = 1000`: 0 in five runs. The cause that fits is in
  `walIndexReadHdr` (a reader briefly takes the WAL write lock); I read that in
  the source and did not prove it.
  - Under the scheduler this never happens (0 in 10 seeds of 1000 transactions,
  asserted). The same code with the scheduler off got it 70 times in 10000
  transactions (16 under `-race`, 0 under `-asan` on Linux). So production needs
  a busy timeout or retry on the write connection, and the deterministic mode
  can never exercise that path.

  **2. The scheduler** is `sim.Scheduler` in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.c
  laude/worktrees/sim-spike/internal/sim/sim.go`.
  - The brief's claim holds: four goroutines sleeping to the same fake instant
  woke in all 24 orders in 300 runs, and so did four `time.AfterFunc` timers.
  The scheduler therefore keeps its own clock and never uses the bubble's.
  - Locks belong to the scheduler too (`NewSemaphore`; one permit is a lock). A
  channel lock would wake the next holder directly, and two goroutines would
  then run at once.
  - A task's identity travels in its `context.Context`.
  - Half of all yields get a random simulated delay. Without that, a yielding
  task always runs again before time moves, and a timer-driven task never gets
  in between.
  - `sim.Free` has the same methods mapped to plain Go.

  **3. The toy library** is test code in `toy_test.go`, with the workload and
  checks in `simulation_test.go`. It has a compare-and-swap manifest, a SQLite
  cache, a writer lock held for the whole interactive transaction, a separate
  swap lock so renewal can run meanwhile, a renewal task, pooled readers with
  yield points, and a store with latency and three faults (error, applied but
  reported failed, delay longer than the lease).

  **4. Run twice and compare** (`S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=10000`):

  | Workload | Seeds, each run twice | Pairs that differed | Distinct traces |
  Distinct task orders | Invariant violations |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | Default (4 callers that pause) | 10000 | 0 | 10000 | 10000 | 0 |
  | 8 callers that never pause | 2500 | 0 | 2500 | 2500 | 0 |

  The default runs covered 137837 commits, 13305 failed commits, 309377 reads
  (2105 overtaken by a commit while they ran) and 2643 reads refused for an
  expired lease.

  **5. Free-running mode** works with no change to the library code and passes
  under `-race` with no violations.
  - The race detector is blind in scheduled mode: a planted unsynchronised
  counter passed 50 seeds under `-race`. With the scheduler off it was reported
  in the first run.
  - Free mode on the bubble's fake clock usually repeats itself for callers that
  pause (1 to 13 of 200 seeds gave two different traces) and never for callers
  that don't (50 of 50).
  - Free mode is weak at producing real overlap. The toy workload never got
  `SQLITE_BUSY` with the scheduler off in 2000 runs, on the fake clock or the
  real one; only a tight loop did.

  **6. Planted bugs**, over 10000 seeds; the first four seeds that found each
  were rerun five times with identical trace hash and violations.

  | Bug | Seeds that found it |
  |---|---|
  | Renewal swaps the manifest after releasing the swap lock (no data race) |
  1051 |
  | Commit reaches the cache before the manifest, run without faults | 1688 |

  `S4QL_SIM_SEED=<n> go test ./internal/sim/ -run TestSimulationSeed -v` replays
  one seed and prints its trace.

  ## Rules the library must follow, and how each is checked

  | Rule | What happens when broken | Enforcement |
  |---|---|---|
  | Wait only through the runtime (no blocking channel operations, `sync.Cond`,
  `sync.WaitGroup`, `time.Sleep`) | Run fails, naming the task and where it last
  waited | Runtime check, tested |
  | Start goroutines only with the runtime's `Go` | Run fails when the goroutine
  reaches the runtime; one that never does is not noticed | Runtime check by
  goroutine ID, tested; a lint is needed for completeness |
  | No context with a deadline | Run fails | Runtime check, tested; a real
  harness needs `WithTimeout` on the simulated clock |
  | No `time.Now` or other use of the real clock | Silent: the clock stands
  still | Lint only, not built |
  | No `sync.Mutex` held across a wait | Hang until the test timeout; the stack
  dump shows `sync.Mutex.Lock` | Not detectable by the scheduler; confirmed by
  experiment. A mutex wrapper could check it; not built |
  | Nothing that differs between two runs of a seed (map order, real randomness,
  SQLite's clock functions) | Traces differ | Run-twice check; shown to catch
  map iteration order |
  | A sent manifest swap is never abandoned | Stale ETag, lease lost later |
  Invariant in the workload |

  ## Where nondeterminism leaked in

  Nowhere in scheduled mode: no pair ever differed while I built it. The
  scheduler owned the clock, the locks and the randomness from the first run,
  and picks from names in sorted order. To show the check works, I gave it a
  task that ranges over a map and it failed as it should.

  ## Measurements (this machine, 10 cores, Apple silicon)

  | What | Speed |
  |---|---|
  | Toy library, scheduled | 227 runs per second (366 steps per run) |
  | Same, SQLite `synchronous = off` | 256 runs per second |
  | Scheduler alone, tasks that only yield | 253130 steps per second |
  | Toy under `-race` | 97 runs per second |
  | Toy under `-asan`, Linux container | 54 runs per second |
  | Toy before the goroutine-ID check was added | 364 runs per second; scheduler
  alone 1217657 steps per second |

  - The goroutine-ID check costs about 3 microseconds per wait.
  - A CPU profile taken before that check (3000 runs) showed 52% in SQLite
  through cgo (opening and setting up the cache 21%, commits 17%, close 7%,
  reads 6%), 35% in thread wake-ups for goroutine handover, and 9% in system
  calls for temporary directories. I did not re-profile afterwards.

  ## What the design cannot do

  - **It switches tasks only at waits.** A check-then-act race with no wait in
  the window was found by 0 of 1000 seeds; with a yield in the window, by 659 of
  1000. FoundationDB has no such gap because its production code is cooperative
  too.
  - **It hides all data races from the race detector.**
  - **It never has two goroutines in SQLite**, so contention inside SQLite is
  never simulated.
  - **It finds only what the invariants check.** Self-review found a real bug in
  the toy that I had not planted: `Close` cancelled a renewal whose swap had
  already been applied, and the lease release then failed silently. The earlier
  20000 "clean" runs had produced that interleaving and nothing looked. Once I
  added the check, the simulation found it at seed 480.
  - **It runs only under `go test`**, because a bubble needs a `testing.T`.
  There is no standalone soak binary.
  - **A seed is tied to the exact code**; any change to the number of waits or
  random draws changes every trace.
  - **Not shown by the spike:** crash and restart, two instances contending for
  the lease, a real S3 client under simulation.
  - It depends on undocumented behaviour of synctest with cgo, and on the format
  of `runtime.Stack` for the goroutine ID.

  ## Conditions for adopting

  1. All waiting goes through one small runtime interface. A lint or syntax-tree
  test forbids `go` statements, the `time` clock and timers,
  `context.WithTimeout`/`WithDeadline`, `sync.Cond` and `sync.WaitGroup` in
  library packages.
  2. Every simulated seed is run twice in CI, permanently.
  3. The free-running mode runs under `-race` in CI, plus a dedicated stress
  test on the real clock for the paths only parallelism reaches, starting with
  `SQLITE_BUSY` on the write connection.
  4. The checker compares data against a model, and planted bugs stay as tests
  of the checker.
  5. Every lock acquire and release is a scheduling point (it is in the spike),
  and yields are placed wherever shared state is touched between waits.
  6. The tests in `cgo_test.go` and `timers_test.go` stay as canaries for Go and
  SQLite upgrades.
  7. Someone decides how the write connection handles `SQLITE_BUSY`, and whether
  the spec's "local I/O failure stops the instance" counts it.

  ## What I would do differently in the real harness

  - Give the runtime `WithTimeout`, a broadcast for "stopped", and a pool.
  - Identify tasks by creation order, so names can repeat.
  - Vary fault rates and jitter per seed, and try a smarter choice than uniform
  random.
  - Simulate crash and restart, and a second instance.
  - Use structured trace events and keep only a hash unless a run fails.
  - Replace the goroutine-ID check with the lint, or make it optional.
  - Try an in-memory SQLite VFS to cut file I/O; that needs a change to the
  binding and I did not measure it.

  ## Self-review

  Two competing reviewers went over the branch. Both found:
  - the hole in the `go`-statement check;
  - context deadlines silently not working;
  - the unplanted toy bug;
  - the inflated count for the renewal bug (about a fifth of the hits needed no
  race);
  - several tests that proved less than their names said.

  One found that the invariants compared counters and never data. All of these
  are fixed. The numbers above are from after the fixes, and they supersede Step
  4 of the diary.

  Left as they are, with reasons in diary Step 5:
  - `is.*` assertions are called inside tasks, off the test goroutine.
  - Four test files have no matching source file, against the project's Go
  convention. The toy had to be test code.
  - `Run` takes no context.

  ## Files

  All under `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/sim-spike/`:
  - `internal/sim/sim.go`
  - `internal/sim/sim_test.go`
  - `internal/sim/cgo_test.go`
  - `internal/sim/timers_test.go`
  - `internal/sim/toy_test.go`
  - `internal/sim/simulation_test.go`
  - `docs/diary/2026-11-10-simulation-spike.md` (your Step 1 untouched, my Steps
  2 to 5)

⏺ Agent "Spike seeded scheduler on synctest" finished · 1h 15m 35s
⏺ The spike passed: plain blocking Go with real SQLite replays exactly from a
  seed. The builder's verdict is to adopt design B with conditions. I ran its
  tests myself and they pass.

  What it showed

  - synctest and cgo work together. The scheduler's wait holds until a running
    SQLite call returns, and fake time does not move while a goroutine is inside
    C.
  - Replay is exact. 10,000 seeds were each run twice with no differing pair,
    and every seed produced a different interleaving. The same seeds give the
    same traces on macOS, under the race detector, and under the address
    sanitizer on Linux.
  - It finds planted bugs. Two realistic concurrency bugs were each found by
    more than 1,000 of 10,000 seeds, and every rerun of a finding seed
    reproduced it.
  - Speed. The toy library ran at 227 simulated runs per second on this machine.
    About half the time is spent inside SQLite.

  One finding against what we assumed

  A Go lock around the write connection does not keep SQLite from reporting
  "busy". With one writer and eight parallel readers, and nothing else writing,
  begin immediate failed 13 to 95 times per 3,000 transactions. The builder's
  explanation is that a reader briefly takes the WAL write lock; it read that in
  SQLite's source but did not prove it. So what I wrote in docs/todo.md is
  wrong: "busy" on the write connection is not a sign of a bug of ours.
  Production needs a busy timeout or a retry there, and whether "busy" counts as
  a local failure that stops the instance is a decision for you, next.

  What the design cannot do

  - It switches goroutines only at waits. A race with no wait in the window was
    found by 0 of 1,000 seeds; with a yield point added, by 659. Production Go
    can interleave anywhere, so yield points must be placed wherever shared
    state is touched.
  - It hides data races from the race detector, and never has two goroutines
    inside SQLite. The free-running mode covers both, without replay. The "busy"
    case above only ever appeared there.
  - It finds only what the checks look for. The toy had a real, unplanted bug
    that 20,000 clean runs produced and nobody checked for.
  - Not yet shown: crash and restart, two instances competing for the lease, and
    a real S3 client.
  - It depends on undocumented behaviour of synctest with cgo, so the spike's
    tests stay as canaries for Go upgrades.

  The conditions

  All waiting goes through one small interface, enforced by a lint. Every seed
  is run twice in CI. The free-running mode runs under the race detector, with a
  separate stress test for what only real parallelism reaches. The checks
  compare data against a model.

  Do you agree to adopt design B on those conditions?

✻ Churned for 38s · done Tuesday 10:29 PM

❯ Launch a subagent to investigate the BUSY on reads in SQLite.                 
                                                                                
  Give be the whole design B again, with the new findings.                      

⏺ Agent(Investigate SQLITE_BUSY in WAL mode) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ An Opus agent is investigating the "busy" errors: it has to prove the cause
  with evidence, list every other way a lock wait can reach us, and test the
  options.

  Here is design B as it stands after the spike.

  Production

  - Plain blocking Go. Each caller's goroutine does its own work top to bottom.
    There is no state machine.
  - Two locks. A writer lock is held for a whole write transaction, so one runs
    at a time. A separate manifest lock is held only around each swap, so lease
    renewal can run while a write transaction sits open.
  - One background goroutine, which renews the lease.
  - Readers run in parallel on pooled SQLite connections.
  - One small runtime interface for everything that waits: starting a goroutine,
    locks, sleeping and timers, the clock, timeouts, and yielding. In
    production it maps straight onto ordinary Go.
  - S3 and randomness sit behind interfaces, as the spec says.
  - SQLite's "busy" must be handled on the write connection. How is open until
    the investigation reports.

  Simulation, scheduled mode

  - Same library code, inside a testing/synctest test. Test goroutines play the
    application's callers.
  - The scheduler implements the runtime interface. It owns the clock, the locks
    and the randomness. The spike found two reasons it cannot leave these to
    Go: timers due at the same instant fire in random order, and a channel-based
    lock wakes the next holder directly, so two goroutines would run at once.
  - One goroutine runs at a time. It runs until it waits: at an S3 call, a
    sleep, taking or releasing a lock, or an explicit yield. The seed then picks
    who goes next.
  - Random delays. Half of all yields get a random simulated delay, or
    timer-driven work such as renewal never gets in between.
  - Faults. The fake S3 injects latency, errors, operations that succeed but
    report failure, and delays longer than the lease. Buggify points add more.
  - Run twice. Every seed runs twice and the traces are compared.
  - Replay. One command reruns a single seed and prints its trace.

  Simulation, free-running mode

  - The same code with the scheduler off, under the race detector. Real
    parallelism, no replay.
  - A separate stress test on the real clock for what only true parallelism
    reaches. The "busy" error is the first such case: it appeared only here.

  Rules the library code must follow

  ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │           Rule           │     If broken     │       Enforced by        │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Wait only through the    │ The run fails and │                          │
  │ runtime interface        │  names the        │ Runtime check            │
  │                          │ goroutine         │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Start goroutines only    │ The run fails     │ Runtime check, plus a    │
  │ through it               │                   │ lint to build            │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ No real clock, no real   │ The clock         │                          │
  │ timeouts                 │ silently stands   │ Lint, to build           │
  │                          │ still             │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ No ordinary mutex held   │                   │ Not detectable by the    │
  │ across a wait            │ The test hangs    │ scheduler; a wrapper     │
  │                          │                   │ could check it           │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Nothing that differs     │                   │                          │
  │ between two runs, such   │ Traces differ     │ The run-twice check      │
  │ as map order             │                   │                          │
  └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  What it cannot do

  - It switches only at waits. A race with no wait in the window is invisible,
    so yields must be placed wherever shared state is touched.
  - It hides data races from the race detector, and never has two goroutines
    inside SQLite. The free-running mode covers both.
  - It finds only what the checks look for. They must compare data against a
    model, and planted bugs stay as tests of the checks themselves.
  - It runs only under go test, and a seed is tied to the exact code.
  - It relies on undocumented behaviour of synctest with cgo. The spike's tests
    stay as canaries.

  Not yet shown

  Crash and restart, two instances competing for the lease, and a real S3 client
  under simulation.

  Do you agree to adopt it?

✻ Crunched for 1m 7s · done Wednesday 9:03 AM

❯ I still have some issues with it. Can we somehow avoid the free-running mode? 
  I'd really love for everything to be deterministic. Let's discuss.            

⏺ Yes, I think most of it can go. The free-running mode exists to cover three
  gaps, and each can be closed another way.

  1. Data races. In scheduled mode the race detector is blind, because every
  handover between goroutines looks like synchronisation to it. There may be a
  way round that: pass the baton between goroutines through something the
  detector cannot see, such as a call into C. The run stays one goroutine at a
  time and fully replayable, but the detector no longer sees an ordering between
  goroutines except where our code really takes a lock. It would then report
  real data races from a seed. This is my idea and untested, so it needs a small
  spike.

  2. Switching only at waits. Production Go can switch goroutines anywhere. But
  if the code has no data races, the only places where a switch can change the
  outcome are the synchronisation operations: taking a lock, releasing it, an
  atomic operation. That is an established result in concurrency testing, not
  something I made up. So if every such operation goes through our runtime
  interface and is a scheduling point, the scheduled mode covers everything
  production can do. Locks already work this way in the spike; atomics would
  have to as well. This gap closes only together with the first one.

  3. Two goroutines inside SQLite at once. We don't need the real overlap, only
  its effects on our code, and "busy" is the one we know. Those can be injected
  by the seed at the binding, like any other buggify point. The investigation
  running now lists every such effect.

  What remains. One small test with real parallelism, with a different job:
  checking that our assumptions about SQLite under contention are true, for
  example which errors can appear where. That is the same role real S3 has in
  the spec. It checks the model and does not hunt for bugs in our code.

  The alternative. FoundationDB gets full determinism by making production
  cooperative too: one thread, switching only at waits. We could do the same
  with one global lock that every goroutine holds while it runs our code. Then
  production cannot do anything the simulation doesn't. The cost is that our SQL
  engine, which is Go code, would run one query at a time, and only the time
  inside SQLite and S3 would be parallel. That weakens the spec's "reads run in
  parallel".

  I'd close the three gaps and keep parallel reads. Which direction appeals to
  you?

✻ Crunched for 1m 8s · done Wednesday 9:15 AM

❯ I'd rather keep the free-running mode for now, and we can revisit later.      

⏺ Kept: design B stays with both modes. I'll note the three ways to close the
  gaps in docs/todo.md as something to revisit, so the idea isn't lost.

  You said you still had some issues with it. What is the next one?

     /context all to expand

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Root cause (proven)

  When a reader starts a read transaction while the writer is committing, it can
  read the two copies of the WAL-index header while they differ. It then takes
  WAL_WRITE_LOCK for a moment to read the header again. If the writer's next
  `begin immediate` arrives in that window, the writer finds WAL_WRITE_LOCK held
  by a sibling connection and gets SQLITE_BUSY at once.

  The path in `internal/sqlite/c/sqlite3.c`:
  - The writer writes the header at commit: `walIndexWriteHdr`, line 67846; it
  copies `aHdr[1]`, then a barrier, then `aHdr[0]` (67855-67857).
  - The reader sees the mismatch in `walIndexTryHdr`: `return 1; /* Dirty read
  */` (69574).
  - `walIndexReadHdr`, 69656-69668: "So get a WRITE lock and try again." It
  calls `walLockExclusive(pWal, WAL_WRITE_LOCK, 1)`.
  - The writer fails at 70679-70683 ("Only one writer allowed at a time...
  Return SQLITE_BUSY if unable"). `unixShmLock` returns BUSY right away when a
  sibling holds the lock (46092-46098).

  **Evidence (verified):** I instrumented a scratch copy of the amalgamation.
  Under the shm mutex it records who holds lock 0, the call site, and why that
  thread's header read failed. In three macOS runs the writer failed 38, 52 and
  70 times. Each time, the log shows a failure at 70682 with a reader holding
  the lock at 69668 after a dirty read, in exactly the same numbers. Linux
  matched too (192 = 192). For the counterfactual I patched readers to retry
  instead of taking the lock: 0 writer failures in 3 runs on macOS and 1 on
  Linux.

  ## Answers

  1. **Reproduce (verified).** The failing call is `begin immediate`, extended
  code exactly 5, with the message "database is locked". Through the Go binding
  I measured 32, 34 and 32 per 3000 transactions (temporary test, now deleted).
  In the C harness it was 36–125 on macOS and 182–389 in a Linux container.
  Readers never got any error in steady state, over about 100 runs of 100k–3M
  reads each, including FULL, RESTART and TRUNCATE checkpoints.
  2. **Docs.** https://sqlite.org/wal.html's section "Sometimes Queries Return
  SQLITE_BUSY In WAL Mode" lists exclusive locking mode, the last connection's
  cleanup, and recovery. It does not mention this race.
  3. **Conditions (verified, 5 runs each).** None of these removes it:
     - turning off autocheckpoint, or manual checkpoints of any mode
     - readers reopening a connection per read, or reading without an explicit
  transaction
     - `synchronous` set to normal or off
     - `begin exclusive`; a deferred `begin` only moves the failure to the first
  write statement
     - the platform: it happens on macOS and Linux, more often on Linux
     - the number of readers: even 1 reader got 85 and 532 failures per 30000 in
  two of five runs

     The smallest fix: `busy_timeout = 1` on the writer, with 0 failures on both
  platforms.
  4. **Other ways BUSY reaches us (verified unless noted):**
     - **Recovery:** the first statement on other connections gets **261
  BUSY_RECOVERY**, readers and writer alike, in the same process. The docs say
  "different process". Measured: a 100 MB WAL took about 20 ms to recover with a
  warm page cache.
     - **Last connection closing:** it holds an EXCLUSIVE lock on the database
  file (`sqlite3WalClose`, 69481-69483), so a new connection's first statement
  gets **5**.
     - **517 BUSY_SNAPSHOT:** returned at once even with a busy timeout.
  Repeating the statement in the same transaction fails again; it is cleared
  only by rollback and restart. It cannot happen with one writer using `begin
  immediate` (inferred).
     - **FULL, RESTART and TRUNCATE checkpoints** return 5 while readers are
  active, even with a 1000 ms timeout.
     - **SQLITE_PROTOCOL (15):** returned after 100 internal retries, under 10 s
  in total (70006-70010). Never seen in testing.
     - **BUSY_TIMEOUT:** cannot occur in our build.
  5. **The busy handler** covers 5 and 261 only at transaction start
  (`btreeBeginTrans`, 76497).

  ## Options

  | Option | Result | Cost | Binding change |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `busy_timeout` on the writer | 0 failures | about 1.5 ms on under 1% of
  begins; worst case is the timeout (or recovery time) | none, the pragma works
  today |
  | `busy_timeout` on readers | 0 errors with reopen churn | none measurable |
  none |
  | Retry loop in Go, or a spinning busy handler | spin=10 gave 0 on macOS but
  4–26 on Linux | | C handler in the preamble |
  | RWMutex gate (readers around the step that starts the read; writer around
  `begin` only) | 0 failures | microseconds; read bodies stay parallel | engine
  must know which step starts the read |
  | `unix-excl` VFS | **does not remove the race** (72–211 failures, same
  mechanism) | | VFS argument to `Open` |

  The gate also relies on SQLite internals and does not cover recovery or the
  last-close case.

  On `unix-excl`, your recollection is right except that it does not fix this.
  Verified on macOS and Linux:
  - It creates no `-shm` file; the WAL index lives in heap memory. That would
  fix the memory-mapped `-shm` problem in `docs/todo.md`; that it removes the
  signal is inferred from the source.
  - Several connections in one process work.
  - Every other process gets "database is locked (5)" for as long as any
  connection is open, including the vendored `sqlite3` shell and with `.timeout
  500`.

  ## Classification (facts; full table in notes.md)

  - **Local I/O or environment failure, stop the instance:** IOERR, FULL,
  CANTOPEN, CORRUPT, NOTADB, NOLFS, PERM, and READONLY on a cache opened
  read-write (READONLY_DBMOVED means the file was unlinked or renamed).
  - **Transient, retry is right:** BUSY at transaction start, BUSY_RECOVERY,
  SCHEMA. BUSY_SNAPSHOT is retryable only by restarting the transaction.
  - **Programming error:** MISUSE, RANGE, LOCKED, NOTFOUND, AUTH, INTERNAL.
  - **The caller's SQL or data:** ERROR, CONSTRAINT, MISMATCH, TOOBIG, ABORT.
  - **Not returned or unused:** NOTICE, WARNING, EMPTY, FORMAT.
  - **Judgement calls:** NOMEM and PROTOCOL.

  ## Recommendation

  - Set `busy_timeout` on every connection: about 1000 ms on the writer and on
  readers.
  - Treat 5 or 261 after the timeout as transient and return it to the caller.
  Log it loudly, because in this design it means a foreign process or a bug.
  - Open the write connection and run one statement before opening the readers,
  and keep it open for the instance's lifetime. Recovery and last-close cleanup
  then never race the pool.
  - Treat PROTOCOL and NOMEM as stops, for simplicity.
  - Drop the assumption that the Go lock means BUSY is a bug: it does not hold
  at timeout 0.
  - Consider `unix-excl` separately, for the `-shm` and single-process benefits,
  if locking out the shell is acceptable.

  What would change my mind:
  - A latency target where an extra 1–5 ms on a begin matters would favour the
  gate, with the timeout kept as a backstop.
  - A need to inspect the live cache from another process rules out `unix-excl`.

  The worktree's `git status` is clean. Scratch builds, the harness and the
  instrumentation are in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4q
  l/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/busy/`, and the fuller notes
  are in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca
  -4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/busy/notes.md`.

⏺ The investigation proved the cause and tested the options.

  The cause. When a reader starts while the writer is committing, it can catch
  SQLite's WAL index header half-updated. It then takes the WAL write lock for a
  moment to read the header again. If the writer's next begin immediate lands
  in that window, it gets "busy" at once. The agent proved this with an
  instrumented copy of SQLite: every writer failure matched a reader holding the
  lock after such a read, on macOS and Linux. SQLite's documentation does not
  mention this case.

  What does not help. Checkpoint settings, synchronous, how readers open
  connections, or fewer readers: even one reader triggers it.

  What I'm choosing, as implementation detail:

  - A busy timeout on every connection, about one second. SQLite then waits out
    the window by itself. With a timeout of one millisecond the failures already
    dropped to zero on both platforms, and the wait is about 1.5 ms on fewer
    than 1% of begins.
  - The write connection opens first and stays open for the instance's lifetime.
    That keeps two other "busy" cases away from the reader pool: WAL recovery
    after a crash, and cleanup when the last connection closes.
  - "Busy" after the timeout is returned to the caller as an error, not treated
    as a stop, and logged loudly, because in our design it then means another
    process or a bug of ours.
  - The spec's "local I/O failure" gets a concrete list of SQLite codes: I/O
    error, disk full, cannot open, corrupt, not a database, permission denied,
    and read-only on a cache we opened for writing. Out of memory and SQLite's
    protocol error also stop the instance, for simplicity.

  The scheduled simulation is unaffected: it runs one goroutine at a time, so
  the window never opens there.

  One decision for you: SQLite's unix-excl mode.

  It does not fix "busy", but the agent confirmed it does two other things:

  - It keeps the WAL index in ordinary memory, with no -shm file. That would
    close the gap we recorded yesterday, where a disk error on that
    memory-mapped file kills the process instead of returning an error. That
    last step is inferred from the source, not yet tested.
  - It locks every other process out of the cache file while we have it open.
    That fits one instance per cache, but it includes the sqlite3 shell, so
    nobody can inspect a live cache from outside.

  I recommend using it: it closes a hole in the stop story and enforces
  something the design assumes anyway. The price is debugging through our own
  tooling or on a copy of the file.

  Do you want unix-excl?

✻ Brewed for 22s · done Wednesday 9:25 AM

⏺ Agent "Investigate SQLITE_BUSY in WAL mode" finished · 21m 41s

❯ I don't fully understand the consequences of unix-excl mode.                  

⏺ In short: it changes who may open the cache file and where SQLite keeps one
  internal structure. It does not change the file format, so it can be switched
  on or off at any time.

  What changes

  - Only one process can open the cache. While our process has it open, every
    other process gets "database is locked". That covers the sqlite3 shell, any
    backup or inspection tool, and a second copy of the application pointed at
    the same cache path.
  - The WAL index lives in our process's memory. Normally SQLite keeps it in a
    -shm file that all connections memory-map, so that several processes can
    share it. With one process there is nothing to share, and no such file is
    created.

  What that means in practice

  - Debugging. You cannot look into a live cache from outside. You would stop
    the instance, or copy the files and open the copy. S3 holds the truth, so I
    expect this to be rare.
  - Restarts on one machine. A new process cannot open the cache until the old
    one has closed it. It could not serve before then anyway, because the old
    one holds the lease.
  - The memory-mapping gap closes. There is no mapped file left for a disk error
    to hit, so such an error can no longer kill the process. The agent inferred
    this from the source and did not test it.
  - Connections inside our process are unaffected. The agent tested the writer
    with parallel readers, and it works as before.

  What I'm less sure of

  - After a crash, the operating system should release the lock when the process
    dies, so nothing stale is left behind. That is my understanding and was not
    tested.
  - It is a less-travelled path. It ships with SQLite, but far fewer deployments
    use it than the default, so it has had less exposure.
  - It exists on macOS and Linux only. Windows would need another answer, if
    Windows ever matters.

  Because it is reversible and nothing we build next depends on it, there is no
  need to decide today. I can leave it in docs/todo.md as an option for when the
  local-failure handling is built, with a test of the two unverified points
  first.

  Do you want to decide now, or park it there?

         +it reaches us, the options including the `unix-excl` VFS, and a
         + classification of SQLite's result codes.

      29    The invariant checked is that no read that starts after an ac
          knowledged commit returns a state without that commit. The simu

      30  - **No fault-injecting layer under SQLite, for now.** SQLite te
          sts its own I/O error handling, and using the official C code i
          s how we inherit that.
      31 +- **Which SQLite errors count as a local I/O failure.** Chosen 
         +by maragubot on 2026-11-11 and told to Markus: I/O error, disk 
         +full, cannot open, corrupt, not a database, no large-file suppo
         +rt, permission denied, and read-only on a cache that was opened
         + for writing. Out of memory and SQLite's protocol error also st
         +op the instance, for simplicity. "Busy" does not: see the next 
         +item. The full classification is in [research/sqlite-busy.md](r
         +esearch/sqlite-busy.md).                                       
      32  - **Open: the wal-index is memory-mapped.** The build sets `SQL
          ITE_MAX_MMAP_SIZE=0`, so SQLite never memory-maps the database 
          file, and an I/O error there arrives as an error code. In WAL m
          ode SQLite still memory-maps its shared index, the `-shm` file:
           "The wal-index is implemented using an ordinary file that is m
          mapped" (https://sqlite.org/wal.html). An I/O failure on a memo
          ry-mapped file arrives as a signal that kills the process, not 
          as an error that SQLite can handle (https://sqlite.org/mmap.htm
          l). How to handle this is not designed.

         +us on 2026-11-11: "park it in TODO.md for now as a potential th
         +ing". It keeps the wal-index in ordinary memory and creates no 
         +`-shm` file, which would close the gap above. It also locks eve
         +ry other process out of the cache file while our process has it
         + open, including the `sqlite3` shell, so a live cache cannot be
         + inspected from outside. Several connections in one process sti
         +ll work. It does not change the file format, so it can be switc
         +hed on or off at any time, and `sqlite.Open` would need a VFS n
         +ame. To find out before deciding: what happens after a crash (w
         +hether the operating system releases the lock, and that the nex
         +t start recovers from the WAL), and a test that no signal arriv
         +es on a disk error. It exists on macOS and Linux only. Details 
         +are in [research/sqlite-busy.md](research/sqlite-busy.md).

      45 -- **No state machine.** The library is plain blocking Go. Each 
         -caller's goroutine does its own work from top to bottom, a lock
         - lets one write transaction through at a time, and one backgrou
         -nd goroutine renews the lease. That goroutine is the only concu
         -rrency the library creates; the rest comes from the callers. Th
         -e spec used to ask for a state machine that does no I/O, and th
         -at sentence was deleted. The reasons: a state machine does not 
         -solve the hard part, which is the order goroutines run in; it f
         -its interactive transactions poorly; and FoundationDB, whose te
         -sting this project follows, runs plain coroutine code on a dete
         -rministic scheduler and has no such state machine.             
      46 -- **Locks are taken in Go before calling into SQLite,** so that
         - SQLite never waits on a lock itself. SQLite's busy timeout sta
         -ys off, so it returns "busy" at once and never sleeps. If the G
         -o locks are right, that error never appears, and seeing it mean
         -s a bug of ours.                                               
      45 +- **No state machine.** The library is plain blocking Go. Each 
         +caller's goroutine does its own work from top to bottom, and on
         +e background goroutine renews the lease. That goroutine is the 
         +only concurrency the library creates; the rest comes from the c
         +allers. The spec used to ask for a state machine that does no I
         +/O, and that sentence was deleted. The reasons: a state machine
         + does not solve the hard part, which is the order goroutines ru
         +n in; it fits interactive transactions poorly; and FoundationDB
         +, whose testing this project follows, runs plain coroutine code
         + on a deterministic scheduler and has no such state machine.   
      46 +- **Two locks.** A writer lock is held for a whole write transa
         +ction, so one runs at a time. A separate manifest lock is held 
         +only around each swap, so lease renewal can run while a write t
         +ransaction sits open.                                          
      47 +- **One small runtime interface for everything that waits:** st
         +arting a goroutine, locks, sleeping and timers, the clock, time
         +outs, and yielding. In production it maps onto ordinary Go. In 
         +simulation the scheduler implements it.                        
      48  - **The stop is synchronised with the readers.** After a local 
          I/O failure (see [Local I/O failure handling](#local-io-failure
          -handling)), no read can start between the failure and `Commit`
           returning. The simulation tests this with readers racing the s

      50 +The simulation, as shown to work by a spike on 2026-11-10 (in `
         +internal/sim` once merged; the diary is `docs/diary/2026-11-10-

      51  
      51 -- The real library runs unchanged inside a `testing/synctest` t
         -est. S3 is a simulated object store, time is fake, and randomne
         -ss comes from the seed. The test starts goroutines that play ap
         -plication callers.                                             
      52 -- Wherever a goroutine meets the outside world, it parks: at an
         - S3 call, at a wait on time, and at marked points in a read. A 
         -scheduler waits until every goroutine is parked, picks one by t
         -he seed, lets it run to its next parking point, and repeats. So
         - one goroutine runs at a time, and the seed decides the order. 
         -This is FoundationDB's model rebuilt in Go.                    
      53 -- Every seed is run twice and the two runs are compared, so non
         -determinism that leaks in fails the test.                      
      54 -- A second mode runs the same tests with the scheduler off unde
         -r the race detector. It finds data races, but a failure there c
         -annot be replayed from the seed.                               
      55 -- Known costs: all waiting on time goes through our clock inter
         -face, a lock held across a parking point cannot be a `sync.Mute
         -x` (waiting on one does not count as blocked for `testing/synct
         -est`), and parking points need stable names. The design cannot 
         -replay two SQLite calls that run at the same instant.          
      52 +- **Scheduled mode.** The real library runs unchanged inside a 
         +`testing/synctest` test, with test goroutines playing applicati
         +on callers. One goroutine runs at a time. It runs until it wait
         +s (at an S3 call, a sleep, taking or releasing a lock, or an ex
         +plicit yield), and the seed then picks which goroutine goes nex
         +t. This is FoundationDB's model rebuilt in Go.                 
      53 +- **The scheduler owns the clock, the locks, and the randomness
         +.** It cannot leave them to Go: timers due at the same instant 
         +fire in random order under `testing/synctest`, and a channel-ba
         +sed lock wakes the next holder directly, so two goroutines woul
         +d run at once. Half of all yields get a random simulated delay,
         + or timer-driven work such as renewal never gets in between.   
      54 +- **Every seed is run twice and the traces are compared,** so n
         +ondeterminism that leaks in fails the test. The spike ran 10000
         + seeds twice with no differing pair, and each seed gave a diffe
         +rent interleaving.                                             
      55 +- **Free-running mode.** The same code runs with the scheduler 
         +off under the race detector, with real parallelism and no repla
         +y. A separate stress test on the real clock covers what only tr
         +ue parallelism reaches.                                        
      56 +- **`testing/synctest` works with cgo.** A goroutine inside a S
         +QLite call counts as running, so the scheduler waits for the ca
         +ll to return, and fake time does not move meanwhile. This is no
         +t documented, so the spike's tests of it stay as canaries for G

      58 +Conditions for keeping the design, from the spike:             
      59

         +l clock and timers, `context.WithTimeout` and `context.WithDead
         +line`, `sync.Cond`, and `sync.WaitGroup`. An ordinary mutex mus
         +t not be held across a wait; the scheduler cannot detect that,

      61 +- Every seed is run twice in CI, permanently.                  
      62 +- The free-running mode runs under the race detector in CI.    
      63 +- The checks compare data against a model, and planted bugs sta
         +y as tests of the checks. The spike's toy library had a real bu
         +g that 20000 clean runs produced and no check looked for.      
      64 +- Every lock acquire and release is a scheduling point, and yie
         +lds are placed wherever shared state is touched between waits. 
      65 +                                                               
      66 +What the scheduled mode cannot do:                             
      67 +                                                               
      68 +- It switches goroutines only at waits. In the spike, a race wi
         +th no wait in the window was found by 0 of 1000 seeds, and by 6
         +59 once a yield was added.                                     
      69 +- It hides data races from the race detector, and it never has 
         +two goroutines inside SQLite.                                  
      70 +- It finds only what the checks look for.                      
      71 +- It runs only under `go test`, and a seed is tied to the exact
         + code.                                                         
      72 +- Not yet shown: crash and restart, two instances competing for
         + the lease, and a real S3 client under simulation.             
      73 +                                                               
      74 +SQLite's "busy", chosen by maragubot on 2026-11-11 after an inv
         +estigation and told to Markus ([research/sqlite-busy.md](resear
         +ch/sqlite-busy.md)):                                           
      75 +                                                               
      76 +- **A lock in Go does not keep SQLite from reporting "busy".** 
         +A reader that starts while the writer commits can take the WAL 
         +write lock for a moment, and the writer's next `begin immediate
         +` then fails at once. It happens with a single reader. It never
         + happens in scheduled mode, only with real parallelism.        
      77 +- **Every connection gets a busy timeout of about one second.**
         + SQLite then waits out the window itself. In the investigation 
         +a timeout of one millisecond already removed every failure, and
         + the wait was about 1.5 ms on fewer than 1% of begins.         
      78 +- **The write connection opens first, runs one statement, and s
         +tays open for the instance's lifetime.** Recovery of the WAL af
         +ter a crash and the cleanup when the last connection closes als
         +o report "busy", and this keeps both away from the reader pool.
      79 +- **"Busy" after the timeout is returned to the caller as an er
         +ror and logged loudly.** It is not a local I/O failure and does
         + not stop the instance. In this design it then means another pr
         +ocess has the cache open, or a bug of ours.                    
      80 +                                                               
      81 +To revisit later. Markus on 2026-11-11, on whether the free-run
         +ning mode can go: "I'd rather keep the free-running mode for no
         +w, and we can revisit later." The ideas:                       
      82 +                                                               
      83 +- Pass the baton between goroutines through something the race 
         +detector cannot see, such as a call into C, so that the detecto
         +r finds data races in scheduled mode. Untested.                
      84 +- For code without data races, only synchronisation operations 
         +are places where a switch can change the outcome. If every lock
         + and atomic operation is a scheduling point, the scheduled mode
         + then covers what production can do.                           
      85 +- Inject SQLite's contention effects, such as "busy", from the 
         +seed at the binding, and keep one small test with real parallel
         +ism whose only job is to check our assumptions about SQLite, as
         + real S3 does for the simulated store.                         
      86 +- The alternative is FoundationDB's own way: one global lock, s
         +o that production also runs one goroutine at a time. It would m
         +ake the SQL engine run one query at a time and weaken "reads ru
         +n in parallel".

  Searched for 2 patterns, read 3 files, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Lease, question 1: how does another instance decide that the lease has timed 
  out?

  Writes are safe whatever the clocks do, because the manifest swap is a
  compare-and-swap. Clocks decide only one thing: whether a holder can serve a
  stale read from its cache after another instance took over.

  - A: an absolute expiry time in the manifest. The other instance compares it
    with its own wall clock. This is only safe if the wall clocks of different
    machines agree. Litestream does this, with no margin.
  - B: a duration and a counter in the manifest. Every renewal increments the
    counter. The other instance takes over only after it has watched the counter
    stand still for the lease duration plus a margin, on its own monotonic
    clock. This needs only that two clocks tick at nearly the same rate, and no
    wall clock is involved.

  I recommend B. Its cost is that takeover after a crash always waits a full
  lease period (30 s plus the margin) from the other instance's first look, even
  if the holder died an hour ago. With A the wait is 10 to 30 s after a fresh
  crash and nothing after an old one. A clean shutdown releases the lease, so
  neither waits then.

  Go with B?

  The first full CI run of the spike on main is still in progress; I'll check it
  when it finishes.

✻ Cooked for 48s · done Wednesday 11:30 AM

❯ A is fine for now.                                                            

⏺ A it is: the manifest records an absolute expiry time, and another instance
  compares it with its own wall clock.

  Lease, question 2: how does the holder check the lease before it serves a 
  read?

  My proposal:

  - The check comes after the read's snapshot is fixed, not before. If the
    process is paused between the two, a check-first read could take its
    snapshot after a takeover. Checking second means the snapshot was taken
    while the lease still held.
  - Both clocks must agree that the lease holds. The wall clock must be before
    the expiry written in the manifest, and the monotonic clock must show less
    than the lease duration since that renewal was sent. The wall clock catches
    a suspended machine, where the monotonic clock can stand still. The
    monotonic clock catches a wall clock that was set backward.
  - The expiry counts from when the renewal was sent, not from when S3 answered,
    so a slow S3 call can only shorten the lease as the holder sees it.

  The check is one atomic load and two clock reads, with no S3 contact.

  What remains with A: if two machines' wall clocks disagree, a holder can serve
  reads that miss another instance's commits, for as long as the clocks
  disagree and at most one lease period. Writes are never affected.

  Agree?

✻ Brewed for 23s · done Wednesday 11:42 AM

❯ I don't understand this. What's the scenario?                                 

⏺ The scenario is two instances on the same database, where the old holder does
  not know it has lost the lease.

  1. 12:00:00: instance X renews the lease. The manifest says it expires at
     12:00:30.
  2. X loses its connection to S3. Its application and its disk still work, but
     renewals fail.
  3. 12:00:30: the lease expires. Instance Y takes it and commits balance = 50.
  4. 12:00:40: the application on X runs select balance. X's cache still
     says 100.

  Reads come from the cache without contacting S3, so X cannot ask S3 whether it
  still holds the lease. Its only way to know is its own clock: it is past
  12:00:30, so X must refuse the read instead of answering 100.

  That clock check is what question 2 is about. My three points are refinements
  for cases where the simple check gives the wrong answer: X's process is paused
  at a bad moment, X's clock stands still or jumps, or S3 answers slowly.

  Does the basic scenario make sense? Then I'll take the three refinements one
  at a time.

✻ Baked for 11s · done Wednesday 11:52 AM

❯ Ah, yes, I understand the scenario                                            

⏺ Working the first refinement through as a scenario, I found that it doesn't
  hold, so I withdraw it. X's cache never contains Y's commits, so a snapshot
  taken after a pause shows the same state as one taken before it, and that
  state was correct at the moment of the check. One check when the read starts
  is enough, and its order relative to the snapshot doesn't matter.

  Refinement 2: which clock does X use for the check?

  Either clock alone can be fooled:

  - Monotonic clock only. X's machine is suspended at 12:00:10 for five minutes,
    for example a laptop that sleeps or a virtual machine that is paused. On
    some systems the monotonic clock stands still during a suspend. After the
    resume X believes that ten seconds have passed, and serves reads while Y
    holds the lease.
  - Wall clock only. The wall clock on X is set back by a minute, by a person or
    by a time-sync correction. X then believes its lease lasts a minute longer
    than it does.

  So I'd require both: a read passes only if the wall clock is before the expiry
  and the monotonic clock shows less than 30 s since the renewal was sent. Each
  clock covers the other's failure, and the cost is two clock reads per read
  transaction.

  Agree?

✻ Sautéed for 41s · done Wednesday 11:58 AM

❯ Both. BUT: If something is suspended with this running, I'll accept stale     
  reads as a consequence. I don't want to design around theoretical failures.   
  This is designed mostly for always-on server-side usage, keep that in mind.   
  But I know things can get weird on servers. But if the server is in such bad  
  a state that both clocks fail, stale reads are probably okay too. All bets    
  are off then?                                                                 

⏺ Yes. When both clocks fail, reads can be stale and we accept that. Only reads
  are affected: writes stay correct whatever the clocks do, because the manifest
  swap is checked by S3. I'll keep the always-on server in mind and not bring
  you suspend-style cases again.

  The third refinement is a detail, so I've decided it: the 30 s count from when
  X sent the renewal, not from when S3 answered.

  Lease, question 3: X's lease runs out because X cannot reach S3. What does X 
  do?

  No other instance has necessarily taken over. With one instance, which is the
  common case, this is just an S3 outage of more than ten seconds.

  - A: stop for good, as for a local I/O failure. The application reopens, which
    also fails until S3 is back.
  - B: refuse and keep trying. Reads and writes fail at once with a distinct
    "lease expired" error, and X keeps trying to renew. If a renewal succeeds,
    the manifest was unchanged, so nobody else wrote and the cache is still
    current. X then serves again.

  I recommend B. With A, every S3 outage longer than ten seconds kills every
  handle, and the application needs a reopen loop to get back. What X does if it
  finds that another instance did take the lease is the next question.

  B?

✻ Sautéed for 17s · done Wednesday 12:02 PM

❯ B, definitely. That's likely a temporary state. Comparing to a client-server  
  case, this is where we'd return a HTTP 502 and let the clients retry.         

⏺ B is recorded: a temporary state with a distinct error, and the caller
  retries.

  Lease, question 4: X finds that another instance has taken the lease. What 
  does X do?

  X notices when it can reach S3 again and its renewal is rejected, because the
  manifest now names Y as holder.

  - A: stop for good. The application reopens, and the normal start waits for
    the lease and catches up the cache.
  - B: refuse and keep trying, as in question 3. X returns the same kind of
    temporary error while Y holds the lease. When Y releases the lease or it
    times out, X takes it, applies the change files that Y wrote to its cache,
    and serves again.

  I recommend B. It makes questions 3 and 4 one rule: an instance without a
  valid lease refuses and keeps trying to get it, and catches up before it
  serves. The catch-up is the same code as at startup. A permanent stop is then
  reserved for local I/O failures. It also gives failover for free: a second
  instance that is refused takes over by itself when the first one dies.

  A write transaction that is open on X when it loses the lease fails, and is
  not committed.

  B?

✻ Cooked for 17s · done Wednesday 12:04 PM

❯ B. It's also nice for failover: we can have a spare instance running, and     
  it'll never serve anything until it gets the lease. That's just a normal      
  state of operation.                                                           

⏺ Agreed, and that makes a spare instance a normal state, not an error state.

  Lease, question 5: does opening a database wait for the lease?

  The spec says another instance "waits or fails" until the lease is free. With
  your spare instance, neither fits well: a spare must open successfully and
  then stay refused for as long as the holder lives.

  - A: opening waits until the instance holds the lease and has caught up. A
    spare then hangs in the open call, perhaps for days.
  - B: opening returns without the lease. It fails only on local problems, such
    as a cache that cannot be opened. The instance then tries for the lease in
    the background, and calls get the temporary error until it serves. A
    separate call waits, with a context, until the instance serves, for
    applications that want to block at startup. This is how database/sql splits
    Open and Ping.

  I recommend B. Its cost is that wrong S3 settings, such as a bad bucket name,
  do not fail the open call. They show up in the waiting call and in the error
  that every query returns.

  B?

✻ Brewed for 15s · done Wednesday 12:05 PM
❯ Isn't that kind of the same as 4? At least the solution is. So B.             

⏺ Yes, it is the same rule applied at startup: an instance without a valid lease
  refuses and keeps trying. So B.

  Lease, question 6: does a write transaction have a maximum duration?

  The scenario: an application begins a write transaction and never finishes it,
  for example because of a bug. Only one write transaction runs at a time, so
  every other writer on that instance waits. The lease is still renewed, because
  renewal does not need the writer lock.

  - A: no maximum. A waiting writer gives up when its own context expires.
    SQLite behaves the same way.
  - B: a maximum, after which the instance rolls the transaction back and its
    calls fail.

  I recommend A for now. A stuck transaction is an application bug that the
  application can bound with a context on the transaction, and B can be added
  later without changing anything else.

  A?

⏺ A, no maximum. That settles the lease questions, and they are recorded in
  docs/todo.md and pushed. CI on main is green, including the first full run of
  the spike.

  Two details I chose myself: the lease duration counts from when the renewal
  was sent, and another instance waits a small margin past the expiry before it
  takes the lease.

  Spec wording for the lease. The spec's bullet "The holder keeps the lease"
  ends with "and it waits or fails until then", which no longer fits. I propose
  to cut that clause and add four bullets:

  ▎ The holder keeps the lease. While the holder keeps renewing the lease, no 
  ▎ other instance can take it. Another instance gets the lease only after the 
  ▎ holder has released it or it has timed out.
  ▎
  ▎ The lease expires at a time on the wall clock. The manifest records when the
  ▎ lease expires, and another instance takes the lease once its own wall clock
  ▎ has passed that time. The wall clocks of the instances must therefore 
  ▎ agree.
  ▎
  ▎ Clocks affect only reads. Before a read, the holder checks the lease against
  ▎ its wall clock and its monotonic clock, and both must show that the lease 
  ▎ holds. If the clocks are wrong, a read can miss the commits of a newer 
  ▎ holder. Writes are never affected, because S3 checks the manifest swap.
  ▎
  ▎ An instance without a valid lease refuses and keeps trying. Reads and writes
  ▎ fail at once with a temporary error, and an open write transaction is not 
  ▎ committed. The instance keeps trying to get the lease, catches up its cache 
  ▎ when it gets it, and then serves again. This holds at startup, when S3 
  ▎ cannot be reached, and while another instance holds the lease, so a spare 
  ▎ instance is a normal state.
  ▎
  ▎ Opening does not wait for the lease. Opening a database fails only on local 
  ▎ problems. A separate call waits until the instance serves.

  Shall I write these into the spec?

⏺ The lease rules are in the spec and pushed.

  Next topic: a manifest swap whose outcome is unknown.

  The scenario:

  1. The application on X calls Commit. X uploads the change file and sends the
     manifest swap.
  2. The connection drops before the answer arrives.
  3. X does not know whether S3 applied the swap. The transaction is either
     committed or not, and X cannot tell which.

  X can find out by asking S3 again, and my proposal for that is:

  - X retries the same swap. If the first attempt never arrived, the retry
    succeeds and the transaction is committed. If the first attempt did arrive,
    S3 rejects the retry, because the manifest has changed.
  - After a rejection, X reads the manifest. The transaction is committed if and
    only if its change file is in the log that the manifest points to. Change
    files are named by their content, so the check is exact.
  - Commit returns only when X knows. It returns nil if the transaction is
    committed and an error if it is not, as in every other case.

  A request that is stuck in the network cannot commit the transaction later
  behind X's back: once the manifest has changed, S3 rejects the old request.

  This leaves one case open: S3 stays unreachable, so X cannot find out. That is
  the next question.

  Agree with this part?

✻ Worked for 42s · done Wednesday 12:18 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Unknown outcome, question 2: S3 stays unreachable, so X cannot find out. What 
  does Commit do?

  - A: Commit waits without limit until X knows.
  - B: Commit waits until the caller's context ends, and then returns a distinct
    "outcome unknown" error. X keeps trying in the background. When S3 is back,
    X reads the manifest and catches up its cache, which applies the
    transaction if it turned out to be committed.

  I recommend B, because a caller must be able to give up. The error is
  distinct, so an application can tell "not committed" from "unknown". For
  "unknown" it must check its own data or make its transactions safe to repeat.
  FoundationDB does the same with its commit_unknown_result error.

  This is the one case where Commit returns an error for a transaction that may
  be committed. It cannot be avoided: when S3 does not answer, nobody knows.

  B?

✻ Cooked for 13s · done Wednesday 12:20 PM

❯ B

⏺ B is recorded in docs/todo.md and pushed.

  One detail I added goes beyond what I told you: after Commit has returned
  "outcome unknown", X serves nothing until it knows. Otherwise the application
  could check its data on X's cache, which lacks the transaction, conclude "not
  committed", and repeat it, while the first one turns out to be committed. With
  this rule the application's next successful read shows the truth.

  Spec wording. I propose one new bullet after "A commit is acknowledged once S3
  has it":

  ▎ An unknown commit outcome is resolved from S3. If the manifest swap ends 
  ▎ without an answer, the instance asks S3 until it knows. The transaction is 
  ▎ committed if and only if its change file is in the log. Commit returns an 
  ▎ "outcome unknown" error only if the caller gives up first, and the instance 
  ▎ then serves nothing until it knows.

  Shall I write it into the spec?

      53  - **Our own SQLite binding.** The cache and the oracle use the 
          official SQLite C code, at a version we pin, through a small cg
          o binding of our own. The module therefore needs cgo.
      54  - **The cache is never ahead of S3.** A commit swaps the manife
          st first and commits to the cache second. The cache records the
           name of the last change file it has applied, in the same local
           transaction as the rows. An instance whose cache is behind the
           manifest applies the missing change files before it serves any
          thing. A cache whose recorded position is not in the log is ref
          used loudly, not rebuilt silently.
      55  - **A commit is acknowledged once S3 has it.** `Commit` returns
           success if the manifest swap succeeded. Before it returns, the
           instance commits the transaction to the local cache, or stops 
          if that fails. A read that starts after a commit is acknowledge
          d therefore always sees it.
      56 +- **An unknown commit outcome is resolved from S3.** If the man
         +ifest swap ends without an answer, the instance asks S3 until i
         +t knows. The transaction is committed if and only if its change
         + file is in the log. `Commit` returns an "outcome unknown" erro
         +r only if the caller gives up first, and the instance then serv
         +es nothing until it knows.                                     
      57  - **A local I/O failure stops the instance.** If reading or wri
          ting the local cache fails, the instance stops serving for good
          . Running reads and waiting writes fail, every later call retur
          ns an error that carries the cause, and the instance signals th
          e stop to the application. It then tries once to release the le
          ase. It does not retry, and it does not reopen itself.
      58  - **Recovery from a stop is a normal start.** The application r
          eopens the database, and the instance catches the cache up from
           its recorded position, as after a crash. If the cache cannot b
          e opened, the start fails, and the cache is not rebuilt automat
          ically.
      59  - **One commit at a time.** Commits to a database happen strict
          ly one after another, and the storage enforces this, not the le
          ase or a clock. Only the lease holder writes, and any instance 
          can become the holder.

⏺ Yes, it is the same state. The instance does not know whether its cache is
  current, so it refuses with the temporary error and keeps trying. The bullet
  is in the spec and pushed, and I noted your observation in the to-do list.

  Next topic: the formats. First the manifest.

  The spec already fixes most of it: JSON, a version, a branches map with main,
  the lease holder's random ID, and now the expiry time. My proposal:

  {
    "version": 1,
    "branches": {
      "main": {
        "head": "9f2c…e41a",
        "commits": 1042
      }
    },
    "lease": {
      "holder": "5b0e…77c3",
      "expires": "2026-11-11T12:00:30.123Z"
    }
  }

  Three things in it are new and yours to decide:

  - A branch entry is an object, not a plain string, so that it can gain fields
    later.
  - commits counts the change files in the log. Each change file would carry its
    own number too. An instance then knows how far its cache is behind before
    it walks the log, and a broken chain is detected. The names of the change
    files stay pure checksums.
  - An instance keeps the fields it does not know when it rewrites the manifest.
    Without this rule, an older version would silently delete the fields of a
    newer one at every renewal.

  A new database has no head, and a released lease has no lease entry.

  Agree?
✻ Crunched for 32s · done Wednesday 12:24 PM

❯ Drop the "commits", let's keep the essentials                                 

⏺ Dropped. The manifest then holds version, branches.main.head, and lease with
  holder and expires, and instances keep unknown fields when they rewrite it.

  Change files, question 1: the encoding.

  A change file holds the name of the file before it, the row changes, the
  schema changes, and the statements as evidence. The spec asks that it can gain
  fields later.

  - A: JSON Lines. One JSON object per line: a header line first, then one line
    per change. Anyone can read a log with standard tools, a large commit can be
    written and read as a stream, and it adds no dependency. The costs are
    size, and that JSON has no exact form for every SQLite value: integers above
    2^53 in other tools, infinity, blobs, and text that is not valid UTF-8. We
    would give every value an explicit type tag and prove the round trip with
    fuzz tests.
  - B: Protocol Buffers. Compact, and exact for every value by construction. The
    .proto file is a precise description of the format. The costs are a
    dependency, generated code, and that nobody can read a change file without a
    tool.
  - C: our own binary format. Full control, and the most for us to get right.

  I recommend A. A wrong value encoding is the dangerous failure, and it is the
  kind that fuzz tests catch well. A log that a person or an agent can read
  directly helps every later investigation into correctness. One caution applies
  to all three: change files are immutable, so whatever we choose, every future
  version must still read it.

  A, B, or C?

  {
    "version": 1,
    "branches": {
      "main": {
        "head": "9f2c…e41a"
      }
    },
    "lease": {
      "holder": "5b0e…77c3",
      "expires": "2026-11-11T12:00:30.123Z"
    }
  }

  - version is the version of the manifest format.
  - branches.main.head is the name of the newest change file, which is the
    SHA-256 checksum of its content. A new database has no head.
  - lease.holder is the random ID of the process that holds the lease, and
    lease.expires is the wall-clock time at which it expires. A released lease
    has no lease entry.
  - An instance keeps the fields it does not know when it rewrites the manifest.

  Agree with this as the manifest?

  Back to change files, question 1: the encoding, which is still open:

  - A: JSON Lines. Readable with standard tools and no dependency. It is larger,
    and every value needs an explicit type tag, proven by fuzz tests.
  - B: Protocol Buffers. Compact and exact by construction. It needs a
    dependency, generated code, and a tool to read a file.
  - C: our own binary format. Full control, and the most for us to get right.

  because change files are immutable.

❯ Subagent-research protocol buffers alternatives. I'd prefer a binary format   
  that is forwards and backwards compatible.                                    

⏺ Agent(Research binary encoding options) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ A research agent is now comparing binary encodings that are forward and
  backward compatible: Protocol Buffers with and without generated code, CBOR,
  FlatBuffers, Cap'n Proto, Avro and others, SQLite's own changeset format, and
  what comparable systems use. It writes a note to docs/research/, and I'll
  bring you the shortlist when it reports.

  Meanwhile, a question that does not depend on the encoding.

  Change files, question 2: what a change file holds.

  My proposal, with only the essentials:

  - A header: the format version, and the name of the change file before it. The
    first file has no previous name.
  - A list of changes, applied in order. Each change is one of three kinds:
    - Create a table: the table's name and its columns, as data and not as SQL
      text. Replay then needs no SQL parser.
    - Put a row: the table and the full row. It replaces any row with the same
      primary key.
    - Delete a row: the table and the primary key.
  - The statements, as evidence: each statement's SQL text, its parameters, and
    its non-deterministic inputs.

  A file holds the net effect of its commit, so a primary key appears at most
  once per table.

  One thing I left out and want you to decide knowingly: the wall-clock time of
  the commit. Restoring a database "as it was at 14:00" would need it, and it
  cannot be added to old files later.

  Agree, and with or without the commit time?

✻ Crunched for 55s · done Wednesday 12:30 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  I wrote the note at
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/change-file-encodings.md` and
  added a line for it to
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/README.md`. Nothing else
  changed, and nothing is committed.

  **Shortlist**
  1. **Protocol Buffers wire format, written by hand.** Use `protowire` (one
  571-line file, one module) or about 200 lines of our own code on
  `encoding/binary`, which already has the same varint and ZigZag functions. No
  `protoc`, no generated code, no reflection.
     - An old reader skips an unknown field by its wire type. That rule has not
  changed since 2008, and only four wire types are in use, so a strict decoder
  is small enough to review and fuzz completely.
     - Integers, floats (raw IEEE bits) and text (raw bytes) round-trip exactly,
  and NULL, empty text and empty blob stay distinct.
     - A file streams as length-delimited records. `protoc --decode_raw` and a
  published `.proto` file let other tools read it.
  2. **CBOR (RFC 8949, an Internet Standard), a strict subset with integer map
  keys.** It is fully self-describing, has a standard deterministic encoding and
  a standard for streams of items (RFC 8742), and `fxamacker/cbor` is healthy:
  v2.10.0 from 2026-10-28, one dependency, no advisories. It is second because
  there is more to restrict, its text type must be valid UTF-8, and none of the
  systems I surveyed uses it for a log.
  3. **Our own tag-length-value format,** only as a fallback.

  **What would change the ranking:** CBOR moves to first if Markus wants any
  change file to show every value's type without a schema, or if identical
  commits must give identical bytes.

  The note ends with a sketch of the layout: the whole file is one protobuf
  message. Its records are a header (format version, previous file), then table,
  upsert, delete, schema and statement records, then a trailer.

  **Surprising findings**
  - **SQLite text need not be UTF-8:** a STRICT `TEXT` column accepts invalid
  UTF-8 and NUL bytes (measured). `protobuf-go` rejects invalid UTF-8 in proto3
  `string` fields, and CBOR forbids it in text strings, so text must be stored
  as raw bytes.
  - **SQLite loses some floats:** a stored `-0.0` reads back as `0.0`, and NaN
  becomes NULL (measured and in the source).
  - **DAG-CBOR and AT Protocol's DRISL are ruled out:** DAG-CBOR forbids NaN,
  the infinities, `-0.0` and concatenated items. DRISL forbids floats.
  - **SQLite's changeset format has no version and no way to extend it,** and it
  cannot hold schema changes. Our build does not include the session extension
  anyway. Its value encoding, and its "undefined" value that is separate from
  NULL, are still good models.
  - **Weak Go libraries:** the best Go Avro library, `hamba/avro`, is archived
  and has 4 advisories. Go FlatBuffers has no buffer verifier. go-capnp is still
  alpha and calls itself beta software.

  **Could not verify**
  - The size and speed benchmark I found does not include
  `google.golang.org/protobuf` or `fxamacker/cbor`.
  - What `protobuf-go`'s fuzzing covers, and whether `fxamacker/cbor`'s fuzz
  corpus is the one it publishes.
  - A few points from memory, marked `[I]` in the note: the tools SBE and Cap'n
  Proto need, and the JSON rules on Infinity and NaN.
  - Neon's formats were not checked.

  A helper agent checked the formats of the comparable systems (Delta, Iceberg,
  Kafka, FoundationDB, CockroachDB, pgoutput, MySQL binlog, etcd, SlateDB, LTX,
  turbopuffer) in primary sources during this run.

⏺ Agent "Research binary encoding options" finished · 13m 35s

⏺ The research is done and committed as docs/research/change-file-encodings.md.
  Its answer for question 1, the encoding:

  1. The Protocol Buffers wire format, written by hand. We use only the byte
     format, without protoc, generated code, or reflection. An old reader skips
     a field it does not know by a rule that has not changed since 2008. The
     format has only four wire types, so a strict decoder is about 200 lines
     that we can review and fuzz completely. Integers, floats, and text are
     stored exactly, and a file is a stream of records, so a commit of millions
     of rows never has to be in memory at once. Any Protocol Buffers tool can
     still read a file, and we publish a .proto file as documentation.
  2. CBOR, an Internet Standard. Every value carries its type, so a file can be
     dumped without a schema. It is second because there is more of it to
     restrict, its text type must be valid UTF-8, and none of the surveyed
     systems uses it for a log.
  3. Our own format, only as a fallback.

  The rest were ruled out, mostly on their Go libraries: the best Avro library
  is archived with four security advisories, FlatBuffers for Go cannot verify a
  buffer, and Cap'n Proto for Go is still alpha. SQLite's own changeset format
  has no version and no way to gain fields.

  Two findings matter whatever we choose. SQLite accepts text that is not valid
  UTF-8, even in a strict table, so text must be stored as raw bytes. And SQLite
  itself turns -0.0 into 0.0 and NaN into NULL.

  I recommend option 1 with our own small implementation and no dependency,
  tested against Google's protowire package and protoc as oracles. That is the
  same pattern as our SQLite binding.

  Option 1?

  Question 2, what a change file holds and whether it records the commit time,
  is still open above.
