› Message from fabrik:builder
  The manifest now uses `encoding/json/v2` and `encoding/json/jsontext`, and
  nothing in the repository imports `encoding/json` any more. A `depguard` rule
  keeps it out in `make lint` and in CI. `make test`, `make lint` and the race
  test pass, and `FuzzParseManifest` ran five minutes twice with no finding. The
  sample files are byte for byte the same. There are two small, deliberate
  changes of behaviour, described below.

  All work is on `worktree-storage-formats` in
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/storage-formats`. Nothing
  is pushed and the worktree is clean.

  ## Commits
  - `fc0d02f` fixes the comment on `Change.upsert_rows` in `/Users/maragubot/Dev
  eloper/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/storage-formats/proto/s4ql/v1/commit.proto`, as
  you asked mid-run. It includes the regenerated `internal/s4qlpb/commit.pb.go`,
  where only the two comment lines changed. `make lint-proto` passes.
  - `e2a314c` is the move to version 2 and the lint rule.
  - `6f5e824` fixes what the self-review found.
  - `91e2e68` adds Step 7 to `docs/diary/2026-11-12-storage-formats.md`, author
  `json-builder`.

  ## What changed
  - **Manifest** (`internal/format/manifest.go`): parsing and writing now go
  through four unexported structs. Each has a field `Unknown
  map[string]jsontext.Value` with the tag `json:",embed"`, which collects the
  members the struct does not know. In Go 1.27 the tag is `embed`, not `inline`
  or `unknown`. The head is a `*Name`, so the existing `UnmarshalText` checks
  it.
  - **Writing** uses `json.Marshal` with these options:
    - `jsontext.WithIndent("  ")` and `json.Deterministic(true)` give the layout
  and the sorted unknown keys.
    - `jsontext.PreserveRawStrings(true)` keeps the escapes in unknown values,
  as `json.Compact` and `json.Indent` did before.
    - `jsontext.EscapeForJS(true)` writes U+2028 and U+2029 in our own strings
  and in keys as `
  `, as the version 1 encoder did.
  - **Other users of version 1**: `internal/format/name_test.go`,
  `internal/sim/simulation_test.go`, `internal/sim/toy_test.go` and
  `internal/sqlite/sqlite_test.go` only needed the import changed. In
  `internal/sqlite/update/main.go`, `json.MarshalIndent(m, "", "\t")` became
  `json.Marshal(m, jsontext.WithIndent("\t"), json.Deterministic(true))`. I
  checked that it writes `internal/sqlite/c/source.json` with the same bytes.
  - **Test helper**: `decodeJSON` in `manifest_test.go` uses an
  `UnmarshalFromFunc` that keeps numbers as their text, in place of `UseNumber`.
  - **Docs**: the doc comments and `internal/format/README.md` now describe the
  new handling. The README has one new point with Markus's quote and the lint
  rule.

  ## How much of our code went away
  The code in `manifest.go`, without comments and blank lines, went from 328
  lines to 161. Gone: `decodeObject`, `decodeString`, `checkSurrogates`,
  `appendObject`, `appendString`, `member`, the UTF-8 check on the holder, and
  the `json.Indent` step. A holder with invalid UTF-8 is still refused, because
  `json.Marshal` refuses it and the error is wrapped in `ErrMalformed`.

  What had to stay, and why:
  - **A first pass that reads only `version` and `lease` as raw JSON.**
  `{"version":1,"branches":[]}` must still be "newer version", which one pass
  into the full structs cannot do. Also, a null lease must be refused, and
  version 2 reads null into a pointer as nil, the same as a missing lease.
  - **`checkManifestVersion` on the raw text.** Version 2 cannot tell `0.0`,
  `0e0` and versions larger than `uint64` apart the way the tests require.
  - **Checks for missing `branches`, `main` and `head`.** Version 2 leaves these
  nil when they are missing or null.
  - **The parsing and writing of the expiry**, which the brief said to keep.

  ## Behaviour that differs
  1. **A key given twice is now refused at any depth, including inside fields
  the manifest does not know.**
     - The old parser only checked the objects it parsed itself, and wrote such
  duplicates back unchanged. The doc comment already said "a key given twice in
  the same object", so this brings the code in line with it.
     - The whole document is checked before the version is read. So
  `{"version":1,"x":{"a":1,"a":2}}` and
  `{"version":1,"branches":{"main":1,"main":2}}` are now `ErrMalformed`, where
  they were `ErrNewerVersion`.
     - Keeping the old split would mean allowing duplicate names in the first
  pass. Then `{"version":0,"version":1}` would no longer be refused, which a
  test pins.
     - New tests pin the new behaviour. If you want the old split back, it needs
  code of our own again.
  2. **A raw U+2028 or U+2029 inside a value the manifest does not know is now
  written escaped.**
     - It is the same JSON value, but different bytes.
     - The alternative was to drop `EscapeForJS`. That would instead write our
  own holder and keys with raw U+2028, where version 1 escaped them. I chose to
  keep what s4ql writes itself unchanged.
  3. **Smaller things:**
     - Some error messages are less precise, and some name the unexported types,
  such as `cannot unmarshal JSON number into Go format.leaseJSON`. The error
  classes are unchanged.
     - In the toy of `internal/sim`, a change file with no writes is now written
  with `"sets":[]` instead of `"sets":null`, so its name changes. Nothing
  depends on these names.

  I compared the old and new code with a differential fuzz that I did not
  commit:
  - Parsing: about 12.7 million runs, comparing the error class and the written
  bytes.
  - Writing: 2.8 million runs with any holder and expiry.

  Both reviewers ran their own. None found a difference other than 1 and 2.

  **One thing to decide (yours and Markus's):** the order of the output now
  rests on how version 2 behaves, not on anything it documents. The
  documentation of `Deterministic` only guarantees the same output from the same
  binary, "not across different builds of a program (such as different source
  or toolchain version...)". Where the unknown fields go is not documented
  either. The tests would catch a change in a Go upgrade, but fixing it would
  need ordering code of our own again.

  ## The guard and CI
  - **The rule:** the new `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.golangci.yml` keeps
  the standard linters that ran before and adds `depguard`, denying
  `encoding/json$`. The `$` makes the match exact, so `encoding/json/v2` and
  `encoding/json/jsontext` are allowed.
  - **It fires:** before the migration, `golangci-lint run` reported `import
  'encoding/json' is not allowed from list 'json'` in `manifest.go`.
  - **Crosscheck module:** in `internal/sqlite/crosscheck`, `golangci-lint
  config path` prints `../../../.golangci.yml`, and a temporary file there that
  imported `encoding/json` was reported.
  - **How I checked CI:** I read the reusable workflow with `gh api
  repos/maragudk/workflows/contents/.github/workflows/lint.yml`. It runs
  `golangci/golangci-lint-action@v9` with `version: latest` at the repository
  root, so it reads the same file. The crosscheck job runs the action in its own
  directory, where the file is found in the parent directories in the same way.
  I did not run CI itself.
  - **What it does not check:** generated files, by golangci-lint's default, so
  the rule does not see `internal/s4qlpb/commit.pb.go` (which does not import
  `encoding/json` anyway). Also files behind a build tag that is off where lint
  runs, such as `internal/sqlite/asan.go`.

  ## Verification, on the final code `6f5e824`
  - `make test` passes in both modules. `internal/format` coverage is 96.9%,
  with every block of `manifest.go` covered.
  - `go test -race -shuffle on ./internal/format/` passes.
  - `make lint` reports 0 issues in both modules, and `buf lint` and `buf
  format` are clean.
  - `FuzzParseManifest` ran 5 minutes with no finding: 24,987,689 runs on
  `e2a314c` and 27,298,043 on `6f5e824`.
  - Removing each of the options `PreserveRawStrings`, `EscapeForJS` or
  `Deterministic` makes at least one test fail. The new order test failed 5 runs
  out of 5 without `Deterministic`.

  ## Self-review
  I ran the `fabrik:code-review` skill. Both reviewers found nothing serious and
  found the same set of minor points, which I fixed in `6f5e824`:
  - New tests for:
    - the version being checked before the lease (swapping the two checks had
  passed every test)
    - the order of unknown keys at all four levels (the old test had passed by
  chance about 1 run in 8 without `Deterministic`)
    - U+2028 in unknown values
    - the parsed manifest not sharing memory with its input, at every level
  - More accurate doc comments and README wording.
  - A missing expiry is now reported as "has no expiry".
  - The test-only `jsonNumber` type moved into the one function that uses it.

  I left the less precise error messages and the double parse as they are.

  ## Editing tools broke escapes in the tests
  The editing tools turn the text `\uXXXX` into the raw character. This had
  already weakened two tests and a fuzz seed from the earlier build:
  - "should refuse a key given twice in another spelling" had no escape.
  - "should read an escaped surrogate pair" read a raw emoji.
  - The `FuzzParseManifest` seed had a raw emoji.

  I restored the escapes in the two tests and added a second seed with the
  escaped pair, keeping the raw one. The diary says how to write such escapes
  safely. Other files written in earlier steps may have the same problem; I did
  not audit them.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Summary

  The harness is built, verified at 10,000 seeds, and self-reviewed. Every
  review fix is committed on `worktree-sim-harness`; only the last diary step is
  not. Near the end, the tooling moved this session's working directory to the
  other builder's worktree
  (`/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/storage-formats`) and
  refused writes and git commands in `sim-harness`. I did not touch
  `storage-formats`. Step 5 of the diary (self-review and final numbers) is
  therefore in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515
  -adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/diary-step-5.md`, to append to
  `docs/diary/2026-11-12-simulation-harness.md` and commit. Steps 2 to 4 are
  committed.

  **One design question for Markus.** A wall clock set backward on a holder that
  can still reach S3 gives stale reads with only one clock wrong. The holder
  renews with an expiry that is already past on the other instances' clocks.
  Another instance takes the lease, and both of the holder's own checks still
  pass. Both reviewers showed it with the correct toy once the partition is
  removed:
  - reviewer B: seeds 4, 31 and 47 of 100;
  - reviewer A: 19 of 200 seeds.

  So `docs/todo.md`'s "A wall clock that is set backward is caught by the
  monotonic clock" holds only while the holder writes no expiry from the wrong
  clock. The workload keeps a backward-shifted instance cut off from the store,
  and says why in `internal/sim/simulation_test.go`. One possible answer, which
  I have not tested: the holder refuses to renew when its wall clock and
  monotonic clock disagree since its last renewal.

  Commits, oldest first: `af0ad2e` (host), `1882e48` (harness and toy),
  `43e762c` (lint), `a5d1f24` (diary Steps 2–4), `6a2d1a4` (review fixes).

  ## What I built and chose

  **`internal/host`: the interface, `host.Host`.**
  - Nine methods: `Go`, `NewSemaphore`, `Sleep`, `WithTimeout`, `Yield`, `Wall`,
  `Monotonic`, `Uint64`, `Buggify`, plus `Task.Wait` and
  `Semaphore.Acquire`/`Release`.
  - `host.New()` is production. `Wall` is `time.Now().Round(0)`; `Monotonic` is
  the time since `New`; `Uint64` uses `crypto/rand`; `Buggify` is always false.
  - There is no `Trace` on the interface. Library code doesn't need it, and
  simulation code gets it from the concrete types.

  **`internal/sim`: the scheduler.**
  - `*sim.Scheduler` is the host of the test itself. `s.NewInstance(name)`
  returns a `*sim.Instance` for each simulated process.
  - An instance adds `ShiftWall`, `StopMonotonic`/`StartMonotonic`, `Crash`,
  `Crashed` and `Trace`.
  - Sleeps and timeouts of an instance run on its monotonic clock, as Go's
  timers do, so a stopped clock stalls the renewal. Yields and lock hand-overs
  run on simulated time.
  - A task waits, sets timeouts and uses semaphores only through its own
  instance's host, or through the scheduler; otherwise the run fails.
  - Buggify follows FoundationDB's three gates, with probabilities that are
  options; every decision is in the trace.
  - Ties between a due timeout and a due task are decided by the seed.
  - `sim.Free` is `host.New()` plus `Gosched` for yields, buggify, and a `Run`
  that waits for all tasks. It has no instances.
  - The `goroutineID` check stays on with no option. It costs about 1.6× on the
  workloads and 3.6–5× on the scheduler alone (A/B, loaded machine), but the
  lint can't see goroutines that dependencies or test code start.

  **How a crash works.**
  - A crashed instance's tasks are never picked again.
  - They are unwound with `runtime.Goexit` after every task that did not crash
  has returned. Their deferred calls run then.
  - The test restarts by copying the cache and its `-wal` (not the `-shm`) at
  the moment of the crash, and starting a new instance on the copy.
  - The differences from a real crash are written in the package doc.

  **`internal/simlint`: the lint.**
  - A `go/analysis` analyzer, type-resolved, in its own module so that
  `golang.org/x/tools` stays out of the library's `go.mod`.
  - It forbids everything the diary listed, plus: `context.AfterFunc`,
  `Background` and `TODO`; channel receive, send, range, and a select without a
  default; `iter.Pull`/`Pull2`; `runtime.SetFinalizer`/`AddCleanup`;
  `hash/maphash`; and `x/sync/errgroup`.
  - It skips `_test.go` files. It exempts `internal/host`, `internal/sim` and
  `internal/sqlite/update`.
  - `make lint` builds it in its module and runs it on the root. `make test`
  runs its tests.
  - The reusable `test.yml` already runs `go test -race`. I added a CI job,
  `simulation`, that also tests the lint, runs it, lints it, and runs the
  free-running mode under `-race`, so that guarantee doesn't rest on the other
  repository.

  **The toy** follows the agreed lease design where an ability needs it: two
  clocks, spare takeover after expiry plus a margin, catch-up from the log, and
  a stop after a local failure. The workload model:
  - every read's value is compared with the log at its commit;
  - commits are matched by a transaction ID;
  - some instance must serve a real read within 2 minutes after the workload;
  - a restarted cache must equal the crashed one.

  **Real bug found in the toy.** The two-instance workload found a bug that the
  spike's single instance could not have produced. A swap was applied with its
  acknowledgement lost, another instance took over during the resolution, and
  the toy reported "lease lost" for a commit that is in the log. It now walks
  the log back, as `todo.md` "A manifest swap whose outcome is unknown" says.

  ## Verification numbers (after the review fixes)

  `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=10000`, every seed run twice:

  ## Self-review

  The `fabrik:code-review` skill ran two competing `fable` reviewers over the
  diff. I fixed every consensus finding and every serious single finding; Step 5
  lists them all. The most serious was that a host timeout wrapped in
  `context.WithValue` made the context package start a goroutine, so one seed
  gave two runs. The timeout now embeds a `context.WithCancelCause` copy of its
  parent. The trade-off: a context derived from it reports `Canceled` with
  `DeadlineExceeded` as its cause, and the host's waits return
  `DeadlineExceeded`.

  Left as they are (single-reviewer, minor): release and task-start jitter have
  no test of their own, and the planted-bug test only asserts "found" at 200
  seeds or more.

  ## What the harness still cannot do

  - It switches tasks only at waits. It hides data races from the race detector,
  and never has two goroutines in SQLite.
  - Crash:
    - deferred calls of crashed tasks run at the end of the run;
    - a request in flight at a crash is never delivered;
    - power loss is not simulated.
  - A stopped monotonic clock stalls sleeps and timeouts, but not yields or
  locks. There is no "pause the whole instance".
  - Contexts derived from a host timeout report `Canceled`, not
  `DeadlineExceeded`.
  - The lint can't see:
    - goroutines started inside dependencies (an S3 client);
    - files behind other build tags;
    - map-order or file-name nondeterminism;
    - a `sync.Mutex` held across a wait, which hangs the run.
  - `Free` has no instances, clocks or crashes, and keeps no trace.
  - A real S3 client under simulation is still not shown.

  ## What builders of the store and the core must know

  - **Library code takes a `host.Host`, passes on the context it was given, and
  never creates a root context.** The lint forbids `context.Background`; use
  `context.WithoutCancel(ctx)` for the lease task.
  - **Task names must be unique** among a host's running goroutines; put an ID
  in them.
  - **Locks:** hold `host.Semaphore`s across waits, never a `sync.Mutex`. Call
  `Yield` where shared state is touched between waits.
  - **Timeouts:** tell a timeout from a cancellation by the error of a host wait
  or by `context.Cause`, not by `ctx.Err()` of a derived context.
  - **No condition-wait primitive.** "Wait until serving" must poll with `Sleep`
  or use semaphores. This is worth deciding before the core is written.
  - **The simulated store:**
    - waits through the scheduler's host (network time) before it applies
  anything;
    - gives each instance its own client, which a partition can cut off;
    - may not use a world semaphore from instance tasks;
    - has to carry requests on a task of the test if they should survive their
  sender's crash.
  - **Checks must run inside the run, by a task.** After a crash, close the
  crashed instance's connections after `Run`. To restart, copy the db and its
  `-wal` at the crash, not the `-shm`.

  ## What is now out of date in `docs/todo.md`

  - **"Concurrency and the simulation harness":**
    - "in `internal/sim` once merged", the spike wording, and "One small runtime
  interface…" are now built as `internal/host`, `internal/sim` and
  `internal/simlint`.
    - The lint condition is built, and covers more than listed.
    - "Every seed is run twice in CI" holds at 200 seeds.
    - The free-running mode runs under `-race` in its own CI job.
    - "Not yet shown: crash and restart, two instances competing for the lease"
  is shown; a real S3 client under simulation is still not.
    - "A separate stress test on the real clock" is not built.
  - **"The lease and its clocks":** the backward wall clock sentence needs
  qualifying, per the design question above.
  - **"A manifest swap whose outcome is unknown":** the toy found exactly the
  case the design describes (walking the log back), which confirms the design.
  Nothing in the item is wrong.
  - **"Local I/O failure handling":** "The simulation injects the failure. A
  buggify point…" is now possible; the toy has such a point with both variants.
  The item stays, because the core isn't built.

  Not out of date: `docs/spec.md` "Testing" and "Concurrency", `README.md`, and
  `AGENTS.md` (no changes needed).

⏺ The harness is built. Its report, and one finding for you.

  What exists on branch worktree-sim-harness, not yet in a pull request:

  - internal/host: the one interface that library code waits through. It covers
    goroutines, locks, sleep, timeouts, two clocks, random numbers, and buggify.
    I read it, and it is small and clean.
  - internal/sim: the scheduler, now with several instances per run. Each has
    its own wall clock and monotonic clock that a test can move, and each can
    crash and restart.
  - internal/simlint: the lint. It forbids more than I listed, for example
    blocking channel operations and context.Background.

  Verification over 10,000 seeds, each run twice: no pair of traces differed,
  with two instances, clock moves, a crash per run, and buggify on. Six planted
  bugs were each found by between 1,352 and 9,915 seeds, and by none when the
  ability they need was switched off.

  The two-instance run also found a real bug in the toy: a swap with a lost
  acknowledgement, followed by a takeover, was reported as "lease lost" although
  the commit was in the log. The fix was exactly the walk back through the log
  that we designed.

  You were partly right about the worktrees. When I switched into the formats
  worktree, the harness builder's working directory moved with me, and it could
  no longer write in its own. It lost nothing, but it couldn't commit its last
  diary step, which I have now done. I won't switch worktrees while a builder
  runs.

  The finding: one wrong wall clock is enough for stale reads. The todo says a
  wall clock set backward is caught by the monotonic clock. That is not true in
  this case:

  1. The holder's wall clock is behind. It renews, and writes an expiry that is
     already past for everyone else.
  2. A spare takes the lease, correctly by its own clock, and commits.
  3. The holder's two checks both pass, so it serves reads from its cache until
     its next renewal fails, up to 20 seconds later.

  A spare whose clock is ahead does the same. The spec already covers this: it
  says the wall clocks must agree and that wrong clocks can make a read miss
  commits. Only the todo's sentence overclaims, and I'll correct it.

  There may be a way to drop the need for agreeing wall clocks altogether:
  decide takeover from S3's own timestamps, the manifest's last-modified time
  against the time on S3's response. No instance's wall clock would then matter.
  I haven't checked whether S3 and compatible stores make that reliable.

  The lease code is part of the core, which is the next piece but one. Should I
  have that idea researched before the core is built, or keep the wall-clock
  design as it is?

  ## Reviewer findings I did not act on

  I left these alone because you ruled out changes to `internal/format` and
  `docs/todo.md`:
  - **Protobuf error text varies by build.**
  `internal/format/reader.go:138,176,186` and `check.go:137` include protobuf's
  error text. Protobuf picks the separator after `proto:` from a hash of the
  binary, so a `-ldflags=-s` build gives different text. A traced error would
  replay only within the same binary. Nothing traces these errors yet.
  - **`ParseManifest` depends on the time zone.** It uses `time.Parse`, which
  reads `time.Local`. For an expiry with an offset other than `Z`, the returned
  location and the printed error text depend on the machine's time zone. The
  instant itself is right. A `.UTC()` on the parsed time would fix it. simlint
  does not flag `time.Local`.
  - **simlint does not flag `io.Pipe`.** It waits outside the host, and it is
  the natural way to connect `CommitWriter` to a store's request body.
  - **`docs/todo.md` is stale.** It still lists the harness as not built: line
  50 says "in `internal/sim` once merged", and line 72 says "Not yet shown:

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +11, created PR #6 (ctrl+o to expand)

› Message from general-purpose
  I wrote the note at
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/s3-clock.md`. It is the only
  change in the repository, and I made no git changes. The test objects under
  `research/s3-clock/` are deleted; a final listing of that prefix returned
  KeyCount 0. The note contains no credential values.

  **Short answer.** The idea holds up in principle, and it is better than
  today's design. Instead of needing every machine's wall clock to be right, it
  needs the store's own clocks to agree with each other. But it is not
  clock-free, and it is safe only under these conditions:
  - **The taker uses `If-Match` on the ETag from the same response it
  measured.** Then every delay errs in the safe direction: a slow response, a
  `Date` stamped late, a stale cache, a renewal that lands after the read, or a
  taker that acts late.
  - **The margin covers header resolution and skew inside the store.**
  Resolution costs under 1.5 s. Skew where the `Date` clock runs ahead is the
  unsafe direction, and nothing in a single response shows it. With `L` = 30 s,
  `m` = 10 s covers resolution plus about 8 s of store skew. No store promises
  even that.
  - **The holder follows rules no margin can replace.** These hold for every
  lease design, the Kubernetes one included:
    - Anchor the window at the start of the first attempt of a renewal, not at a
  retry and not at the response.
    - Check the window after reading the cache, not before.
    - Measure with a clock that counts suspended time. Go on Linux uses
  `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, which "does not count time that the system is suspended".
  - **The store must set `Last-Modified` itself**, at or after the moment the
  request arrived. Stamping at the start or at completion is fine. A value
  supplied by the client is not.

  My recommendation, for your design call: if trust in correctness comes first,
  use the clock-free rule as the default. The taker reads version V, waits `L +
  m` on its own monotonic clock, and then does a CAS against V. Kubernetes and
  the DynamoDB Lock Client work this way. Add a graceful release on clean
  shutdown, so only a crash costs about 40 s. The S3 clock could then be an
  optional fast path ("CAS at `t + max(0, L + m − D)`"). That helps liveness,
  not safety, and it should be turned on only for stores that have been checked.

  **Findings that matter most for the decision:**
  1. **Tigris lets any client set `Last-Modified`, which I found in testing.** A
  PUT carrying a `Last-Modified: <anything>` request header stores that value.
  HEAD and GET then return it verbatim: a date in 2001, one in 2049, even
  `garbage`. `If-Match` PUTs accept it too, and `CopyObject` copies it to the
  new object. The listing's `LastModified` stays the real server time. Two
  things had no effect on Tigris: a skewed `x-amz-date` (I tried −1000 to +300
  s) and `x-minio-source-mtime`. MinIO has the same hole by another route. In
  the source, any caller with `s3:PutObject` can set the modification time with
  `x-minio-source-mtime`, and the replication permission is checked only when a
  replica-status header is present. MinIO also allows its nodes 15 minutes of
  clock skew, in both its docs and its code.
  2. **`Last-Modified` and `Date` usually come from different machines.** On
  Tigris, `Date` comes from an nginx front end; nginx does not pass the upstream
  `Date` by default. In distributed MinIO, the node that received the write
  stamps `Last-Modified` and the node that serves the read stamps `Date`. AWS
  says nothing about S3's internal clocks. The closest it comes is the legacy
  Glacier docs: "`Date` returned may drift slightly from other dates".
  3. **I found no precedent for `Date − Last-Modified` from one response.**
     - The clock-free observe-and-wait rule is well established: Kubernetes
  client-go, the DynamoDB Lock Client, Iceberg, arrow-rs `object_store`.
     - Most object-store leases do what s4ql does today, a holder-written expiry
  checked against the taker's wall clock: Litestream, Hudi (with a 500 ms
  buffer), Vault GCS, and several small libraries.
     - Gunnar Morling's S3 leader election uses S3's `Last-Modified`, apparently
  against local time, and concludes it is "only ever eventually correct".
  4. **Tigris measurements** (about 400 requests):
     - `Date` was present on every response, including 412 and 404.
     - Both headers have one-second resolution, and so does the listing.
     - `Last-Modified` is the completion time: a 10 s throttled upload was
  stamped at its end.
     - `Date − Last-Modified` was never negative in about 300 samples, and was
  0–1 s right after a write.
     - `Date` is stamped when the response is produced: a HEAD that took 2.6 s
  carried a `Date` 2 s after its request arrived.

  **Surprises:**
  - The Tigris `Last-Modified` override above. It also breaks RFC 9110's rule
  that `Last-Modified` must not be later than `Date`.
  - Tigris's `X-Amz-Request-Id` is a Unix time in nanoseconds. That is
  undocumented, but it gives a sub-second server clock for every request.
  - Tigris accepted a request whose signature was 1000 s old, past AWS's
  15-minute limit.
  - Garage bumps each new version to the previous one's timestamp plus 1 ms. So
  `Last-Modified` can be after `Date`, which would stall a taker that relies
  only on the S3 clock.
  - AWS does not document whether a single PutObject is stamped at the start or
  at completion, or whether it ever takes the time from `x-amz-date`. Either
  moment is safe, but taking it from `x-amz-date` would bring back the holder's
  wall clock. That needs a test against AWS: sign a PUT with an old `x-amz-date`
  and read `Last-Modified` back. We have no AWS credentials, so I could not run
  it.

  Sourcing: two research agents gathered the prior art and the per-store
  details. I re-checked the key source claims myself: Kubernetes, Hudi, the
  DynamoDB Lock Client, MinIO and Garage. The note marks which quotes I
  re-checked and which I did not. My probe scripts are in
  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9e
  b0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/s3clock/`.

⏺ Agent "Research S3 as lease clock" finished · 17m 51s

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The harness is up as pull request #6 with CI running, and the S3-clock
  research is back. I'm committing the research note, then I'll give you the
  findings.

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The research says S3 as the clock is better than what we have, but not safe
  enough to rest the lease on. The note is in docs/research/s3-clock.md,
  committed.

  Why not:

  - A client can set Last-Modified on Tigris. The researcher found this by
    testing our own bucket: a PUT with a Last-Modified header stores whatever it
    says, even a date in 2049. MinIO has the same hole through a header of its
    own.
  - The two timestamps usually come from different machines. On Tigris, Date
    comes from a front-end proxy and Last-Modified from storage. Distributed
    MinIO allows its nodes 15 minutes of skew. AWS documents nothing about its
    internal clocks.
  - Nobody does it. The researcher found no system that compares Date with
    Last-Modified.

  So it swaps "our machines' clocks agree" for "the store's clocks agree", which
  we can't check either.

  What the research found instead is the rule that Kubernetes and the DynamoDB
  Lock Client use, which compares no clocks at all:

  1. An instance that wants the lease reads the manifest.
  2. It waits for the lease duration plus a margin, on its own monotonic clock.
  3. If the manifest is unchanged, it takes the lease with the compare-and-swap.
     If it changed, the holder is alive, and the wait starts again.

  This needs nothing of the store beyond the compare-and-swap we already
  require. Each machine only has to measure a duration correctly, and never has
  to agree with another machine about the time.

  What it costs: an instance that starts after a crash always waits the full
  time, about 35 to 40 seconds, before it serves, even if the crash was an hour
  ago. A clean shutdown still releases the lease, so a normal restart is
  immediate. A spare that is already running takes over as fast as today.

  What changes in the design:

  - The manifest: lease.expires goes. In its place come the lease duration and a
    value that changes at every renewal, so that a renewal is visible.
  - The holder's read check stays as agreed, with both of its own clocks. It now
    measures only "less than the lease duration since my renewal was sent".
  - The spec: the bullet on the wall-clock expiry is replaced, and "the wall
    clocks of the instances must agree" disappears.

  This is the "duration and a counter" option you passed over on Tuesday with "A
  is fine for now". The simulation has since shown that A fails with a single
  wrong clock, so I now recommend this one. S3's timestamps could later shorten
  the wait after a crash, switched on per store.

  Do you agree to the clock-free rule?

✻ Baked for 18s · done Thursday 2:23 PM

❯ Re. the clock-free: didn't we discard this design already? But I think it     
  sounds good.                                                                  

⏺ Yes, we did. On Wednesday I offered two ways to record the lease: an absolute
  expiry time, or a duration and a counter that another instance watches stand
  still. You chose the expiry time with "A is fine for now". The clock-free rule
  is that second option.

  It lost then because the expiry time was simpler and we believed the two-clock
  check covered a single wrong clock. The simulation has now shown that it
  doesn't, which is the new information.

  Since you agree, the spec needs new wording in two bullets, and the manifest
  format changes. I'll take them one at a time.

  The first bullet today:

  ▎ The lease expires at a time on the wall clock. The manifest records when the
  ▎ lease expires, and another instance takes the lease once its own wall clock
  ▎ has passed that time. The wall clocks of the instances must therefore 
  ▎ agree.

  Proposed:

  ▎ The lease times out when the manifest stands still. The manifest records how
  ▎ long the lease lasts, and it changes at every renewal. Another instance 
  ▎ takes the lease only after it has watched the manifest stay unchanged for 
  ▎ longer than that, by its own monotonic clock. No instance compares its clock
  ▎ with another's. An instance that starts after a crash therefore waits at 
  ▎ least one lease duration before it serves.

  Okay to write that?
✻ Cogitated for 23s · done Thursday 2:52 PM

❯ It's not entirely that design, right? There is still no counter?              

⏺ It is that design, counter included. My wording hid it behind "it changes at
  every renewal", which was too vague.

  Something in the manifest has to change at each renewal, because S3's ETag
  comes from the content. A renewal that wrote the same bytes would give the
  same ETag, and two things would break:

  - The watcher couldn't see the renewal. It would conclude that the holder is
    dead.
  - Its compare-and-swap would still succeed against the renewed manifest, so it
    would take the lease from a live holder.

  With the expiry time we got this for free, since the time was different at
  every renewal. Without it, we need a value that exists only to change. The
  options:

  - A counter that the holder raises at every write of the manifest. It can
    never repeat.
  - A random value, new at every renewal. It works as well, but says nothing to
    a person who reads the manifest.
  - The time of the renewal from the holder's wall clock. It is readable, but it
    brings a wall clock back in, and a clock that is set backward could in
    theory repeat a value.

  I recommend the counter. The manifest's lease would then hold three things:
  the holder, the duration, and the counter.

  Is the counter okay?

✻ Brewed for 12s · done Thursday 2:53 PM

❯ What if we just keep writing the wall clock time but don't use it? In         
  nanosecond precision it's extremely unlikely to collide.                      

⏺ It would work in practice, but I'd still take the counter, for two reasons:

  - Safety would rest on the wall clock again. The collision that matters is a
    renewal that writes the exact bytes another instance is watching. That takes
    a clock set backward by just the wrong amount, or a clock that stands
    still, which some virtual machines have done. Both are rare, but a wall
    clock that misbehaves is exactly the dependency we just removed. Some
    platforms also have much coarser clocks than a nanosecond.
  - The simulation would find it. It sets wall clocks backward on purpose, with
    simulated times that collide far more easily than real ones. We'd then have
    to teach the checker that this failure "can't happen", and that kind of
    exception is hard to trust later.

  The counter can't repeat, costs one integer, and the simulation can check it
  exactly.

  What the time gives that the counter doesn't is readability: a person looking
  at the manifest sees when the lease was last renewed. If you want that, we can
  store both, with the counter for correctness and the time as information that
  nothing reads.

  Counter alone, or counter plus the time for people?

✻ Sautéed for 26s · done Thursday 2:55 PM

❯ Or the clock + a random salt                                                  

⏺ That works too. With a 64-bit random salt, safety no longer rests on the
  clock, and the time is there for people.

  Against time plus a counter, the difference is small:

  - The counter can't repeat at all. The salt repeats with a chance of 1 in 2^64
    per pair, which is nothing in practice.
  - The counter reads better in a manifest and in a simulation trace: "renewal
    17" against sixteen random hex digits.
  - The salt needs no state. The holder doesn't have to remember the last value.

  I lean towards time plus counter, for the first two points, but either is
  sound. Which one do you want?

✻ Churned for 9s · done Thursday 2:55 PM

❯ But then we have to handle counter overflows and all that stuff. And I like   
  that there's no state to keep track of.                                       

⏺ Time plus salt it is. The holder writes the time of the renewal and a fresh
  random value, and nothing ever reads either for a decision.

  Back to the first spec bullet, now with that in it:

  ▎ The lease times out when the manifest stands still. The manifest records how
  ▎ long the lease lasts, and every renewal changes it by writing a new random 
  ▎ value. Another instance takes the lease only after it has watched the 
  ▎ manifest stay unchanged for longer than that, by its own monotonic clock. No
  ▎ instance compares its clock with another's. An instance that starts after a
  ▎ crash therefore waits at least one lease duration before it serves.

  It replaces "The lease expires at a time on the wall clock". Okay to write
  that?

✻ Churned for 7s · done Thursday 2:56 PM

❯ Yes. But we agree that regular shutdowns still don't have to wait, right?     

⏺ Yes. A clean shutdown releases the lease, and a released lease has no lease
  entry in the manifest, so the next instance takes it at once without watching.
  The spec already says so: "A clean shutdown releases the lease; otherwise it
  times out." Only a crash, or a holder that can't reach S3, costs the wait.

  I'll write the bullet into the spec when the object store builder has
  finished, because I share its worktree and don't want to commit under it.

  The second bullet today:

  ▎ Clocks affect only reads. Before a read, the holder checks the lease against
  ▎ its wall clock and its monotonic clock, and both must show that the lease 
  ▎ holds. If the clocks are wrong, a read can miss the commits of a newer 
  ▎ holder. Writes are never affected, because S3 checks the manifest swap.

  Proposed:

  ▎ Clocks affect only reads. Before a read, the holder checks that less than 
  ▎ the lease's duration has passed since it sent its last renewal, by its wall 
  ▎ clock and by its monotonic clock, and both must show it. If an instance 
  ▎ measures time wrongly, a read can miss the commits of a newer holder. Writes
  ▎ are never affected, because S3 checks the manifest swap.

  The holder keeps both of its clocks because Go's monotonic clock on Linux
  stands still while a machine is suspended, and the wall clock catches that.
  Each clock is only compared with itself, never with another machine's.

  Okay to write that?

✻ Sautéed for 17s · done Thursday 2:57 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Both bullets are agreed; I'll write them together.

  The third and last piece is the manifest. Its lease entry today holds holder
  and expires. Proposed:

  {
    "version": 0,
    "branches": {
      "main": {
        "head": "9f2c…e41a"
      }
    },
    "lease": {
      "holder": "5b0e…77c3",
      "duration_ms": 30000,
      "renewed": "2026-11-12T12:00:30.123456789Z",
      "salt": "c41d07a9e2b35f68"
    }
  }

  - duration_ms is how long the lease lasts, in milliseconds. It has to be in
    the manifest, because an instance that watches must use the holder's
    duration and not its own setting. If the two differed, a watcher with a
    shorter setting would take the lease too early.
  - renewed is the holder's wall-clock time at the renewal. Nothing reads it for
    a decision.
  - salt is 64 random bits, new at every write of the manifest by the holder,
    written as 16 hex digits.
  - expires goes.

  A released lease still has no lease entry, and the format is still a draft at
  version 0.

  Okay?

  from the review, plus a gofmt fix)
  - `b7196a5` Record the new lease entry of the manifest in the diary (Step 8,
  author `lease-builder`)

  **Go types:**
  ```go
  type Lease struct {
          Holder   string
          Duration time.Duration
          Renewed  time.Time
          Salt     uint64
          unknown  map[string]jsontext.Value
  }
  ```
  - The writer writes the duration as `Duration.String()` gives it, and the salt
  as 16 lowercase hexadecimal digits.
  - In `leaseJSON` the three new fields are strings. `go doc` for
  `encoding/json/v2` says `time.Duration` "currently has no default
  representation and results in a SemanticError" unless `FormatDurationAsNano`
  is set, and it documents no `format:` tag. So the code checks each spelling
  itself.
  - `parseDuration` calls `time.ParseDuration`, then requires `d.String() == s`,
  then `d > 0`.

  **Limits:**
  - The duration must be above zero. There is no upper limit of our own:
  `ParseDuration` refuses anything beyond the range of `time.Duration`, and the
  largest value, `2562047h47m16.854775807s`, reads and writes back exactly.
  Tests pin both ends.
  - Fractions of a second are allowed, such as `500ms`.
  - The renewal time keeps the old expiry rules: RFC 3339, returned in UTC,
  years 0 to 9999.

  **Refusals added to `ParseManifest`, all `ErrMalformed`:**
  - **Missing fields:** no duration, no renewal time, or no salt, and each of
  them given as null. Also a lease with `expires` and no `renewed`, and the old
  form with only `holder` and `expires`.
  - **Durations:** a JSON number or an empty string, and `0s`, `0`, `-30s`,
  `+30s`, `0.5m`, `30000ms`, `30s0ms`, `1m`, `90s`, `1.50s`, `030s`, `1us`,
  `1μs` (Greek mu), ` 30s`, `30S`, `30`, `3e1s`, and the overflow
  `2562047h47m16.854775808s`.
  - **Salts:** a JSON number or an empty string, 15 or 17 digits, uppercase
  digits, a `g`, a `0x` prefix, `+`, `_`, or a space.
  - **Renewal time:** every refusal the old expiry had.
  - **Writer:** `Manifest.Marshal` now also refuses a duration of zero, `-1s`,
  or `math.MinInt64`.

  When `expires` sits next to `renewed`, it is kept as an unknown field and
  written back.

  - Nothing outside `internal/format` uses `format.Lease`.
  `internal/sim/toy_test.go` has an `Expires` on its own toy manifest type.
  - Both sample manifests changed only inside `lease`.

  below).

  clearer errors such as "has no salt".

  **Open points for you:**
  1. `docs/spec.md` line 39 still says "The lease expires at a time on the wall
  clock. The manifest records when the lease expires". `docs/todo.md` in this
  worktree also still describes the expiry. I did not edit either, as the brief
  says, so the spec now contradicts the format until it is reworded with
  Markus's agreement.
  2. `Marshal` accepts a zero `Salt` and a zero `Renewed` (year 1). The reader
  must accept both: an all-zero salt is a valid random draw, and the old rules
  accept year 1. Refusing them only in `Marshal` would mean some manifests that
  are read cannot be written again, and the fuzz test would fail. So nothing in
  the format catches a holder that forgets to draw a new salt; the lease code
  must do it. Say if you want a zero salt refused on both sides anyway.
  3. An escaped spelling such as `"1µs"` decodes to the canonical text, so it is
  accepted and then written with the raw character. The holder works the same
  way.

  One consequence of "only the spelling Go writes" that may surprise you: one
  minute must be written 1m0s, and 1m or 90s are refused, because Go itself
  writes 1m0s and 1m30s. That only matters to someone who edits a manifest by
  hand.

  value, even all zeros, is a valid random draw. That check belongs to the lease
  code, and the simulation experiment has a planted bug for exactly this.

  mutants of the store make a test fail. The workload's model alone catches 14
  of them, the ones that break a promise of S3 or change which writes land late;

  ## What I built and chose

  **`internal/store/store.go`, package `store`:**
  - `Get(ctx, key) (io.ReadCloser, Version, error)`.
  - `Create(ctx, key, content io.ReadSeeker, checksum *Checksum) (Version,
  error)`.
  - `Swap(ctx, key, from Version, content io.ReadSeeker, checksum *Checksum)
  (Version, error)`.
  - `Version` is an opaque, never-empty string. `Checksum = [32]byte`; nil means
  no checksum.
  - Four sentinels mean "not applied": `ErrNotFound` (also for a swap where no
  object is, as S3's 404), `ErrExists`, `ErrChanged`, `ErrChecksum`.
  - The package documentation says that every other error is an unknown outcome,
  including context errors. It also says:
    - The write may land after the call returned.
    - A read cannot settle it. Only a conditional write that succeeds can: a
  create rules out every create of the key, and a swap rules out swaps from the
  version it replaced, while that version does not come back.
    - A store must not turn the refusal of its own retry into one of the four
  errors.
  - Writes return the new version, so renewals need no read.

  **`internal/store/simstore`, under `simlint` with no exemption:**
  - `simstore.New(host, Options)` runs on the host of the test (the
  `*sim.Scheduler`), never on an instance's host. `Store.Client(name, sender)`
  gives each instance a client; the sender is anything with `Crashed()`, such as
  a `*sim.Instance`, or nil.
  - A task of the host carries every request, so a request outlives its caller
  and its sender.
  - A version is an HMAC-SHA256 of the content, keyed from the seed: same
  content, same version at every key, and nothing outside can compute it.
  - Conditions are checked at arrival.
  - Direct view: `Object`, `Keys`, and `History` (each write with time, client,
  request number, key, from, version, content). `Wait` waits for every request,
  including stuck ones. `Client.CutOff` and `Store.Outage` cut clients off by
  hand.
  - `Options.Name` lets two stores share a host. `AllFaults()` is the default
  with every fault on.
  - Every error a fault causes wraps `simstore.ErrFault`.
  - It panics on keys that S3 refuses or that tools may treat as another key
  (empty, over 1024 bytes, not UTF-8, `.` or `..` segments), and on a swap from
  an empty version.

  **`internal/store/storetest`:**
  - `storetest.Test(t, Harness)` runs 17 cases; `storetest.Cases()` lists them.
  - Cases report through `storetest.T` (`Helper`, `Errorf`), because they run in
  host tasks where `FailNow` must not be called.
  - A `Harness` runs one case against a new, empty store as a task of any host,
  so the real client runs it with `host.New()`.

  ## Departures from the requirements
  1. **Content is `io.ReadSeeker`, not `io.Reader` (req. 3).** S3 needs the
  length: "Amazon S3 requires the content length to be provided for all object's
  uploaded to a bucket", and SDK v2 needs `io.Seeker` to find it and hash the
  payload (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sdk-for-go/v2/developer-guide/sdk-utiliti
  es-s3.html). A file and a `bytes.Reader` are both seekers, so nothing is held
  in memory.
  2. **Faults beyond req. 11.** Both reviewers found that, as worded, the
  requirement let an error other than the caller's context settle the outcome:
  3422 writes failed with `ErrFault` over 300 seeds, and none was applied after
  its call returned. That is kinder than a real network. I added:
     - A lost or undelivered write can, by `Late`, be stuck in the network and
  land up to `LateBy` later.
     - `Hang`: the call gets no answer until its context ends.
     - `Conflict`: a swap from the right version is refused with `ErrChanged`
  while another write to its key is in flight, as S3's documented 409 does.
  3. **Wrong stores wrap a correct client** rather than being stores of their
  own (req. 15).
  4. **"Never with it off" is checked two ways (req. 16):** by the trace marker
  with every other fault on, and by the effect seen through the interface and
  the history with the faults that look the same also switched off.
  5. **The suite accepts zero winners among racing swaps** if the object is then
  unchanged, because of the 409.
  6. **With `Late` or `AfterCrash` at 0**, an abandoned or crashed write is
  never applied. A reviewer called that the kind side. I kept it: at 0.5 both
  outcomes happen, and "off" means "never applied after the caller stopped
  waiting", which is what req. 16 tests.

  ## S3 behaviour, with sources
  - **Consistency:** read-after-write is strong, updates to one key are atomic,
  and a concurrent read gets "either the old data or the new data"
  (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome.html). "Amazon
  S3 never adds partial objects"
  (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/API/API_PutObject.html).
  - **Conditional writes** (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguid
  e/conditional-writes.html):
    - `If-None-Match`: 412, and "the first write operation to finish succeeds".
    - `If-Match`: 412, "You can also receive a `409 Conflict` response in the
  case of concurrent requests", and 404 when no object is there.
    - The PutObject reference says to fetch the ETag and retry after a 409.
  - **ETag** (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/checking-obj
  ect-integrity-upload.html): it is the MD5 only for single-part plaintext or
  SSE-S3 objects, not for SSE-KMS, SSE-C, or multipart. Whether the same content
  gets the same ETag under KMS is undocumented, so the suite does not require
  it, and the sim takes the harsh side (same content, same version).
  - **Checksums** (same page): a mismatched checksum is rejected with
  `BadDigest` and nothing is stored. A `PutObject` checksum covers the full
  object; SHA-256 is only composite for multipart.
  - **Missing objects:** GET gives 404 only with `s3:ListBucket`, and 403
  without it
  (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/API/API_GetObject.html).
  - **Keys:** UTF-8 of at most 1024 bytes, and `.` or `..` segments cause
  trouble
  (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-keys.html).
  - **From our research notes, not AWS:** Tigris does not validate a checksum
  sent as a header (`docs/research/tigris-probe-results.md`), and SDK v2 retries
  a conditional PUT by itself after a reset
  (`docs/research/s3-fault-injection.md`).

  ## For the builder of the real S3 client
  - Run `storetest.Test` with a harness that gives each case a fresh, empty key
  prefix and `host.New()`.
  - **Switch off SDK retries for conditional writes**, or report any refusal of
  a retry as unknown. Otherwise a 412 on the retry becomes a false `ErrChanged`
  or `ErrExists`.
  - **Error mapping:**

  | S3 response | Maps to |
  |---|---|
  | 412 on create | `ErrExists` |
  | 412 on swap | `ErrChanged` |
  | 404 on swap | `ErrNotFound` |
  | 409 on swap | `ErrChanged` (not applied) |
  | 409 on create | unknown, or retry internally |
  | `BadDigest` | `ErrChecksum` |
  | GET 404 | `ErrNotFound` |

    Grant `s3:ListBucket` rather than treating a 403 as `ErrNotFound`.
  - Send the SHA-256 so that the store actually checks it. On Tigris that means
  a trailer checksum.
  - Never send `If-Match: ""`. Tigris treats it as create-only, and the sim
  panics on an empty version.
  - Pass ETags back verbatim.
  - The contract says the store reads content only until `Create` returns.
  net/http can read a request body after `RoundTrip` returns on cancellation (a
  reviewer's point from knowledge, not checked), so the client must make sure
  the transport is done with the body first.
  - A truncated body must give an error, never `io.EOF`.
  - Telemetry is still undecided; it belongs with this client.

  ## For the builder of the core
  - **Every store call needs a timeout.** With `Hang`, a call without one never
  returns, and the scheduler reports the run as stuck.
  - Treat every error other than the four as unknown. A write can land up to
  `LateBy` (60 s in `AllFaults`) after its call returned. A read never settles
  that; only a successful conditional write that changes the version does.
  - **Manifest content must never repeat.** Versions come from content, so a
  renewal that writes the same bytes is invisible, and a version can come back.
  - **`ErrChanged` can be a conflict while the manifest is unchanged and an
  earlier attempt is still in flight.** So after a rejected swap, "the commit
  object is not in the log" does not by itself prove "not committed". The detail
  in `docs/todo.md` about renewing without the commit handles this; the bullet
  before it reads otherwise.
  - **Using the store:** `simstore.New(scheduler, simstore.Options{Faults:
  simstore.AllFaults(), ...})`, then `st.Client(name, inst)` for each instance.
  Call `st.Wait(ctx)` before final checks, in a task. Check against
  `st.History()`.
  - A crashed instance's client sends nothing.
  - Content can be read by any task, and breaks off if its client is cut off
  while reading.

  ## Out of date in the docs (I edited none)
  - **`docs/todo.md`, "The S3 client and S3's checksums":**
    - The status says the client "comes after the simulated object store", which
  is built now.
    - The download bullet ("compares the checksum that S3 returns with the name,
  before it reads the body") cannot be carried by `store.Store.Get`, which
  neither returns the stored checksum nor takes an expected one.
    - The item should probably add that Tigris needs trailer checksums.
  - **`docs/todo.md`, "A manifest swap whose outcome is unknown":**
    - "If the first attempt did arrive, S3 rejects the retry" and "After a
  rejection, the instance reads the manifest. The transaction is committed iff
  its commit object is in the log" both ignore that a rejection can be a 409
  while the first attempt is still stuck.
    - "Once the manifest has changed, S3 rejects the old request" holds only if
  manifest bytes never repeat.
  - `docs/spec.md`, `README.md`, `AGENTS.md`: nothing I found is out of date.
  The spec's "Real S3 checks that the simulated store behaves like the real one"
  now has a concrete mechanism in `storetest`.

       96  - **The duration is in the manifest** because an instance that
            watches must use the holder's duration and not its own settin
           g. With a shorter setting of its own, it would take the lease 
           too early.
       97  - **Clocks affect only reads.** Writes are correct whatever th
           e clocks do, because S3 checks the manifest swap. The danger i
           s a holder that has lost the lease without knowing it and answ
           ers a read from its cache, which lacks the new holder's commit
           s.
       98  - **A read checks the lease against both of the holder's clock
           s.** It passes only if less than the lease's duration has pass
           ed since the holder sent its last renewal, by the wall clock a
           nd by the monotonic clock. Each clock is compared only with it
           self. Both stay because Go's monotonic clock on Linux stands s
           till while a machine is suspended, and the wall clock catches 
           that. A wall clock that is set backward is caught by the monot
           onic clock. If both fail, reads can be stale, and Markus accep
           ts that: "if the server is in such bad a state that both clock
           s fail, stale reads are probably okay too."
       99 -- **One check when the read starts is enough.** Its order rela
          -tive to the SQLite snapshot does not matter. The cache never h
          -olds another instance's commits, so a snapshot taken after a p
          -ause shows a state that was correct at the moment of the check
          -.

          +tive to the SQLite snapshot does not matter. The cache never h
          +olds another instance's commits, so a snapshot taken after a p
          +ause shows a state that was correct at the moment of the check
          +. That moment lies between the start and the end of the read, 
          +which is all that a read promises. The research note on S3's c
          +lock advises the check after the read instead; either order is
          + correct, and before is simpler.

⏺ The object store is built and up as pull request #10
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/10). I read the interface myself, and
  I'm back on main.

  What exists:

  - store.Store with three methods: read an object, create one where none is,
    and swap one from a version. Four errors mean a write was not applied. Every
    other error means the outcome is unknown and the write may still land
    later.
  - A simulated store whose faults all come from the seed: lost requests and
    responses, writes that land after their caller gave up or crashed, calls
    that hang, reads that break off, cut-off clients, and outages.
  - A conformance suite of 17 cases that the real S3 client will have to pass.
    Five deliberately wrong stores stay as tests of the suite.

  Verification: 10,000 seeds, each run twice, with no differing pair and no
  violation. The builder also changed the store in 45 small ways, and a test
  failed for every one of them.

  A finding that sharpens the design. S3 can refuse a swap from the right
  version while another write to the same key is on the way, with "409
  Conflict". For us that other write can be our own first attempt, still stuck
  in the network. So a rejected retry does not prove the first attempt arrived,
  and reading an unchanged manifest does not prove the commit failed. The rule
  we already had as a detail is what settles it: renew the lease without the
  commit, and once that swap succeeds, the stuck request is refused whenever it
  arrives.

  This also gives the salt a second job. A stuck request must never find the
  manifest's old version again, and a new salt at every write guarantees it. I
  corrected the todo item accordingly; the spec's rule stands as written.

  Two choices of the builder that I accepted:

  - Content is passed as a seekable reader, because S3 needs the length before
    the upload.
  - Three faults beyond my list: a lost write that lands late, a call that
    hangs, and the 409. Without them the simulated store would have been kinder
    than a real network.

  Waiting on CI for #9 (the manifest's lease entry) and #10. The simulation
  experiment on the new lease design is still running.

⏺ Pull request #10 is merged, so three of the four pieces are on main: the
  harness, the formats, and the object store with its simulated store. main is
  clean, and the only worktree left belongs to the running simulation
  experiment.

  Two pieces remain: the client for real S3, and the core. The core waits for
  the experiment's result. The S3 client can start now, but it needs one
  decision that I think is yours, because it sets a large dependency.

  Which library talks to S3?

  - The AWS SDK for Go, version 2. It handles request signing, checksums in
    trailers, endpoints, and the whole credential chain, including IAM roles on
    AWS. It is a large dependency. It also retries on its own, which we must
    switch off for conditional writes, and it starts goroutines that our lint
    can't see.
  - A small client of our own on net/http. We need only two operations, GET and
    PUT with a few headers, plus request signing. We'd control retries and the
    request body completely, and it could run under the simulation with a fake
    transport. But credentials would be static keys until we build more, and IAM
    roles are how servers on AWS normally authenticate.

  Either way the client sits behind the three-method interface and has to pass
  the same conformance suite, so swapping it later is cheap.

  I lean towards the AWS SDK now, mostly for the credential chain, which is
  security-sensitive code I'd rather not write ourselves. Our earlier research
  on fault injection also assumed the SDK.

  SDK, or our own client?

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The design holds. Across all three workloads at 10000 seeds and the ten
  planted-bug workloads at 10000 seeds each, no seed shows a stale read or any
  other violation. That includes wall clocks set backward or held still while
  the instance can reach the store. Neither independent reviewer found a fault
  that the design fails and that the workload neither includes nor names.

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a0f97f77ab75dfb21`
  **Worktree:**
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a0f97f77ab75dfb21`

  It starts from `origin/main` at `e116bdc` and has four commits: `5c1fa31`,
  `ba75c52`, `3aa7698` and `98ab7f2`. Nothing is pushed.

  ## The store, before and after

  The simulated store numbered its versions (`v1`, `v2`, …), so the salt could
  not have mattered. Its ETag is now the first 8 bytes of the SHA-256 of the
  content, so two writes of the same bytes have the same version.

  ## Faults kept out of the workload

  There are exactly the two you expected. The comment on `clockFaults` in
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a0f97f77ab75dfb21/int
  ernal/sim/simulation_test.go` names them:

  1. **Both clocks of one instance wrong in the same direction.** This means a
  wall clock set backward or held still, together with a stopped monotonic
  clock. So an instance whose monotonic clock stops has its wall clock moved
  only forward. Both reviewers showed the exclusion is real: allowing it gives
  stale reads with no bug planted (reviewer A: 35 of 400 seeds).
  2. **A monotonic clock that runs fast or jumps forward on the instance that
  waits to take the lease.** The harness cannot make one anyway; it can only
  hold a monotonic clock still.

  Everything else is in the workload, on connected instances:
  - Wall clocks move forward and backward, by under 2 s or up to 2 h, several
  times per instance and for good.
  - Wall clocks are held still for up to 5 min.
  - Monotonic clocks are stopped as before.
  - Partitions are now a fault of their own, and leases are 20, 30 or 45 s.

  ## Numbers at `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=10000`, on commit `3aa7698`

  | Workload | Seeds, each run twice | Differing pairs | Violations | Served at
  most, after the workload | Bound |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | One instance | 10000 | 0 | 0 | 57 s | 2 m 14 s |
  | Full | 10000 | 0 | 0 | 1 m 6.6 s | 2 m 44 s |
  | Callers that never pause | 2500 | 0 | 0 | 1 m 7.5 s | 2 m 14 s |

  In the full workload that was 97456 commits, 489 of unknown outcome, 239048
  reads, 35905 clock moves, 10000 crashes and 25029 buggify firings.

  `make test` and `make lint` pass. `go test -race -shuffle on ./internal/sim/
  ./internal/host/` passes in 668 s on a loaded machine.

  ## Planted bugs

  Each was run over 10000 seeds. "Control" is the same workload without the
  fault the bug needs. "Without the bug" is the bug's workload with the toy
  unchanged, which is new: the full workload rarely reaches the salt or the
  two-clock check (`keepSalt` planted in it was found by 0 of 2500 seeds), so
  these runs are where those rules are really tested.

  | Bug | Found | Control | Without the bug |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Renewal without a new salt (`keepSalt`; needs a wall clock held still) | 813
  | 0 | 0 |
  | Watcher waits for its own duration (`ownDuration`; leases of 10 and 90 s) |
  1367 | 0 | 0 |
  | Swap against the version read at the end of the wait (`lateVersion`) | 6372
  | 0 | 0 |
  | Holder counts its lease from the answer (`leaseFromAnswer`; needs late
  answers, which are new) | 696 | 0 | 0 |
  | Holder checks the wall clock only (`wallOnly`, adapted) | 2355 | 0 | 0 |
  | Holder checks the monotonic clock only (`monotonicOnly`, adapted) | 2090 | 0
  | 0 |
  | Renewal outside the lock | 1534 | no control | 0 |
  | Cache before the manifest | 1666 | no control | 0 |
  | Takeover only after a release | 9917 | 0 | 0 |
  | Ignore a failure of the cache | 2001 | 0 | 0 |

  - The controls of `ownDuration` (one duration) and `lateVersion` (one
  instance) cannot fail by construction, and their comments say so. The other
  controls draw the same faults at the same times and apply none of them.
  - No old bug was removed. None had lost its meaning: `takeoverAfterRelease`
  now means an instance that takes the lease only when there is no lease entry.
  - The lease bugs needed their own workloads to be found by many seeds: 30
  transactions per caller, faults on every instance, and some tuning per bug.
  The reason is structural. The old holder learns it lost the lease at its next
  write, which is usually a commit seconds later, so the stale window is short.

  ## Liveness

  `workload.liveness` is 2 × (longest lease + 2 s margin) + 70 s. The 70 s is
  the longest delay of one store operation.

  The first version of the bound was broken: reviewers found seed 354 at 1 m 34
  s against a 94 s bound. The store now stops injecting faults when the workload
  ends, so only an operation already in flight can still be delayed.

  ## Change to the harness

  `sim.Instance.StopWall` and `StartWall`, with a test, about 25 lines in
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a0f97f77ab75dfb21/int
  ernal/sim/sim.go`.

  It is needed because a renewal without a new salt writes the same bytes only
  if the holder's wall clock reads the same at two writes seconds apart.
  `ShiftWall` cannot do that, because simulated time moves between any two
  steps.

  ## What the self-review changed

  The `fabrik:code-review` skill sent two reviewers over the diff. They agreed
  on two serious findings, which are fixed:
  - **The free-running test of callers that never pause had become vacuous.**
  `acquire` read the manifest before taking the writer lock, so callers finished
  before the lease was taken and the planted data race was no longer reported.
  `acquire` now holds its locks except during the wait, and `TestFreeRunning`
  requires commits and reads.
  - **The liveness bound was wrong**, as above.

  Other fixes from the review:
  - The toy no longer waits twice on an unchanged version.
  - `Close` releases a lease that an acquire of unknown outcome may have taken.
  This came from seed 1738: a lease was left held after every instance closed,
  which is not a stale read.
  - Several comments were corrected.

  The 10000-seed run then caught a bug I had introduced: a double release of the
  swap lock when an instance crashes inside `acquire`. It is fixed in
  `3aa7698`.

  Everything is recorded, with commands and errors, in
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a0f97f77ab75dfb21/doc
  s/diary/2026-11-12-lease-without-clock-comparison.md`, Steps 1 to 3.

  ## Gaps the simulation leaves

  - **The margin is untested.** Reviewer A shrank it to 1 ms, and had the
  watcher wait the duration minus 1 s: 0 failures. The harness's clocks run at
  the true rate and its latency is always positive, so the margin only matters
  for a fast clock, which the harness cannot make.
  - **Two other mechanisms fail nothing when removed:** taking the lease at once
  after a release (only liveness depends on it), and the release in `Close`
  after an unknown acquire.
  - **Late delivery is not modelled.** The harness never applies a request after
  its sender saw it fail. By argument the lease is safe there, because an
  instance counts its lease only from a swap it knows happened, but the
  simulation does not show it.

  ## For a builder of the real core

  - **Count the lease from when the first attempt is sent.** Adopt an unknown
  renewal only once the manifest shows its exact bytes, using the time it was
  sent.
  - **Take a new salt at every write by the holder**, commits included. With a
  wall clock that runs, the wall-clock field already makes the bytes new; the
  salt is what protects against a wall clock that stands still.
  - **Wait on the duration written in the manifest**, from when the read's
  answer arrived. Swap against the version from before the wait, and wait again
  only if the version changed.
  - **A read of "same ETag, so the swap did not happen" is unsound for a swap
  from a released manifest.** Two releases at the same commit have the same
  bytes. It is harmless for the lease, but `resolveSwap` relies on that
  inference.
  - **Remember that Linux `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` stops during suspend.** After a
  suspend, the wall-clock check is what catches the holder, which is exactly the
  "one clock wrong" case.
  - **The core's simulation needs the same "without the bug" runs of targeted
  workloads.** A clean run of the full workload alone says almost nothing about
  the salt.

  `internal/sim/README.md` does not exist in this branch. I did not touch
  `docs/spec.md`, `docs/todo.md`, `README.md` or `AGENTS.md`.

⏺ Update(.claude/worktrees/agent-a0f97f77ab75dfb21/internal/sim/README.md)
  ⎿  Added 3 lines, removed 1 line
      66  - **Three workloads run it.** The first has one instance. The s
          econd has everything: two instances that compete for the lease,
           clocks that move, a crash and a restart, buggify, and faults o
          f the store. The third has callers that never pause. They are i
          n `simulation_test.go`, and the doc comment of `workload.run` l
          ists the invariants.
      67  - **The checks compare data against a model, not counters.** Ea
          ch commit is recorded with its writes before `Commit`, and then
           marked acknowledged, failed, or unknown. Each read is compared
           with the value that the log has at the read's commit.
      68  - **A restart finds a copy of the files.** A crash copies the c
          ache and its write-ahead log to a new path at the moment of the
           crash, and a new instance starts on the copy.
      69 -- **The toy's lease is the design of 2026-11-11,** with an expi
         -ry on the holder's wall clock. Its clock faults are limited to 
         -what that design claimed to survive: at most one wrong clock pe
         -r instance at a time, and a wall clock set backward only while 
         -the instance is cut off from the store. Without the second limi
         -t, the review of the build found stale reads in 3 of 100 seeds.
         - That finding changed the lease's design; see [The lease and it
         -s clocks](../../docs/todo.md#the-lease-and-its-clocks).        
      69 +- **The toy's lease is the design of 2026-11-12,** which compar
         +es no clocks between instances; see [The lease and its clocks](
         +../../docs/todo.md#the-lease-and-its-clocks). The toy first had
         + the design of 2026-11-11, with an expiry on the holder's wall 
         +clock. The review of the build found stale reads with it in 3 o
         +f 100 seeds, as soon as a holder whose wall clock was set backw
         +ard could still reach the store. That finding changed the lease
         +'s design, and the toy was then changed to test the new one (`d
         +ocs/diary/2026-11-12-lease-without-clock-comparison.md`).      
      70 +- **Two clock faults stay out of the workload,** because the de
         +sign does not claim to survive them: both clocks of one instanc
         +e wrong in the same direction, and a monotonic clock that runs 
         +fast on the instance that waits to take the lease. The comment 
         +on `clockFaults` in `simulation_test.go` says why. Every other 
         +move of a clock is in the workload, on instances that can reach
         + the store.

         + ETag does, so that a renewal that writes the same bytes is inv
         +isible. With numbered versions, the salt in the manifest could 
         +not have been tested.

⏺ The lease design held in simulation, and I've merged main into its branch and
  updated the harness README. The harness tests take 132 seconds here, so I'm
  timing CI under the race detector next. · summary

  Created PR #11, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The new lease design holds in simulation. The experiment is up as pull request
  #11 (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/11), with CI running.

  What was tested. The toy library now takes the lease by watching the manifest
  stand still, with the duration and the salt in the manifest. The workaround
  for the old design is gone: wall clocks move forward and backward, by up to
  two hours, and stand still, on instances that can still reach the store.

  The result over 10,000 seeds per workload, each run twice: no read missed an
  acknowledged commit, and no pair of traces differed. Two independent reviewers
  looked for a fault that breaks the design and that the workload neither
  includes nor names, and found none.

  What the design does not survive, exactly the two cases we expected:

  - Both clocks of one instance wrong in the same direction. Allowing it gave
    stale reads in 35 of 400 seeds, so the exclusion is real. This is the case
    you accepted on Wednesday.
  - A monotonic clock that runs fast on the instance that is waiting to take the
    lease. The harness can't produce this, so it is argued and not shown.

  The salt earns its place. A renewal without a new salt was found by 813 seeds.
  It needs a wall clock that stands still, because with a running clock the
  renewal time already makes the bytes new. The salt is what protects when the
  clock doesn't move.

  Planted bugs: four new ones for this design and the six existing ones, found
  by between 696 and 9,917 seeds each, and by none without the fault they need.

  One lesson for the core's tests: planted in the big everything-at-once
  workload, the missing salt was found by 0 of 2,500 seeds. Each lease rule
  needed a workload aimed at it, so a clean run of the big workload alone says
  little.

  One cost I'm watching: the harness's tests went from about 50 to 132 seconds
  here. If CI gets too slow under the race detector, I'll have the default seed
  count for the aimed workloads lowered.

  Still waiting on you: the AWS SDK or a small client of our own for real S3.

⏺ CI failed on pull request #11, for the reason I was watching: the planted-bug
  test of the harness alone ran past Go's 10-minute test limit, both under the
  race detector and under the address sanitizer. Nothing is wrong with the
  result of the experiment; the default run is just too large for CI.

  A builder is fixing it on the same branch. It measures first, then cuts the
  default run, by fewer seeds for the aimed workloads, by running them in
  parallel, or both. The conditions:

  - Every planted bug is still found in the default run, and the test still
    fails if one isn't. Runs are deterministic per seed, so this can be made
    exact.
  - Every seed that runs is still run twice.
  - The full 10,000-seed run stays available behind the existing variable, with
    the exact command in the README.

  I'll merge #11 when CI is green.

  Still waiting on you: the AWS SDK or a small client of our own for real S3.

  on this machine, down from 143 s and 388 s. Every planted bug is still found
  in every mode, and nothing from your point 3 is lost. Two things are run less
