  The load was never low. Other sessions kept this 10-core Mac at a load of 11
  to 21 all the time, so none of these numbers is from an unloaded machine. The
  CI time is an estimate; I could not run CI because I did not push.

  Branch `worktree-agent-a72db84b19a8263f9`, worktree
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a72db84b19a8263f9`.
  Two commits on top of `origin/lease-toy` (`2219a32`):
  - `0658796`: the first cut.
  - `43ae420`: the fixes from the review, plus diary Step 4.

  Not pushed. `sim.go`, `free.go` and `internal/host` are unchanged.

  ## Before
  Measured on `2219a32` with `go test -count=1 -json`.

  | | Plain (load 11.6–14.0) | `-race` (load 12.6) |
  |---|---|---|
  | Package | 143 s | 388 s |
  | `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` | 107 s | 320 s |
  | Each of the six bugs on `competing` workloads | 13.6–15.9 s | 41–48 s |
  | The four bugs on `singleWorkload` | 2.7–6.9 s | 7.6–20.2 s |
  | `TestSimulation` | 11.6 s | 33 s |
  | `TestSynctestWithCgo`, `TestSchedulerWithSQLiteLocks`, `TestFreeRunning` |
  5.9 / 3.5 / 2.2 s | 6.5 / 7.0 / 5.5 s |

  - The time went into the planted bugs. Each ran 200 seeds of three workloads:
  with the bug, the control, and without the bug.
  - The race detector cost about 3x.
  - `-race` does not change the traces. The hash of all traces for each
  workload, and the seeds that found each bug, were identical with and without
  it.

  ## After
  Final code.

  | | Plain | `-race` |
  |---|---|---|
  | Package, load 10.9–16.1 | 57.8 s | 87.0 s |
  | `GOMAXPROCS=4`, as a stand-in for CI's 4 cores, load 14.2 | | 100 s |
  | Longest subtest | 43.8 s | 56.9 s |

  Both targets are met (under 1 minute plain, under 3 minutes with `-race`). The
  plain run is close to its target at this load. I expect about 2 minutes for
  this package in the CI race job.

  ## What I chose

  1. **The bug's own workload stops early.** It runs from seed 0 until four
  seeds have found the bug, up to 200 seeds, and the test fails if none does.
  2. **The control and the run without the bug have a fixed count.** Each runs
  100 seeds by default.
  3. **Parallel subtests.** `TestSimulation`, `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` and
  their subtests call `t.Parallel()`.

  When `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS` is set, all three workloads of a bug run that many seeds
  and the finds are counted. That is the same work as before, so the full run
  is still full size.

  I did not add a shorter default for `-race` or `-asan`. CI runs this package
  only under those two, so a shorter default there would have meant CI no longer
  proves the planted bugs. Every mode now runs the same seeds.

  Why parallel is safe:
  - The package-level variables are error values, constants and workloads that
  are only read.
  - Each run has its own scheduler, store and `t.TempDir()`.
  - SQLite is built with `SQLITE_THREADSAFE 1` and without shared cache.

  I checked it rather than assumed it:
  - Run in parallel, the three hashes of all `TestSimulation` traces were
  identical to serial.
  - Both reviewers got identical hashes with `-parallel 1`.
  - Go runs top-level parallel tests only after all sequential tests have
  finished. So `TestFreeRunning`, `TestPlantedDataRace` and the real-time cgo
  tests still run alone.

  ## The review changed the first cut
  The first cut gave each bug a seed count sized from how often 10000 seeds
  found it, and used that count for all three of its workloads. Both reviewers
  found two real problems with it:
  - **Controls and clean runs were cut by an unrelated measure.** Clean runs
  went from 2000 to 490 and control runs from 1600 to 400. The easiest bugs got
  the fewest clean seeds.
  - **The test could fail by chance.** The "1 in 1000" miss chance was per bug.
  Any change that changes the traces reshuffles all ten bugs at once, so the
  test would fail about once in 240 such changes.

  The early stop fixes both. It also removes ten hand-computed numbers, and a
  gap where the must-find check could be skipped without any message for a
  workload with `think == 0`.

  ## What it trades away
  These are all smaller in the default run only:
  - **Symptom check:** it covers the first four finds of each bug, not every
  find in 200 seeds.
  - **Controls and runs without the bug:** 100 seeds each instead of 200.
  - **Find rate per bug:** counted only when `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS` is set.
  - **Speed lines in `TestSimulation`:** taken while other subtests run. A
  comment now says `TestSimulationSpeed` is the measure of speed.

  ## How I made sure each bug is found
  The runs are deterministic, so these finds are facts, not probabilities. They
  were the same with and without `-race`, and they are the same first seeds as
  in the run before the change.

  | Planted bug | Finds |
  |---|---|
  | Renewal outside the lock | 4 of 40 seeds |
  | Cache before the manifest | 4 of 29 |
  | Wall clock only | 4 of 24 |
  | Monotonic clock only | 4 of 30 |
  | No new salt | 4 of 71 |
  | Waits for its own duration | 4 of 29 |
  | Swap against the version after the wait | 4 of 6 |
  | Lease counted from the answer | 4 of 33 |
  | Takeover only after a release | 4 of 4 |
  | Ignores a failure of the cache | 4 of 10 |

  - Every control and every run without the bug found 0 of 100.
  - The `asan` run passes with this code, and the must-find check is part of it,
  so every bug is found there too.
  - If a later change reshuffles which seeds find a bug, the chance that some
  bug goes unfound in 200 seeds is under one in a million for the whole test.
  That uses the shares from the 10000-seed table.

  ## The full-size run
  This is now in `internal/sim/README.md`, section "Tests":
  `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=10000 go test -timeout 4h -run
  '^TestSimulation(_plantedBugs)?$' ./internal/sim/`

  - 1000 seeds took 8 min 20 s (load 14–16), with every bug found and every
  control clean.
  - The time grows with the seed count, so 10000 seeds should take about 83
  minutes here. `4h` leaves room for a slower machine.
  - I did not run 10000 seeds.

  ## Verification
  All on the final code:
  - `make test` passes in 59.5 s (`internal/sim` 57.4 s).
  - `make lint` exits 0.
  - `go test -race -shuffle on ./internal/sim/ ./internal/host/` passes in 84.4
  s.
  - `S4QL_SIM_PLANT_RACE=free go test -race -run 'TestPlantedDataRace$'
  ./internal/sim/` still reports `WARNING: DATA RACE`. The `scheduler` mode
  still passes.
  - `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=20` runs every bug for all 20 seeds and passes.
  - `make test-asan FUZZTIME=5s` in Docker (Linux, 10 CPUs, 4 GB) passes in 3
  min 26 s, with `internal/sim` at 149 s.

⏺ CI on pull request #11 is almost there. The test job now passes in 5 minutes
  41 seconds, where it timed out at 10 before. The address-sanitizer job failed
  again, but for a different reason:

  - What failed: one of the canary tests for testing/synctest with cgo. One
    connection holds SQLite's write lock while it does slow work, and a second
    waits for it with a 5-second busy timeout. On the loaded CI machine under
    the sanitizer, the slow work took longer than 5 seconds, so the waiter gave
    up.
  - What it means: the test depends on how fast the machine is, which it
    shouldn't. It is not a fault in the lease design or in the harness.

  A builder is making that test independent of machine speed and checking the
  other real-clock tests for the same weakness. I also reran the failed job to
  see whether it passes on a second try.

✻ Baked for 6m 22s · done Thursday 7:06 PM

※ recap: We're building s4ql's storage core: harness, formats, and object store are merged, and pull request #11, which tests the new lease design in simulation, is waiting on a fix for one timing-fragile test. Next, you choose: AWS SDK or our own S3 client.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The failing subtest now passes regardless of machine speed, and the `asan`
  job's command, run with 2 CPUs, passes in a Linux container.

  **Branch and commit**
  - Branch: `worktree-agent-a41987dbd9aef5c91`
  - Worktree:
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a41987dbd9aef5c91`
  - Commit: `992fd8d` "Make the tests of `internal/sim` that wait in real time
  independent of the speed of the machine". It sits on top of `43ae420`, the tip
  of `origin/lease-toy`. Not pushed, no PR.

  **Cause**
  In the subtest, the waiter had a 5000 ms busy timeout and the holder counted
  to 3,000,000 in C. SQLite's busy wait runs in real time, so the test passed
  only if the holder's work took under 5 s. It takes about 0.4 s on this machine
  and took more than 5 s on the loaded sanitizer runner.

  **Changes (test code only)**
  - `internal/sim/cgo_test.go`, the failing subtest:
    - The waiter's busy timeout is now 60,000 ms, with a comment saying why.
    - The holder's work is 1,000,000 instead of 3,000,000. That still leaves it
  about 0.13 s in C, so the waiter really waits. 60 s is about 450 times the
  reduced work.
  - `internal/sim/cgo_test.go`, "should not give SQLITE_BUSY to one writer among
  parallel readers with a busy timeout": the busy timeout goes from 1000 to
  60,000 ms. It asserts no `SQLITE_BUSY` on `begin immediate`, which held only
  if no lock holder was delayed by more than 1 s of real time.
  - `internal/sim/simulation_test.go`: `busyTimeout: 1000` becomes `60_000` in
  `singleWorkload`, `fullWorkload` and `hammerWorkload`. Under the scheduler a
  task never waits for a lock, so nothing changes there. In `TestFreeRunning`,
  with the scheduler off, the toy's connections can wait for each other on real
  time.
  - `internal/sim/sim_test.go`, `TestScheduler_Sleep`: `time.Since(realStart) <
  time.Minute` becomes `< time.Hour`. The test shows that 6 hours of simulated
  sleep takes no real time, and the first sleeper alone sleeps 1 hour, so the
  bound still tells the two apart.
  - `docs/diary/2026-11-12-lease-without-clock-comparison.md`: new Step 5 by
  `canary-builder`, with the failure quoted and the audit.

  **Control**
  With the waiter's busy timeout set to 0, the subtest failed 8 of 8 runs with
  `database is locked`, using the reduced 1,000,000 work. The holder is still
  working in C when the waiter asks, so the test is not empty. I put 60,000 back
  afterwards.

  **Audit: looked at and left alone**
  - `TestSynctestWithCgo`, the other subtests:
    - The first two depend only on `synctest.Wait` and fake time.
    - The two with a 1500 ms busy timeout fail because the lock's holder is
  durably blocked or asleep on fake time. They assert only a lower bound of real
  time (`>= 1500ms`), which a slower machine only helps.
    - The no-busy-timeout case and the deadlock case use a timeout of 0.
  - `timers_test.go` (`TestSynctestTimerOrder`) and `free_test.go`: fake time
  only.
  - The scheduler tests in `sim_test.go`, and the toy and workload tests in
  `toy_test.go` and `simulation_test.go`: they run on simulated or fake time.
  - The `elapsed` measures in `TestSimulation`, `TestSimulationSpeed`,
  `TestFreeRunning` and the SQLite lock test only log. I left them as asked.
  - `internal/host/host_test.go`: `time.Since(now) < time.Minute` checks a
  wall-clock reading taken a moment before. That is a ratio of about a million
  to one, so I left it.

  One trade-off: a waiter that really never gets the lock now takes up to 60 s
  to fail instead of 5 s or 1 s. I judged that fine for tests that must not fail
  by chance.

  **Verification**
  - `go test -count=1 ./internal/sim/` passes (57 s).

⏺ The core builder is running, in its own worktree, on Fable because this is the
  hardest piece. Pull request #11 is merged, and main holds everything else.

  What it builds: the lease, commits, startup with catch-up and replay,
  resolving unknown outcomes, and the stop on local failure, all on the
  simulated store. The toy library is its template. The simulation gets a model
  that checks every read against the log, the toy's ten planted bugs carried
  over, and new ones for what only the real code has.

  Choices I made for it that touch what you care about. Say if you disagree with
  any:

  - Keys in S3: <prefix>/manifest.json and <prefix>/commits/<name>, with the
    name as 64 hex digits.
  - A reserved table-name prefix. The core keeps its own tables in the cache,
    for example the recorded position, under names starting with s4ql_. So s4ql
    refuses to create a table whose name starts with s4ql_, as SQLite does with
    sqlite_. This is one more restriction for the roadmap.
  - What can't be read stops the instance. If the log holds a commit object that
    needs a newer version of s4ql, or one that is corrupt, the instance
    releases the lease, so that a newer version can serve, and stops for good
    with a cause that says which.
  - Telemetry: one structured event per unit of work, such as a commit, a lease
    change, a catch-up, or a stop, through a slog logger that the application
    passes in. No OpenTelemetry dependency, traces, or metrics yet; I'd decide
    those with the public driver.

  The toy stays in the harness for now. Removing it is a follow-up once the
  core's simulation is shown to cover it.

  Still waiting on you: the AWS SDK or a small client of our own for real S3.
  That piece can run alongside the core.

  The storage core is built, reviewed, and committed, and nothing is pushed.
  Three things need you: two open design questions, one wrong sentence in the
  todo, and two verification gaps (planted bugs counted over 1000 seeds, not
  10000; the last mutation run was killed by the system and not restarted).

  - **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a0ea7c79436181a4c`
  - **Worktree:**
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a0ea7c79436181a4c`
  - **Last commit:** `8ff0a57`, 17 commits on top of `a226a44`, tree clean.
  - **Where to read:** `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-
  a0ea7c79436181a4c/internal/core/README.md` and
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a0ea7c79436181a4c/doc
  s/diary/2026-11-12-storage-core.md` (Steps 1 to 6, as `core-builder`).

  ## Design questions for you

  1. **A manifest without a lease entry repeats its bytes.** Whenever the head
  is the same, such a manifest has the same bytes and so the same version.
     - **Effect:** a swap that takes a free lease and is stuck in the network
  can be applied after a later release, also after a clean `Close`.
     - **Evidence:** both reviewers reproduced it by reasoning. I have no
  failing seed, because the end-of-run lease check allows a holder whose last
  write was applied after it closed.
     - **Cost:** it adds no commit, so safety holds; the cost is one wait for a
  lease.
     - **Options:** a salt outside the lease entry, which changes the manifest
  format, or accept it and say so in the spec.
     - **Consequence for the todo:** the item "The manifest's bytes never
  repeat" is false as written.
  2. **The manifest has no guard against damaged bytes that still parse.** The
  swap that takes the lease would write such a manifest back. The store's
  contract promises unchanged content, and the design has no checksum for the
  manifest. Options: a checksum inside the manifest, or rely on the store's
  contract and say so.
  3. **The todo's sentence on the order of the lease check and the read snapshot
  is wrong.** It says the order does not matter. Checking only after the
  snapshot is unsound, so the code checks before.

  ## What was built

  Package `internal/core`, in plain blocking Go with two locks and one
  goroutine, and `simlint` passes with no exemption.

  - **Interface:** `Open`, `Wait`, `Close`, `Done`/`Err`, `Read`, and write
  transactions (`CreateTable`, `DropTable`, `Upsert`, `Delete`, `Annotate`,
  `Get`, `Scan`, `Commit`, `Rollback`).
  - **Commit:** in the spec's order, which is spool, upload create-if-absent
  with checksum, manifest swap, cache commit. `Commit` returns nil only when the
  commit is known to be in the log.
  - **Lease:** as agreed on 2026-11-12, at 30 s with renewal after 20 s, and the
  window counted from the first attempt's send.
  - **Unknown outcomes ("flights"):** every holder swap is sent from one
  version, unanswered swaps are kept, at most one is ever applied, and a read of
  the manifest settles them. Commits and renewals use the same mechanism.
  - **Catch-up and replay:** the walk goes back through the log verifying each
  object and applies oldest first. Replay is strict, one SQLite transaction per
  commit object.
  - **Large transactions:** rows go through temp tables and an unlinked spool
  file, never through memory.
  - **Other packages touched:** `internal/sqlite` gained `BindText`, `BindNull`,
  `ColumnText`, and `Reset`; `internal/store/simstore` gained `Store.Calm`.

  ## Choices and departures

  These are the ones I think you should confirm, because they touch what a
  caller sees:

  - **A transaction without changes writes no commit object.** Its annotations
  are dropped, and it checks the lease by the clocks only.
  - **`Commit` has one more "outcome unknown" case than the spec's "only if the
  caller gives up first":** the lease was lost and the log cannot be read to
  find out.
  - **`Commit` keeps finding out after a stop** when a swap may have been
  applied, and wraps only `ErrOutcomeUnknown`.
  - **New stop reasons:** `ErrLogBroken` for a missing or non-fitting commit
  object, and `ErrCacheVersion` for a cache with tables but no `s4ql_cache` row.
  - **New limits:** primary key at most 250 columns, table at most 2000 columns,
  lease at most 1 year.
  - **SQLite `INTERNAL` is not a local failure.** I followed the todo's list
  exactly.

  The rest are implementation choices:

  - The watcher's margin is max(2 s, duration/10), and it re-reads the manifest
  every 5 s.
  - `Begin` checks the clocks, and `Commit` does not.
  - A new holder drops the old lease's unknown fields.
  - `Close` retries the release for up to one lease duration.
  - Catch-up downloads an object a second time on a name mismatch before
  stopping.
  - Replay holds one decoded batch, about 20 MB.
  - Waiting uses cancelled contexts, with no change to `host`.
  - Planted bugs are switched through an unexported `Options.bugs`.

⏺ The storage core is built and up as pull request #12
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/12). I read its README and the commit
  path myself, and the code follows the spec's order step by step. CI is
  running.

  What exists in internal/core: the lease, write and read transactions, commits
  through the manifest swap, catch-up and replay, resolving unknown outcomes,
  and the stop on local failure.

  Verification:

  - Three workloads, 10,000 seeds each, every seed twice: no violation of the
    model and no differing pair. The run with everything held 110,225 commits,
    473 of unknown outcome, 38,763 lease takeovers, and 10,000 crashes with
    restart.
  - Nineteen planted bugs in the real code: each is found, and no control finds
    one. They were counted over 1,000 seeds each, not 10,000, which would take
    about five hours here.
  - A transaction of 4,000,000 rows gave a 157 MB commit object while the heap
    grew by 3.4 MB.
  - One gap: the last mutation run was killed by the system for low memory after
    64 of 144 mutants. A builder is finishing it now, one mutant at a time.

  The build corrected the todo. I had written that the lease check before a read
  can come before or after the read's snapshot. After is unsound: an instance
  could take its snapshot as a spare, then take the lease and catch up, and a
  check that came only afterwards would pass on stale data. The code checks
  before, and I'll fix the todo.

  Two questions for you.

  1. Move the salt to the top level of the manifest? A manifest without a lease
  entry has the same bytes whenever the head is the same, so its version can
  come back. A stuck request to take a free lease can then land after a later
  clean release. It can't add a commit, so the data is safe. The cost is that
  the manifest names a holder that isn't running, and the next instance waits
  one lease duration. With the salt at the top level and new at every write,
  including a release, the rule "the manifest's bytes never repeat" holds
  without exception. I recommend it.

  2. One more case of "outcome unknown" in the spec. The spec says Commit
  returns it "only if the caller gives up first". The build found a second case:
  the lease was lost, so the commit has to be looked for in the log, and the
  log holds a commit object that can never be read. Nothing can tell then. I'd
  add that case to the bullet; I'll propose wording once you agree in principle.

  Choices of the builder that a caller would notice, which I accepted:

  - A transaction that changed nothing writes no commit object. It commits if
    the lease is valid by the clocks, as a read does.
  - Limits: at most 250 columns in a primary key, where SQLite allows 2,000, and
    a lease of at most one year.
  - The core checks value types itself, because SQLite's strict tables quietly
    convert the text '1' to an integer.

  Also still open: the AWS SDK or our own client for real S3.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Branch `worktree-agent-a323fc251db2ed494`, worktree
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a323fc251db2ed494`.
  It is three commits on `8ff0a57`, not pushed:
  - `2210558` the leak fixes and the checks
  - `873b689` the time cut
  - `ffbc756` the diary, Step 7

  **Result:** the leak that CI saw came from a test, not from the core. While
  fixing it I found two more: one in the simulation harness that also leaks C
  memory, and a real bug in the core. All three are fixed. The core under
  `-asan` dropped from 240 s to 126 s, and every check passes.

  ## The leak CI saw: a test

  - **Reproduced:** I built with `go test -c -asan` in `golang:1.27.3` with
  `--cpus 4`, and set
  `ASAN_OPTIONS=symbolize=1:external_symbolizer_path=/usr/bin/addr2line`. That
  gives symbolized stacks, because `go test -c` keeps the debug info that `go
  test` strips. The stacks are all SQLite's: `sqlite3_open_v2` (12 objects),
  `pcache1AllocPage` under `sqlite3BtreeBeginTrans`, and prepared statements.
  - **Two traps hid it:**
    - Go runs the leak check only when the test binary exits 0 (`os_beforeExit`:
  `exitCode == 0 && asanenabled`). In the container two tests failed because it
  runs as root, so the first runs showed no leak at all.
    - The report depends on when the Go collector ran. Each test alone showed
  nothing until I ran it with `GOGC=1`.
  - **Pinned down:** the scenario tests together leaked `907136 bytes in 748
  allocations`, the same 748 as CI. `TestLease_clocks` alone with `GOGC=1`
  leaked `226784 bytes in 187`, exactly a quarter. The simulations,
  `TestFreeRunning` and `TestTx_Commit_large` leaked nothing.
  - **Cause:** the helper `clocked` in `lease_test.go` cuts the store off for an
  hour, then calls `DB.Close` with one simulated second. `Close` spends that
  second on releasing the lease, cannot take the writer lock, and leaves the
  cache open. Its doc comment allows exactly that. The logged error was `close:
  release of the lease: context deadline exceeded ... close: context deadline
  exceeded`. Four calls of 3 connections each give the 12. The test threw the
  error away.
  - **Fix:** `Close` gets five simulated minutes, and the test checks that it
  finished in time.

  ## How it is caught without the sanitizer

  SQLite deletes the write-ahead log when the last connection to a database
  closes. `leftOpen` in `core_test.go` lists the caches whose log is still
  there.
  - Scenario tests get their directory from `cacheDir`, which fails the test at
  its end.
  - `simulate` and `free` record a leftover cache as a violation of the run, so
  the failure names its seed.
  - Before the fix, plain `go test` failed exactly the three subtests of
  `TestLease_clocks`.
  - It misses a connection that never read the cache, which keeps no log. The
  README says so.

  ## What the self-review found

  Two reviewers on `fable` both found these two:

  1. **The harness, real C memory:** a crashed instance with a read in flight
  kept its read connection open. `closeCache` closed only the idle connections
  and the writer. The default seeds never hit it, but seeds 470, 946 and 1254 of
  2000 of the full workload did, and so did seed 22 of `takeoverAfterRelease`.
  Under `-asan` that seed gives a LeakSanitizer report, so CI would have failed
  again whenever a default seed crashed an instance mid-read.
     - Fix: `DB` keeps all its read connections in `readers`, and `closeCache`
  closes them all. With the old code, `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=23` on that bug fails with
  `seed 22 found something without the bug: [a connection to the cache a.db is
  still open after the run]`.
  2. **The core:** a scan ended by a panic or `runtime.Goexit` left its
  statement stepped and unfinalized (`conn.scan` in `cache.go` had no deferred
  call).
     - On a read, the connection went back to the pool holding its old snapshot,
  so a later read served state from before an acknowledged commit.
     - On a write, the next commit failed with `database table is locked (code
  6)`.
     - `Close` then could not close the connection.
     - `TestScan` in `cache_test.go` failed before the fix with `Expected
  "t1(k*0): 1 | 2", but got "t1(k*0): 1"` and with that commit error. A deferred
  call now gives the statement back.

  The other paths you named are clean, and both reviewers agree: `Open`'s error
  returns, `Close` after a stop, rollback, catch-up and replay, and a read that
  ends with an error. `conn.query` and `conn.count` only call back into the
  core's own code, which never waits.

  ## Root in Docker

  Two `chmod`-based subtests of `TestDB_stop` fail as root, and one of them also
  hangs for 60 s. So `make test-asan` on macOS failed and hid every leak
  through the exit code. A helper, `readOnly`, now skips them where the
  directory still accepts a file, and restores the mode in a cleanup. CI's
  runner is not root, so they still run there.

  ## Time

  Under `-asan` the time went to the planted bugs (133 s; controls and clean
  runs were about 700 of 1090 runs) and to `TestTx_Commit_large` (67 s,
  sequential). I cut three things:
  - Each planted bug's control and its run without the bug go from 20 seeds to
  10. The run with the bug still goes until four seeds find it, reruns are kept,
  and `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS` still runs everything.
  - `TestFreeRunning` runs in parallel with the simulations.
  - Under `-asan`, `TestTx_Commit_large` uses a tenth of the rows and skips the
  heap check, as it already did under `-race`. CI's test job uses `-race`, so
  the heap check would then run nowhere in CI. I added a step to the `asan` job
  in `ci.yml` that runs `go test -run '^TestTx_Commit_large$' ./internal/core/`
  without either. That is the one change outside `internal/core`.

  Every bug was still found by the same four seeds, and every control and clean
  run found nothing. Load on the 10-core Mac stayed between 13 and 19
  throughout.

  | `internal/core` | Before | After |
  |---|---|---|
  | `-asan`, `--cpus 4`, package alone | 240 s (300 s with the root hang) | 126
  s |
  | `-asan`, `--cpus 4`, inside the whole-repo run | 265 s | 158 s (`make
  test-asan FUZZTIME=5s`) |
  | `go test -race`, this machine | 136 s | 98 s |
  | `GOMAXPROCS=4 go test -race` | 164 s (measured with only the seed cut) | 133
  s |
  | plain `go test` | 55 s | 46 s |

  CI's earlier 585 s was about 2.2 times this container's 265 s. At that ratio
  the core should take about 350 s in CI, which is an estimate.

  ## Verification

  - `make test` and `make lint` pass.
  - `make test-asan FUZZTIME=5s` in a copy of the Docker command with `--cpus 4`
  passes with no leak report, in about 6 minutes for the whole repository.
  - `go test -race -shuffle on ./internal/core/` passes in 98 s.
  - Beyond the defaults: 2000 seeds of the full workload, each run twice, pass
  (224 s). `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=200` on every planted bug, control and clean run
  passes (355 s).

  ## For you

  - **A design question:** `DB.Close` spends its context on releasing the lease
  first. With a short deadline and an unreachable store, the caller gets an
  error and an open cache, again on every retry. That is the trap the test fell
  into. Two options: close the cache at once when no transaction holds a lock,
  or give the release only part of the context. I left the behaviour as
  documented.
  - **Trade-offs to accept or reverse:**
    - Controls and clean runs drop to 10 seeds. Step 6 of the diary already
  notes that 20 control seeds say little.
    - The heap check now runs in CI only in the new step.
    - 40 of the 190 clean runs repeat identical workloads
  (`cacheFirst`/`keyTwice`/`forgetDelete`,
  `ackUnknown`/`settleByRead`/`serveUnknown`). Removing the repeats would win
  back coverage, but I left it out to keep the merge with the other builder
  small.
  - **Follow-ups:**
    - `Close` marks the instance closed before it knows the connections closed.
  After the scan fix, only an `iter.Pull` of a scan without `stop` can still
  reach that.
    - A reviewer found that a restarted instance never opens a cache with a log
  left by the crash, because `cacheDump` checkpoints the copy first. So the
  simulation never exercises recovery in `Open` after a crash.
  - **Merging:** my changes touch `core_test.go`, `simulation_test.go`,
  `workloads_test.go`, `tx_test.go`, `lease_test.go` and `cache_test.go`, all
  with small hunks. I added two files, `asan_test.go` and `noasan_test.go`.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The leak is found and fixed, and the fixes are pushed to pull request #12. CI
  is running again.

  The leak CI saw came from a test, not from the core. A test cut the store off
  and then gave Close one simulated second. Close spent that second trying to
  release the lease and never got to closing the cache. The test threw the error
  away.

  Looking for it turned up a real bug in the core. A scan that ended by a panic
  left its SQLite statement open. On a read, the connection then went back to
  the pool still holding its old snapshot, so a later read could serve state
  from before an acknowledged commit. That is exactly the kind of stale read the
  whole design exists to prevent. A new test fails without the fix, and the fix
  is a deferred call that gives the statement back.

  No simulation run had found this, because no workload ends a scan by a panic.

  A third find, in the test harness: a crashed instance with a read in flight
  kept that read's connection open. The default seeds never hit it, but it would
  have failed CI at random later.

  A permanent check that needs no sanitizer: SQLite deletes its write-ahead log
  when the last connection closes, so the tests now fail when a cache's log is
  still there at the end. A leftover connection in a simulation is reported with
  its seed.

  Time: the core's tests went from 240 to 126 seconds under the sanitizer in a
  4-CPU container, and from 136 to 98 seconds under the race detector here.

  One thing I'll have changed in a follow-up: Close spends its whole deadline on
  releasing the lease before it closes the cache. With a short deadline and an
  unreachable store, the caller gets an error and a cache that stays open, on
  every retry. Closing local resources shouldn't depend on the network.

  Still running: the builder finishing the mutation run.

  ## Anything I could not place, or that disagreed with the brief
  - **Stop and release happen the other way round.** The brief says what cannot
  be read "stops the instance after it released the lease". The code stops first
  (`DB.stop`), and then the lease task tries once to release (`releaseOnce`). I
  wrote it the way the code does it.
  - **Two quote dates are inferred.** "regular shutdowns still don't have to
  wait" and "\"duration\" is fine, and make it seconds" are not in any exported
  conversation, so I dated them 2026-11-12, the day the design changed. The
  frame quote and the 502, spare, both-clocks and "kind of the same" quotes are
  dated 2026-11-11 from the transcript.

  ## Wording Markus has not seen
  - **The stop rule, now in the README's Concurrency section.** I settled the
  old open point with new wording rather than keeping it in the todo. A read can
  start while SQLite fails and before the core sees the error. That read sees
  the state before the commit, which is allowed, because the commit is not yet
  acknowledged. The rule the build keeps is the spec's: a read that starts after
  an acknowledged commit sees it.
  - **The two open questions for Markus**, and the "damaged manifest" open
  point.
  - **The "Not decided:" and "To decide with the public driver:" labels** in the
  todo.

  ## Task 4: where the spec differs from the build ("Concurrency" and
  "Constraints")
  1. **One write transaction at a time.** Beyond waiting, a waiting writer fails
  at once when the instance stops serving, and when its context ends. The lease
  task also holds the writer lock while it takes the lease and catches up.
  2. **Reads run in parallel.** A read waiting for a connection also fails at
  once when the instance stops serving. The pool size is `Options.Readers`, 4 by
  default.
  3. **Many databases per bucket.** An empty prefix is allowed ("the keys have
  none"). A prefix must be valid UTF-8 of at most 900 bytes, with no empty, "."
  or ".." path segment.
  4. **Always a lease, "Every write renews it".** A transaction that changed
  nothing writes nothing and does not renew the lease.
  5. **The lease belongs to a running process, "A clean shutdown releases the
  lease".** A request to take the lease that is stuck in the network can be
  applied after `Close`, so the manifest then names a closed instance. `Close`
  with a short context can also fail to release.
  6. **The holder keeps the lease.** Beyond "released or timed out", an instance
  takes the lease at once if the manifest names itself.
  7. **The lease times out when the manifest stands still.** The wait is the
  duration plus a margin, the larger of 2 s and a tenth of the duration. That
  fits "longer than that". "Every renewal changes it by writing a new random
  value" does not hold for a release, which writes a manifest without a salt.
  8. **"Clocks affect only reads … Writes are never affected."** In the build,
  `DB.Begin` refuses when a clock says the lease has run out. A commit of
  nothing is judged by the clocks alone. `DB.Wait` and calls waiting for a lock
  also follow the clocks. The check happens before the state of a read is fixed
  and again after; the spec names neither moment.
  9. **"An open write transaction is not committed"** without a valid lease. In
  the build, `Tx.Commit` ignores the clocks. A transaction that is open while
  the lease has run out by the clocks still commits if its swap is applied. It
  fails only if the instance has lost the lease or stopped serving.
  10. **Opening fails "only on local problems".** It also fails on options that
  make no sense, on an invalid prefix, on a path of `:memory:` or `file:`, and
  on a cache made by another version (`ErrCacheVersion`).
  11. **The cache "holds the same tables and rows as the database".** It also
  holds `s4ql_cache` and temporary tables, and table names with the prefixes
  `s4ql_` and `sqlite_` are refused.
  12. **Statements are kept as annotations "with their parameters and their
  non-deterministic inputs".** `Tx.Annotate` takes the SQL text and parameters
  only. The annotations of a transaction that changed nothing are stored
  nowhere.
  13. **"The manifest swap is the commit."** A commit of nothing uploads nothing
  and swaps nothing; it commits by the clocks.
  14. **The cache is never ahead of S3, and a cache not in the log "is refused
  loudly".** `Open` does not refuse it. The instance stops with
  `ErrCacheNotInLog` when catch-up finds it.
  15. **An unknown commit outcome, "only if the caller gives up first".** The
  build has one more case, now an open question in the todo. Also, `Tx.Commit`
  goes on finding out after the instance stops.
  16. **A local I/O failure stops the instance.** The build also stops on
  something in the store it cannot read, and on `Close`. "Waiting writes fail"
  has one exception: a `Tx.Commit` whose swap may be applied goes on. "It then
  tries once to release the lease" is true for the lease task, but `Close` tries
  again, for up to one lease duration. "Busy" does not stop it; a failed spool
  file and an `ErrCacheVersion` found while running do.
  17. **No difference found** in "ACID per database", "Reads come from the local
  cache", "S3 holds a log", "The log is a linked list", "Commit objects are
  row-based", the storage-format bullets, "The manifest maps branch names", "The
  manifest has a format version", "Our own SQLite binding", "A commit is
  acknowledged once S3 has it", "Recovery from a stop is a normal start" and
  "One commit at a time".

    what is still open about the core, and what its tests don't show yet.

  details the spec doesn't need to carry. One is new and needs you, so the list
  of what's waiting for you is now four:

  1. Salt at the top level of the manifest, so that its bytes never repeat, also
     after a release. I recommend it.
  2. A second "outcome unknown" case in the spec: the lease is lost and the log
     can't be read to find out.
  3. A write transaction that is open when the lease runs out by the clocks. The
     spec says it "is not committed". The core lets it commit if its manifest
     swap still succeeds, because the swap proves that nobody else took the
     lease, and it renews the lease at the same time. I think the code is right
     and the spec's sentence is too strict, but that sentence is yours.
  4. The AWS SDK or a small client of our own for real S3.

  I'll take them one at a time when you're back. Meanwhile the mutation run is
  still going, and I'm holding the other follow-ups until the usage reset.

  Two `fable` reviewers read the new tests. Both found two real problems, which
  I fixed:
  - The default busy-timeout test only bounded the wait from above, so a busy
  timeout of zero passed. It now measures the wait: at least 1 s, under 8 s.
  - The test of a look in an unreadable log raced the lease task when it read
  the error afterwards. The manifest it publishes now has another holder's
  lease.

  I removed them: the 100 ms floor on a wait, and the wording of a replay error.

  the catch-up tests next to the older catch-up tests, in `lease_test.go` and
  `core_test.go`.

✻ Churned for 16s · done 8:57 AM

❯ /fabrik:one-at-a-time for the choices and decisions                           

⏺ Choice 1 of 5: the keys in S3.

  Under a database's prefix, the manifest is at <prefix>/manifest.json, and each
  commit object is at <prefix>/commits/<name>, where the name is the 64 hex
  digits of its SHA-256.

  The commit objects sit in one flat folder, with no sharding by the first
  characters as git does. S3 has no limit on keys under a prefix, and we never
  list them.

  Okay, or do you want a different layout?

✻ Crunched for 3s · done 8:59 AM

❯ Sharding by the first few characters might be a good thing? In case we ever   
  need to replicate commits to a disk or something. What would you propose?     

⏺ Yes, I'd shard, and now is the time: it is one function today and a migration
  of every object later.

  For S3 itself it changes nothing. S3 splits load by the leading characters of
  a key whether or not there is a slash. The case for it is the one you name: a
  generic tool such as rclone or aws s3 sync mirrors the layout to disk as it
  is, and a database writes one commit object per transaction, so a flat folder
  reaches millions of files quickly.

  I propose git's scheme with one change:

  <prefix>/commits/9f/9f2c…e41a

  - One level, named by the first two hex digits, so 256 folders. At ten million
    commits that is about 39,000 files per folder, which any file system
    handles well.
  - The file keeps the full 64-digit name. Git drops the two digits that are in
    the folder name. Keeping them means a file still identifies itself when it
    is moved or copied alone, and a tool gets the name from the file name
    without joining path parts.

  I'd not go to two levels. It only pays off beyond a hundred million commits,
  and a small database would then get nearly one folder per commit.

  Okay to change it to that?

✻ Crunched for 15s · done 9:01 AM

❯ yes                                                                           

⏺ Noted: commit objects go to <prefix>/commits/9f/9f2c…e41a. I'll have the
  changes from this round made together once we're through the list, in one
  builder, because usage is tight.

  Choice 2 of 5: a reserved prefix for table names.

  The core keeps one table of its own in the cache, s4ql_cache, which holds the
  recorded position. It must be in the same SQLite file as the data, so that the
  position and the rows commit in one local transaction. To keep it from
  colliding with an application's table, s4ql refuses to create any table whose
  name starts with s4ql_, in any letter case. SQLite does the same with sqlite_.

  The effect on the SQL surface: create table s4ql_x (...) works in SQLite and
  is rejected by s4ql. The roadmap lists it as a restriction.

  Okay?

✻ Crunched for 6s · done 9:02 AM
❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Choice 3 of 5: what cannot be read stops the instance.

  If the log holds something this version of s4ql can't read, the instance never
  skips it. It stops for good, with a cause that says which case it is, and
  releases the lease so that a newer version can serve. The cases:

  - a manifest or a commit object that needs a newer version of s4ql
  - one that is malformed
  - a commit object whose bytes don't match its name, after a second download
    gave the same result

  This adds a second kind of permanent stop next to the local I/O failure. The
  spec covers the first case loosely, where it says an older version "refuses,
  and asks for a newer version", but it doesn't say that the instance stops or
  that it gives up the lease.

  Okay?

✻ Churned for 3s · done 9:02 AM

❯ yes                                                                           

⏺ Choice 4 of 5: telemetry.

  The core emits one structured event for each unit of work: a commit, a lease
  change, a catch-up, a refusal for lack of a lease, an unknown outcome and how
  it was resolved, and a stop. Each event carries what the code knows at that
  point, such as the database's prefix, the holder's ID, the commit object's
  name, counts of changes and bytes, the number of attempts, the duration of
  each phase, and the outcome.

  The events go to a *slog.Logger that the application passes in, and nowhere by
  default. There is no OpenTelemetry dependency, no traces, and no metrics. I'd
  decide those when the public database/sql driver is built, because that is
  where an application's own tracing would connect.

  Okay?

✻ Brewed for 3s · done 9:03 AM

❯ So we log on each commit? Isn't that a bit much? But maybe on a debug or      
  trace level it's fine                                                         

⏺ Fair point. Today a successful commit is logged at Info, which on a busy
  database means one log line per transaction. I'd change the levels so that
  routine work is silent by default:

  - Debug: a successful commit, a routine lease renewal, and a call refused for
    lack of a lease. slog has no Trace level, so Debug is the lowest.
  - Info: a change of state: the lease taken, lost, or released, and a catch-up.
  - Warn: something that needs attention but doesn't stop the instance: a commit
    that failed or whose outcome is unknown, and SQLite's "busy" after the
    timeout.
  - Error: a permanent stop, with its cause.

  With the default Info level, a healthy instance then logs a few lines at
  startup and nothing more until something changes. The code checks whether
  Debug is enabled before it builds a commit's event, so a disabled event costs
  almost nothing.

  Okay with those levels?

✻ Churned for 8s · done 9:04 AM

❯ Yes. And make sure to use their context-aware log function counterparts, so   
  we can add tracing context (span ID etc.) later through the context           

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That's already how it works. Every event goes through one function, which
  calls LogAttrs with the context of the call that did the work. So a commit's
  event carries the context that the caller passed to Commit, and a handler can
  read a span ID from it later.

  One thing to know: the events of the background lease task, such as a renewal
  or a lost lease, have no caller. They carry the context that the database was
  opened with.

  I'll have the levels changed as agreed and add a test that a commit's event
  carries the caller's context, so this can't regress.

  Choice 5 of 5: a transaction that changed nothing.

  A write transaction that made no changes writes no commit object and swaps no
  manifest when it commits. It succeeds if the lease is valid by the clocks, the
  same test a read gets. Statements attached to it as annotations are stored
  nowhere.

  The reason: a commit object without changes would only lengthen the log, and
  every later start would have to download and replay it. The cost is that such
  a commit is judged by the clocks and not by S3, like a read, and that a write
  transaction which only read something leaves no trace of its statements.

  Okay?

✻ Worked for 11s · done 9:05 AM

❯ Yeah, good decision                                                           

⏺ That's the five choices. Now the four decisions.

  Decision 1 of 4: move the salt to the top level of the manifest?

  Today the salt is inside the lease entry. A manifest without a lease entry
  therefore has no salt, and it has the same bytes whenever the head is the
  same. Since S3's version comes from the bytes, that version can come back.

  The consequence: an instance sends a request to take a free lease, and the
  request gets stuck in the network. Later the lease is taken and cleanly
  released again, so the manifest has its old bytes. The stuck request now
  arrives, matches, and is applied. The manifest then names a holder that isn't
  running. No commit can be added this way, so the data is safe. The cost is
  that the next instance waits one lease duration for nothing.

  With the salt at the top level, new at every write of the manifest including a
  release, the rule "the manifest's bytes never repeat" holds without
  exception:

  {
    "version": 0,
    "salt": "c41d07a9e2b35f68",
    "branches": { "main": { "head": "9f2c…e41a" } },
    "lease": {
      "holder": "5b0e…77c3",
      "duration": "30s",
      "renewed": "2026-11-12T12:00:30.123456789Z"
    }
  }

  I recommend it. Okay?

✻ Cooked for 5s · done 9:06 AM

❯ Hmm. Isn't that a bit theoretical? And the cost is only a delay in lease? I'd 
  say no. As long as we don't gamble with correctness.                          

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed, no change. Before accepting I checked that it really is only a delay,
  because your condition is the right one.

  It is. A stuck request can only bring back a manifest version if the same
  bytes are sent twice, and the only version that comes back by itself is the
  one without a lease entry. The requests sent from that version are takes of
  the lease, and the core draws a new salt for every attempt of a take, so no
  two of them carry the same bytes. A late take can therefore install a lease
  and nothing else. Every commit is sent from a version that a holder wrote, and
  those never come back.

  That safety rests on one line of code, the fresh salt per take attempt. I'll
  have a test and a comment pin it, so nobody later "optimises" a retry into
  resending the same bytes. The todo's open point becomes a decided one with
  this reasoning.

  Decision 2 of 4: a second "outcome unknown" case in the spec.

  The spec says Commit returns an "outcome unknown" error "only if the caller
  gives up first". The build found one more case. The instance loses the lease
  while a commit's outcome is unknown, so it has to look for its commit object
  in the log that the new holder continued. If that log holds a commit object
  this version can never read, nothing can tell whether the transaction is in
  it. The instance stops, as agreed in choice 3, and Commit returns "outcome
  unknown".

  I'd add the case to the spec's bullet, so that "only if" stays true. Proposed
  wording for the last sentence:

  ▎ Commit returns an "outcome unknown" error only if the caller gives up first,
  ▎ or if the instance has to look for the transaction in a log that it cannot 
  ▎ read. The instance then serves nothing until it knows.

  Okay to write that?

✻ Brewed for 1m 18s · done 9:09 AM

❯ Why wouldn't it be able to read ever?                                         

⏺ "Ever" means: with this version of the program. There are two ways it happens.

  - A newer version of s4ql wrote it. This is the realistic one, during a
    rolling upgrade. An old instance has a commit whose answer got lost. A
    new-version instance takes the lease and commits on top, in a format the old
    one doesn't know. To see whether its own commit is underneath, the old
    instance must walk back from the head through the new commit objects,
    following their parent pointers. Its reader refuses a commit object of an
    unknown version entirely, so the walk stops at the first one.
  - The object is damaged or missing in the store, and a second download gives
    the same result. That means something outside s4ql changed the bucket, or
    the store broke its contract.

  In both cases the answer exists in S3, but this instance can't get to it. A
  newer version, or a person, could.

  For the first case there is a possible refinement later: promise that the
  parent field keeps its place and meaning in every future format version, and
  let an old instance read only that field to walk the log. It would then find
  its answer even under newer commit objects. I'd not do it now, since it needs
  a lost answer, a takeover, and a format change all at once.

  Does the spec wording work for you with that background?

✻ Worked for 14s · done 9:12 AM

❯ Also sounds quite theoretical, but yes, sounds like the right response.       

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Written into the spec and pushed, and the open point is gone from the todo.

  Decision 3 of 4: a write transaction that is open when the lease runs out by 
  the clocks.

  The spec says of an instance without a valid lease: "Reads and writes fail at
  once with a temporary error, and an open write transaction is not committed."

  The core does the first part: a new read or a new Begin is refused. For a
  transaction that is already open, it does something else. When that
  transaction commits, the core ignores the clocks and sends the manifest swap.
  If the swap succeeds, the transaction is committed.

  I think the code is right. A successful swap proves that the manifest is still
  the one this instance wrote, so nobody else took the lease, whatever the
  clocks say. The same swap also renews the lease. Refusing would throw away a
  transaction that S3 would have accepted. It also fits the spec's own
  neighbouring sentence: "Writes are never affected, because S3 checks the
  manifest swap."

  Proposed wording for that sentence:

  ▎ Reads and new write transactions fail at once with a temporary error. A 
  ▎ write transaction that is already open commits only if its manifest swap 
  ▎ still succeeds.

  Okay to write that?

✻ Churned for 17s · done 9:14 AM

❯ Yes                                                                           

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Written into the spec and pushed.

  Decision 4 of 4: which library talks to real S3?

  - The AWS SDK for Go, version 2. It handles request signing, checksums in
    trailers, endpoints, and the whole credential chain, including IAM roles on
    AWS. It is a large dependency. It retries on its own, which we must switch
    off for conditional writes, and it starts goroutines that our lint can't
    see.
  - A small client of our own on net/http. We need two operations, GET and PUT
    with a few headers, plus request signing. We'd control retries and the
    request body completely, and it could run under the simulation with a fake
    transport. But credentials would be static keys until we build more, and IAM
    roles are how servers on AWS normally authenticate.

  Either one sits behind the three-method interface and has to pass the same
  conformance suite, so swapping later is cheap.

  I lean towards the SDK, mostly for the credential chain, which is
  security-sensitive code I'd rather not write ourselves.

  SDK, or our own client?

› Message from fabrik:builder
  All five changes are built and committed, one commit each, plus a diary step.
  The 1000-seed runs are clean on all three workloads and every planted bug is
  still found. One race run failed once and I couldn't reproduce it (details
  under "Left undone"). One change of behaviour may need Markus: `Close` now
  rolls back an open write transaction that has no call running instead of
  waiting for it.

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a74c7cf17c1097f36`
  **Worktree:**
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a74c7cf17c1097f36`
  The branch contains "Say in the spec that an open write transaction commits if
  its swap still succeeds" (4193c4d). Not pushed.

  **Commits**
  - 827809b Shard the keys of commit objects by the first two digits of the name
  - 408aac0 Log routine work of the storage core at the level Debug
  - ced2b94 Pin that every attempt to take the lease carries a new salt
  - 5c45427 Close the cache in `core.DB.Close` whatever the store does
  - 64e60b1 Restart the core's simulation on a cache with the log that a crash
  left
  - 7c10ad5 Add the step of the five decisions of 2026-11-13 to the diary of the
  storage core (Step 9, author `decisions-builder`)

  **Change 2 (levels):** done as agreed, with tests for the quiet instance at
  Info and for the contexts. Three levels go beyond your list:
  - A commit whose cache fails after the swap is Warn, though `Commit` returns
  nil.
  - A catch-up that fails is Warn, except when a close cancelled it, which is
  Info.
  - A take or a release of the lease that fails is Warn.

  **Change 3: the reasoning holds.** I checked every swap and create in
  `lease.go` and `tx.go`:
  - `DB.acquire` builds new bytes with a new salt on every attempt.
  - The holder's first swap is from the version its own take wrote (`adopt` of
  the take's version), never from the version without a lease entry.
  - Every commit, renewal and release is from a version a holder wrote.
  - A create can never be applied late, because the manifest is never deleted.
  - The simulated store's version is a hash of the content, and it never sends a
  request twice.

  It rests on one condition, which I wrote into the README: nothing sends a take
  twice with the same bytes. If the store client resent a take by itself, the
  take's version could come back. A stuck commit from that version could then
  land after it had been settled as not committed. The core never does this. The
  todo already requires the S3 client never to let the SDK retry a conditional
  write, which covers it.
  - **Tests:** the new salt test fails under a constant salt (`Expected "3", but
  got "1"`). The scenario test does not fail under a constant salt, because the
  take's `Renewed` time differs between attempts in the bubble. It shows the
  case: the head is unchanged, the stuck commit is refused with
  `store.ErrChanged`, and the next instance waits 30 s plus the margin.

  **Change 4: what `Close` does in each case.**
  - **The first step:** in one critical section, it stops the instance and ends
  the lease task, so the task never starts its own try to release. In the same
  step it closes every connection that no running call uses.
  - **Then:** the release gets half of the time the context has left, if it has
  a deadline, and the lease's duration at most. Then `Close` waits for the lease
  task and for the connections that calls still use.
  - **Store unreachable, short deadline:** the cache is closed and the error
  contains "the lease is not released, and times out". A Warn event logs the
  failed release. A second `Close` does nothing, and the next instance waits for
  the lease to time out.
  - **An open transaction with no call running:** its connection is closed at
  once, which rolls a write transaction back. Its later calls fail with
  `ErrStopped`, and `Tx.Rollback` and `ReadTx.Close` return nil. This is the
  point that may need Markus.
  - **A call that is running** (a scan, or a `Tx.Commit` still finding out its
  outcome): its connection is never closed under it. The last call to leave a
  connection closes it. `Close` waits for those calls until its context ends,
  then returns an error that wraps the context error and says a call still uses
  the cache.
  - **Close errors:** errors from closing connections are kept and returned by
  `Close`.
  - **Clock:** the half of the deadline is measured on the host's wall clock,
  the only clock a deadline shares. A step of that clock changes only how long
  the release tries.

  **Change 5: evidence that recovery ran.**
  - The harness no longer reads the copy before the restarted instance opens it,
  so `core.Open` is the first to open it, with the write-ahead log and no
  shared-memory file.
  - After `Open`, the harness checks the cache against the state at the crash
  and against the model at its position.
  - It also reads a copy of the database file alone, without the log. Over 1000
  seeds, in 761 of 1000 restarts that file lacked commits the cache had, and
  `Open` brought them back. Those logs held 5064 frames in all, 36 in the
  largest.
  - Control: copying without the log makes the new check fail at once, e.g.
  `seed 0: the cache of a2 is at 00000000 after the restart, and was at aa3ce840
  at the crash`.

  **Verification on the final code** (10 cores, load 13 to 19)
  - `go test -count=1 ./internal/core/`: 41 s.
  - `go test -count=1 -race -shuffle on ./internal/core/`: 94 s.
  - `make test` and `make lint` pass.
  - `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS=1000`: no violation and 0 pairs of traces that differ in
  each workload. One instance took 93 s, everything 204 s, callers that never
  pause 281 s.
  - All 19 planted bugs are found four times each within 81 seeds in the default
  run, and the controls and clean runs find nothing.

  **Self-review** (code-review skill, level high, 10 findings)
  - **Fixed:**
    - A gap between the stop and closing, in which the lease task could start a
  release that `Close` then cancelled mid-request.
    - A failed release on close logged no event.
    - Errors of connection closes were dropped.
    - A catch-up cancelled by a close was logged as Warn.
    - `commitReport` kept its phases in a fixed array of four, so a fifth phase
  would have panicked; it is a slice now.
  - **Documented:** the wall-clock measure.
  - **Not done (small refinements):** the release takes half the deadline even
  when nothing waits on the cache. Each transaction call takes `DB.mu` three
  times where it could take it twice.
  - **Wrong:** the claim that sharding breaks reading old data. The spec says
  "The storage formats are drafts until version 1."

⏺ Agent "Apply agreed changes to core" finished · 52m 6s

  Created PR #15, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The agreed changes to the core are built and up as pull request #15
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/15), with CI running.

  - Sharded keys, log levels, and the context tests are done as agreed.
  - The salt reasoning holds. The builder checked every swap in the code against
    it. It rests on one condition, now written down: nothing sends a take of
    the lease twice with the same bytes. The S3 client's rule against automatic
    retries of conditional writes covers that side.
  - Close now closes the cache whatever the store does. Releasing the lease gets
    half of the time that is left, and a failed release comes back as an error.
  - SQLite's recovery after a crash is now exercised. In 761 of 1,000 simulated
    restarts the database file alone lacked commits, and recovery brought them
    back from the write-ahead log.

  One behaviour to know about: Close rolls back a write transaction that is open
  with no call running on it, and that transaction's later calls fail. It never
  closes a connection under a running call; it waits for those until its
  context ends. I accepted that as the usual meaning of closing a database.

  One thing I'm watching: a single race-detector run failed while the reviewers
  were also loading the machine, and the builder kept too little output to say
  which test. Five more runs passed. If CI shows it again, I'll have it hunted
  down.

  The S3 client is still being built.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## s3-builder report: the S3 client

  The client is built and passes all 17 conformance cases against the project's
  Tigris bucket. The tests found no case that passes on one of the real store
  and `simstore` and fails on the other. Two things are for you or Markus to
  decide, under "What is open".

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-abd2976e502fba390`, three commits on top of
  4193c4d: dab30b6, b832f47, 35003f6. Nothing is pushed.
  **Worktree:**
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-abd2976e502fba390`

  ### What I built and chose

  Package `internal/store/s3store`:
  - **Files:** `s3store.go`, `content.go`, `telemetry.go`, tests in
  `s3store_test.go`, `fake_test.go`, `content_internal_test.go`,
  `s3store_internal_test.go`, and a `README.md` (a writer sub-agent drafted it;
  I corrected it).
  - **API:** `s3store.New(ctx, s3store.Options)`, with options `Bucket`,
  `Endpoint`, `Region`, `PathStyle`, `RootCAs` and `Logger`. Credentials come
  from the SDK's default chain. `New` refuses a missing bucket or region, and an
  endpoint that is not an http or https URL. There is also `s3store.MaxSize` (5
  GiB) and `s3store.ErrTooLarge`.
  - **Checksum on upload, which the store checks.** I tested three ways on
  Tigris:
    - The `x-amz-checksum-sha256` header is not checked: a wrong value got 200
  and was stored.
    - Our own SHA-256 in an aws-chunked trailer is checked (`400 BadDigest`,
  nothing stored, also for empty content).
    - The request signed with our SHA-256 as `x-amz-content-sha256` is checked
  (`400 XAmzContentSHA256Mismatch`, nothing stored, also for empty content).
    - The client uses the signed payload hash. It also sends the header, so that
  the object carries the checksum. Both codes map to `ErrChecksum`.
    - The SDK cannot put a checksum we computed into a trailer: its trailer
  middleware skips if a checksum header is already set, and otherwise computes
  the checksum from the bytes it reads.
    - I chose the payload hash over a trailer of our own because it needs no
  encoding written by us, it works over plain HTTP, and every SigV4 store must
  compute it anyway.
    - AWS documents a check of both the header and the payload hash; the quotes
  are in the README. This is not checked against AWS.
  - **Error mapping:** a refusal needs both the status and the code.
    - 412 `PreconditionFailed`: create gives `ErrExists`, swap gives
  `ErrChanged`. A 412 without a body counts too, because the SDK takes the code
  from the status text.
    - 409 `ConditionalRequestConflict` on a swap gives `ErrChanged`. On a create
  it is an unknown outcome: AWS documents that 409 only when a delete races the
  write, so no sentinel fits.
    - 404 `NoSuchKey` on a swap gives `ErrNotFound`.
    - Everything else is an unknown outcome. Its error wraps only
  `context.Canceled` or `context.DeadlineExceeded`.
  - **Reads:** `ChecksumMode: ENABLED`, so the SDK compares the stored checksum
  with the content as it is read. The content must be as long as the answer's
  content length.
  - **Memory:** the content is streamed. 64 MiB up and down raised the heap's
  live objects by about 256 KiB at the peak. Content over 5 GiB, or with its
  offset past its end, is refused before anything is sent.
  - **Timeouts:** none of the client's own. The guard of the content waits for a
  read in progress before the call returns.
  - **Telemetry:** one event, `store_request`, per request. For a read it is
  logged when the content is closed.
    - Debug for ok, exists, changed and not_found.
    - Warn for checksum, unknown, unexpected, failed, and `not_sent` (a call
  refused before sending, so a write that is too large is not silent).
    - Attributes: `operation`, `outcome`, `error`, `attempts` and
  `duration.total` reuse the core's names. `store.key`, `store.bytes`,
  `http.status_code` and `store.request_id` are new.
  - **Lint:** the package is exempt in `internal/simlint/simlint.go`, with the
  reason that the SDK and net/http start goroutines, read the clock and wait on
  the network outside the host.

  ### How "never a false not applied" is made sure of

  Each mechanism, and how I checked it:
  - **SDK retries off:** `aws.NopRetryer` and `RetryMaxAttempts` 0, set after
  the config is loaded. A test with `AWS_MAX_ATTEMPTS=5` and
  `AWS_RETRY_MODE=adaptive` sees exactly one PUT and one GET on a 503.
  - **net/http retries on a reused connection:** in Go 1.27.3 it resends a
  request only if nothing of it was written, or if it is replayable. A PUT is
  replayable only with an `Idempotency-Key` header and a body that is nil or has
  `GetBody`; ours has neither. Tests cut a reused connection after the fake
  applied a create or a swap: the result is an unknown outcome, one PUT is sent,
  and the object is there. A control test shows that net/http does resend a GET
  in the same situation, and that the client counts 2 attempts.
  - **HTTP/2:** the transport speaks HTTP/1.1 only. A test against a server that
  offers HTTP/2 sees HTTP/1.1.
  - **Proxy:** none is used. An internal test checks that the transport's
  `Proxy` is nil.
  - **Redirects:** not followed. Go would resend a 307 of an empty PUT. A test
  checks that no second PUT goes out.
  - **Last guard:** `httptrace` `WroteHeaders` counts the attempts. It is put on
  the request in a middleware after signing, so credential requests are not
  counted. A refusal is believed only when the count is exactly 1.
  - **Mutation testing:** 38 of 40 mutants are caught. The two that survive make
  no difference while the SDK behaves as it does: removing `guard.close`,
  because smithy-go already closes the request body after `Do`, and
  `ChecksumMode`, because `ResponseChecksumValidation: WhenSupported` asks for
  the checksum anyway.

  The self-review used two `fable` reviewers. What they found and I fixed:
  - **An error from the caller's content could make an unknown outcome look like
  a refusal.** The unknown path wrapped the SDK error with `%w`, and that error
  wraps whatever the content's last Read returned. Content that failed after
  its length with `ErrExists` made `errors.Is(err, ErrExists)` true for a write
  that was stored. Fixed; a test now fails the content with each of the four
  errors.
  - **The count of attempts included a request for credentials,** for example
  with `AWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_FULL_URI`. That direction is safe (a real
  refusal became unknown), but wrong. Fixed with the middleware above, and
  tested.
  - **No test without a network showed that a swap is conditional.** The
  conformance suite now also runs against the fake, and the If-Match and
  If-None-Match headers are checked.
  - **My first diary entry said net/http did not read the body after returning;
  that was wrong.** It does. smithy-go stops it, so the guard is a second layer.
  The README and diary say so now.
  - Smaller fixes: tests isolated from the machine's AWS configuration, a test
  that failed under load, offset past end, endpoint validation, inlined
  constants, and a comment that named the core.

  ### Differences between the real bucket and simstore

  No suite case and none of my real-bucket tests differ. Tigris also kept apart,
  and read back, keys with `?`, `#`, `%20`, `+`, `//`, leading or trailing
  slashes, tab, newline, `...`, NFC and NFD forms of é, and 1024 bytes.
  Differences in kind:
  - **The delay of a stuck write. Simstore may be kinder here.** `AllFaults`
  delays a stuck write by up to 60 s. A signed request is accepted for 15
  minutes on AWS and for at least 1000 s on Tigris
  (`docs/research/s3-clock.md`), and no store documents how long it may hold a
  request it has received. The core does not rely on a bound, so I changed
  nothing. A workload with a `LateBy` of minutes would show it. Changing
  `AllFaults` alters every seed's trace, so it is not a small change.
  - **Misuse.** An invalid key or a swap from an empty version panics in
  `simstore`. In `s3store` it returns an error, which the contract reads as an
  unknown outcome, and `Tx.upload` would retry it. This is a question of the
  contract.
  - **Connections.** A body that is never closed holds a connection on the real
  client and costs nothing in `simstore`. The core closes every body.
  - **No 409 on Tigris.** `simstore` produces conflicts on purpose, so it is the
  stricter one.
  - **New error code.** `XAmzContentSHA256Mismatch` is not in the todo's
  mapping. It comes from the choice of the payload hash.

  ### The check on download

  I did not add a checksum to `store.Store.Get`. Instead, the client asks the
  store for the checksum it holds, and the SDK checks the content against it as
  it is read; content that does not match fails at its end.

  The agreed todo item says "compares the checksum that S3 returns with the
  name, before it reads the body". Doing that would need the name in the
  interface. It would only catch an object stored with another checksum, which
  the upload check rules out for every object s4ql writes. The core reads to the
  end and checks the name anyway.

  This differs from what was agreed, so I recorded it in `docs/todo.md` as open,
  for Markus. The core is unchanged.

  ### Verification

  - **Without the settings:** `make test` and `make lint` pass. `go test -race
  -shuffle on ./internal/store/s3store/` passes.
  - **With the settings:** three runs in a row of the whole package pass, in
  19.4 s, 15.2 s and 15.4 s. A run with `-race` passes in 19.0 s.
  - **Cleanup:** the bucket has no objects under `s4ql-test/` after the runs.
  - **Credentials:** a count before each commit found 0 occurrences of the
  access key and the secret in the diff.

  ### Dependency size

  - **go.mod:** 4 direct requirements (`aws-sdk-go-v2` v1.48.0, `config`
  v1.33.18, `service/s3` v1.114.10, `smithy-go` v1.28.9) and 14 indirect ones,
  all from AWS. 113 AWS packages are in the build.
  - **Binary on darwin/arm64:** 9.5 MB grows to 15.1 MB unstripped (+5.6 MB),
  and 6.4 MB to 10.3 MB with `-s -w` (+3.9 MB).

  ### What is open

  1. **Check on download:** Markus decides whether the comparison during the
  read is enough (todo).
  2. **Misuse:** should a misused call panic, as in `simstore`, or wrap an error
  of its own? Today it reads as an unknown outcome.
  3. **Commit objects over 5 GiB:** `ErrTooLarge` is taken by the core as an
  unknown outcome and retried until its context ends (todo).
  4. **CI:** running the integration tests would need a bucket for CI and its
  credentials as repository secrets, passed as `S4QL_S3_BUCKET`, `AWS_REGION`,
  `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_S3`, `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` and `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`. The
  credentials need `s3:GetObject`, `s3:PutObject`, `s3:ListBucket` and
  `s3:DeleteObject` (the last for cleanup). Not set up.
  5. **AWS:** nothing was checked against AWS. The README lists what rests on
  its documentation alone: the two checksum checks, the codes 409/412/404, 403
  without `s3:ListBucket`, and the 5 GB limit.
  6. **Smaller points:**
     - `AWS_CA_BUNDLE` makes `New` fail; the doc of `Options.RootCAs` says so.
     - With `AWS_DEFAULTS_MODE=auto`, `New` may call IMDS for the SDK's
  defaults.
     - The todo's bullet "A real S3 client under simulation is not shown" stays:
  the package is exempt from the simulation.

  client. It was a mistake in the test again, not in the client.

  job. I'll add a rule to the builder briefs: a test must not depend on which
  of two legal orders happens, and must be run many times under load before it
  is handed in.

  park, the share of points that get jitter, a timeout whose parent is done
  staying listed, and a few traces. Each now has a direct test. `TestSimulation`
  still runs every seed twice, with a workload called `everything`: several
  instances, clock moves, two crashes, buggify, timeouts and semaphores. It runs
  200 seeds by default, and `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS` changes the number.

  **Coverage**, statements covered by both packages' tests:

  | File | With the toy | Without the toy, before | After |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `sim.go` | 331 of 343 | 323 | 340 |
  | `free.go` | 15 of 15 | 15 | 15 |
  | `host.go` | 29 of 29 | 29 | 29 |

  Three statements in `sim.go` stay uncovered:
  - the two panics in `goroutineID`, which need a stack trace without a
  goroutine ID;
  - the check in `parkAs` that a task waits only while the scheduler runs it. A
  task's own goroutine cannot break that, and the goroutine check catches any
  other goroutine first.

  **Mutants** (one at a time through `-overlay`; 50 in `sim.go`, plus one in
  `free.go` under `-race`):
  - **After the change:** 49 of 50 caught, and the `free.go` one too. The one
  survivor ends crashed tasks in reverse name order. It is equivalent: the
  scheduler promises no order, only that the seed fixes it.
  - **Before, without the toy's tests:** 29 of 50 caught, and the `free.go` one
  survived. The 21 survivors include every toy-only decision above.
  - **Before, with the toy:** stopped after 1 of 22. That one, a timeout of a
  crashed instance that expires, also survived the toy. 21 are left;
  `with_toy.sh` in the scratchpad runs them.

  Several mutants hang the scheduler rather than fail it, because
  `Scheduler.Run` can move time without counting a step. They are caught only by
  the test timeout. A fix would be a change to `sim.go`, so it is your call; it
  is noted in the diary.
